- **Score**: 6/10

### Look Before you Leap: Estimating LLM Benchmark Scores from Descriptions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20645v1)
- **Authors**: Jungsoo Park, Ethan Mendes, Gabriel Stanovsky, Alan Ritter
- **Abstract**: Progress in large language models is constrained by an evaluation bottleneck: build a benchmark, evaluate models and settings, then iterate. We therefore ask a simple question: can we forecast outcomes before running any experiments? We study text-only performance forecasting: estimating a model's score from a redacted task description and intended configuration, with no access to dataset instances. To support systematic study, we curate PRECOG, a corpus of redacted description-performance pairs spanning diverse tasks, domains, and metrics. Experiments show the task is challenging but feasible: models equipped with a retrieval module that excludes source papers achieve moderate prediction performance with well-calibrated uncertainty, reaching mean absolute error as low as 8.7 on the Accuracy subset at high-confidence thresholds. Our analysis indicates that stronger reasoning models engage in diverse, iterative querying, whereas current open-source models lag and often skip retrieval or gather evidence with limited diversity. We further test a zero-leakage setting, forecasting on newly released datasets or experiments before their papers are indexed, where GPT-5 with built-in web search still attains nontrivial prediction accuracy. Overall, our corpus and analyses offer an initial step toward open-ended anticipatory evaluation, supporting difficulty estimation and smarter experiment prioritization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the task of text-only performance forecasting for Large Language Models (LLMs).  It explores the possibility of predicting an LLM's benchmark score based *solely* on a redacted task description and intended configuration *before* any experiments are run, thus addressing a bottleneck in LLM development.  To facilitate research in this area, the authors create PRECOG, a corpus of redacted description-performance pairs mined from arXiv papers.  Experiments demonstrate that the task is challenging but feasible, with retrieval-augmented LLMs achieving moderate prediction performance and calibrated uncertainty.  The authors analyze the retrieval behavior of different models and explore a zero-leakage "streaming" prediction setting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of predicting LLM benchmark performance from textual descriptions *prior* to running experiments is relatively novel. Existing work focuses on missing-entry prediction or efficient evaluation via extrapolation from pilot runs, requiring some initial measurements or closely related tasks. This work proposes a truly zero-shot, open-ended approach.  Predicting outcomes in online markets is related, but the focus on LLM task performance prediction is new.
*   **Significance:** If successful, such a performance forecasting system could significantly impact LLM research and development. It could:
    *   **Reduce wasted compute:**  Allow researchers to prioritize experiments with higher expected returns, avoiding costly evaluations of configurations likely to perform poorly.
    *   **Inform benchmark design:** Provide insights into task difficulty and potential issues with proposed benchmarks before large-scale annotation efforts.
    *   **Guide experiment planning:** Help determine appropriate pilot sizes, relevant baselines, and the overall scope of an experiment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **The PRECOG dataset:**  The creation of a curated dataset of redacted descriptions and corresponding benchmark scores is a crucial contribution, providing a resource for future research.  The dataset construction methodology, with its focus on mitigating leakage, is commendable.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experiments demonstrate that the task is not trivial but is also not impossible.  The performance of GPT-5, especially with search, suggests that LLMs can leverage pre-trained knowledge and literature to make reasonably accurate predictions.
    *   **Analysis of retrieval behavior:**  The analysis of how different models use retrieval tools provides valuable insights into their reasoning processes and limitations. The comparison between tool-use behaviors between proprietary and open-source models is a plus.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset scale:** While the dataset is carefully curated, a larger dataset would strengthen the empirical findings. A lot of the analysis could be improved by a more comprehensive statistical analysis, and/or a deeper dive into failure cases.
    *   **Limited baseline performance:** The classical text-encoder baselines perform poorly, suggesting that there is room for improvement in feature engineering or alternative approaches. Although, this also shows that the task requires deeper language understanding capabilities.
    *   **Reliance on proprietary models:** The best performance is achieved with GPT-5, limiting accessibility and reproducibility for some researchers. While open-source alternatives are explored, they lag significantly behind.
    *   **Potential for unforeseen leakage:** Despite efforts to mitigate leakage, it is difficult to completely eliminate the possibility that models may have encountered information about the tasks or datasets during pre-training. The streaming prediction experiment addresses this somewhat, but further validation is needed.
    *   **Limited diversity of datasets.** Datasets seem to be more focused on areas where the authors are specialized.
    *   **Confidence estimation:** While confidence is measured, it only shows reasonable alignment. More thorough calibration techniques would strengthen the work.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to inspire further research in this area. Building better forecasting systems could revolutionize LLM development and evaluation practices. The work also raises interesting questions about the nature of LLM reasoning and their ability to leverage external knowledge.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant research direction with the creation of a new dataset. The experiments demonstrate the feasibility of the task, while also highlighting the challenges and limitations of current approaches.  The reliance on proprietary models and potential leakage concerns weaken the contribution, but overall, the paper represents a valuable first step toward a more efficient and data-driven approach to LLM development.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Accelerate Creation of Product Claims Using Generative AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20652v1)
- **Authors**: Po-Yu Liang, Yong Zhang, Tatiana Hwa, Aaron Byers
- **Abstract**: The benefit claims of a product is a critical driver of consumers' purchase behavior. Creating product claims is an intense task that requires substantial time and funding. We have developed the $\textbf{Claim Advisor}$ web application to accelerate claim creations using in-context learning and fine-tuning of large language models (LLM). $\textbf{Claim Advisor}$ was designed to disrupt the speed and economics of claim search, generation, optimization, and simulation. It has three functions: (1) semantically searching and identifying existing claims and/or visuals that resonate with the voice of consumers; (2) generating and/or optimizing claims based on a product description and a consumer profile; and (3) ranking generated and/or manually created claims using simulations via synthetic consumers. Applications in a consumer packaged goods (CPG) company have shown very promising results. We believe that this capability is broadly useful and applicable across product categories and industries. We share our learning to encourage the research and application of generative AI in different industries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents "Claim Advisor," a web application designed to accelerate the creation of product claims using generative AI. It leverages in-context learning and fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) for claim search, generation, optimization, and simulation. The system features: (1) semantic search for existing claims and visuals, (2) claim generation/optimization based on product descriptions and consumer profiles, and (3) claim ranking using simulated consumer feedback. The authors demonstrate promising results in a consumer packaged goods (CPG) company, suggesting broader applicability across industries.  Key technologies used include ChatGPT-4, Phi-3 (fine-tuned), LangChain, and Streamlit.  The paper shares implementation details and provides a GitHub repository for reproducibility and further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of several existing techniques (LLMs, prompt engineering, fine-tuning, MaxDiff analysis) into a *practical system* for a specific industrial application – product claim generation. While each component may not be individually novel, their combination, workflow orchestration, and application to this domain, where speed and compliance are critical, demonstrates practical innovation. The fine-tuning of Phi-3 and its performance relative to larger, closed-source models like GPT-4 also adds a layer of novelty. However, the paper acknowledges that specific prompts and datasets are proprietary, limiting full reproducibility and therefore limiting the degree of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is considerable. Product claim generation is a time-consuming and resource-intensive process in many industries. By demonstrating the potential for AI to accelerate this process, the paper addresses a real-world industrial need. If the system can demonstrably reduce the time and cost of claim generation *without* sacrificing scientific rigor or legal compliance, it could have a significant impact on product development cycles. The use of synthetic consumers to simulate initial feedback is a particularly interesting aspect that could greatly reduce the need for expensive and time-consuming consumer testing in the early stages. The sharing of code and example prompts is also a positive contribution, encouraging further research and application.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined system architecture and implementation details.
    *   Demonstration of results in a real-world application.
    *   Emphasis on practical considerations (latency, stability, cost).
    *   Sharing of code and example prompts.
    *   Quantitative metrics (Kendall's Tau, Top-N Coverage) used to evaluate performance.
    *   Discussion of limitations and future work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited reproducibility due to proprietary datasets and prompts. This is a *significant* weakness as it hinders independent verification of the results.
    *   Lack of rigorous comparison against alternative methods (e.g., traditional manual claim generation) to quantify the actual speedup and cost savings. While they qualitatively mention efficiency gains, concrete numbers are missing.
    *   The claims of 100% highly appealing after the third iteration are surprisingly strong and could benefit from more detailed discussion and robustness checks. It's important to know the criteria of highly appealing.
    *   Limited discussion of the potential biases introduced by synthetic consumer simulation. How representative are the synthetic consumers of the real target audience? What measures are in place to avoid reinforcing existing biases?
    *   The study does not delve deeply into the legal aspects of AI-generated claims. Although they emphasize compliance, the paper lacks a detailed analysis of the legal implications and challenges.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   Accelerated product development cycles.
    *   Reduced costs associated with claim generation.
    *   Improved efficiency and creativity of product researchers.
    *   Wider adoption of AI in creative and decision-making tasks in industry.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practical and promising application of LLMs to a real-world industrial problem. The integration of various techniques is well-executed, and the results are encouraging. However, the lack of full reproducibility due to proprietary datasets and prompts significantly limits its impact. While the novelty of the system is respectable, it heavily relies on existing techniques, and it requires more detailed comparisons to standard methods to justify their approach. They do not have rigorous discussions of potential biases or potential limitations of the specific LLMs that are used. These limitations hinder its impact on the broader research community.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Enhancing Molecular Property Prediction with Knowledge from Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20664v1)
- **Authors**: Peng Zhou, Lai Hou Tim, Zhixiang Cheng, Kun Xie, Chaoyi Li, Wei Liu, Xiangxiang Zeng
- **Abstract**: Predicting molecular properties is a critical component of drug discovery. Recent advances in deep learning, particularly Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), have enabled end-to-end learning from molecular structures, reducing reliance on manual feature engineering. However, while GNNs and self-supervised learning approaches have advanced molecular property prediction (MPP), the integration of human prior knowledge remains indispensable, as evidenced by recent methods that leverage large language models (LLMs) for knowledge extraction. Despite their strengths, LLMs are constrained by knowledge gaps and hallucinations, particularly for less-studied molecular properties. In this work, we propose a novel framework that, for the first time, integrates knowledge extracted from LLMs with structural features derived from pre-trained molecular models to enhance MPP. Our approach prompts LLMs to generate both domain-relevant knowledge and executable code for molecular vectorization, producing knowledge-based features that are subsequently fused with structural representations. We employ three state-of-the-art LLMs, GPT-4o, GPT-4.1, and DeepSeek-R1, for knowledge extraction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our integrated method outperforms existing approaches, confirming that the combination of LLM-derived knowledge and structural information provides a robust and effective solution for MPP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework to enhance molecular property prediction (MPP) by integrating knowledge extracted from Large Language Models (LLMs) with structural features derived from pre-trained molecular models. The approach prompts LLMs to generate domain-relevant knowledge and executable code for molecular vectorization, creating knowledge-based features. These features are then fused with structural representations. Experiments demonstrate that this integrated method outperforms existing approaches, confirming the effectiveness of combining LLM-derived knowledge and structural information for MPP. The paper employs three state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-40, GPT-4.1, and DeepSeek-R1) for knowledge extraction and tests on standard molecular property prediction datasets (HIV, BACE, ClinTox, and BBBP).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integrated approach*. While using LLMs for knowledge extraction in MPP is not entirely new (LLM4SD is referenced), the paper distinguishes itself by specifically focusing on the synergistic combination of LLM-derived knowledge (prior and inferred) *and* molecular structural information *without needing fine-tuning LLMs*. This direct integration with structural features extracted from pre-trained models is a valuable contribution. However, the improvement is incremental over LLM4SD which uses LLMs for rule extraction. LLM4SD uses only LLMs, and this work complements LLMs with structure data. It also avoids the sequential method of extracting knowledge in LLM4SD.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in MPP – effectively incorporating human prior knowledge while mitigating the limitations (knowledge gaps, hallucinations) of LLMs, especially for less-studied molecular properties. The results demonstrate consistent performance gains across different datasets and pre-trained backbones, indicating the robustness of the approach. The analysis of knowledge vectors and their correlation with molecular properties offers valuable insights into the behavior and limitations of the LLM-based component.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integration is well-motivated and addresses a relevant problem.
    *   The experimental design is thorough, with comparisons against strong baselines and ablation studies.
    *   The use of multiple LLMs and pre-trained models strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The knowledge analysis provides interesting insights into the LLM's knowledge representation and potential limitations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance gains, while consistent, are sometimes incremental. The magnitude of the improvements varies across datasets and backbones.
    *   The "GNN+Desc" experiments, while included to assess whether the LLMs have inherent feature selection capabilities, suffer from the HIV dataset issues as stated by authors themselves. Further analysis/alternate configurations might be helpful.
    *   While the paper addresses hallucinations, a deeper exploration of the types of errors the LLMs make and how the structural information helps to correct them would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The work has the potential to influence MPP research by highlighting the benefits of combining LLM-derived knowledge with structural information. It encourages exploration of similar integrated approaches in other areas of drug discovery and molecular modeling. Future work could explore more sophisticated fusion techniques or methods for explicitly addressing LLM hallucinations. The combination approach of LLMs and structural information is a step forward.

*   **Overall:** This paper presents a solid contribution to the field of molecular property prediction by demonstrating a practical and effective way to combine the strengths of LLMs with the well-established techniques of structural modeling. The rigorous experimental evaluation and insightful analysis support the paper's claims. The novelty is in the integration architecture and method, while the improvements, though significant, are incremental.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Federated Learning Safeguard Private Data in LLM Training? Vulnerabilities, Attacks, and Defense Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20680v1)
- **Authors**: Wenkai Guo, Xuefeng Liu, Haolin Wang, Jianwei Niu, Shaojie Tang, Jing Yuan
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) with local data is a widely adopted approach for organizations seeking to adapt LLMs to their specific domains. Given the shared characteristics in data across different organizations, the idea of collaboratively fine-tuning an LLM using data from multiple sources presents an appealing opportunity. However, organizations are often reluctant to share local data, making centralized fine-tuning impractical. Federated learning (FL), a privacy-preserving framework, enables clients to retain local data while sharing only model parameters for collaborative training, offering a potential solution. While fine-tuning LLMs on centralized datasets risks data leakage through next-token prediction, the iterative aggregation process in FL results in a global model that encapsulates generalized knowledge, which some believe protects client privacy. In this paper, however, we present contradictory findings through extensive experiments. We show that attackers can still extract training data from the global model, even using straightforward generation methods, with leakage increasing as the model size grows. Moreover, we introduce an enhanced attack strategy tailored to FL, which tracks global model updates during training to intensify privacy leakage. To mitigate these risks, we evaluate privacy-preserving techniques in FL, including differential privacy, regularization-constrained updates and adopting LLMs with safety alignment. Our results provide valuable insights and practical guidelines for reducing privacy risks when training LLMs with FL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the privacy risks associated with using Federated Learning (FL) for training Large Language Models (LLMs).  Contrary to the widely held belief that FL adequately safeguards private data in LLM training, the authors demonstrate through extensive experiments that training data can still be extracted from the global model, even with simple generation methods. They introduce an enhanced attack strategy tailored to FL which tracks global model updates during training to intensify privacy leakage.  The paper also evaluates various privacy-preserving techniques within the FL framework, including differential privacy, regularization, and safety-aligned models, and concludes that they often lead to a trade-off between privacy and training performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core contribution lies in challenging the common assumption that FL inherently protects privacy when training generative models like LLMs. The enhanced attack strategy, which leverages the iterative nature of FL, is a valuable novel aspect. The evaluation of defense mechanisms and their limitations is also a useful addition to the literature.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for organizations considering FL for LLM training. The paper highlights the need for a more nuanced understanding of the privacy vulnerabilities within FL and a more rigorous evaluation of privacy-preserving techniques tailored for generative models. The results should encourage the development of more robust defenses against data leakage in FL-based LLM training.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation, using multiple LLMs of varying sizes and a range of datasets.
    *   **Practical Attack:** The enhanced attack strategy is well-motivated and demonstrably increases privacy leakage in realistic FL scenarios.
    *   **Defense Mechanism Evaluation:** The evaluation of differential privacy, regularization, and safety-aligned models provides practical guidance to researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making the findings easily accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Focus on Other LLM tasks**: The investigation of privacy leakage issues is not performed for many LLM tasks like RLHF and DPO.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence the development of new FL algorithms and defenses specifically tailored for LLM training. It could also inform policy decisions related to data privacy and the ethical use of LLMs.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper's core idea of attacking LLMs in FL is not entirely new, the enhanced attack strategy tailored for FL updates and the detailed experimental validation significantly advance the field. The comprehensive evaluation of defense mechanisms is particularly valuable for practical applications. The significance of the findings for the responsible deployment of LLMs justifies a relatively high score. The main weakness being the limited scope of testing other LLM tasks like RLHF and DPO.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CE-GPPO: Controlling Entropy via Gradient-Preserving Clipping Policy Optimization in Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20712v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenpeng Su, Leiyu Pan, Minxuan Lv, Yuntao Li, Wenping Hu, Fuzheng Zhang, Kun Gai, Guorui Zhou
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a powerful paradigm for optimizing large language models (LLMs) to handle complex reasoning tasks. A core challenge in this process lies in managing policy entropy, which reflects the balance between exploration and exploitation during training. Existing methods, such as proximal policy optimization (PPO) and its variants, discard valuable gradient signals from low-probability tokens due to the clipping mechanism. We systematically analyze the entropy dynamics and reveal that these clipped tokens play a critical yet overlooked role in regulating entropy evolution. We propose \textbf{C}ontrolling \textbf{E}ntropy via \textbf{G}radient-\textbf{P}reserving \textbf{P}olicy \textbf{O}ptimization (CE-GPPO), a novel algorithm that reintroduces gradients from clipped tokens in native PPO in a gentle and bounded manner. By controlling the magnitude of gradients from tokens outside the clipping interval, CE-GPPO is able to achieve an exploration-exploitation trade-off. We provide theoretical justification and empirical evidence showing that CE-GPPO effectively mitigates entropy instability. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that CE-GPPO consistently outperforms strong baselines across different model scales.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CE-GPPO (Controlling Entropy via Gradient-Preserving Policy Optimization), a reinforcement learning algorithm designed to improve the training of large language models (LLMs) for complex reasoning tasks. CE-GPPO focuses on managing policy entropy, which is crucial for balancing exploration and exploitation during training.  The authors argue that existing methods like PPO discard valuable gradient information from low-probability tokens due to clipping. CE-GPPO reintroduces these gradients in a controlled manner, using a stop-gradient operation and scaling coefficients to regulate their influence. This approach allows for fine-grained control over entropy dynamics, preventing both entropy collapse (premature convergence) and entropy explosion (excessive exploration). The paper provides theoretical justification and empirical evidence demonstrating that CE-GPPO effectively mitigates entropy instability and consistently outperforms strong baselines on mathematical reasoning benchmarks across different model scales.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper identifies a specific weakness in standard PPO approaches when applied to LLMs: the potentially detrimental impact of clipping gradients from low-probability tokens on entropy control.  While entropy regularization and clipping adjustments are not entirely new concepts in RL, the specific approach of selectively reintroducing clipped gradients with fine-grained control *is* novel. The analysis of how different token probability categories (PA&HP, NA&LP, etc.) influence entropy dynamics is also a valuable contribution. However, the stop-gradient technique is widely used, and the contribution lies in *how* it is applied.  The link to the PA&LP/NA&LP classification is what provides novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical improvement demonstrated on complex reasoning tasks.  LLM training with RL is a challenging area, and any technique that demonstrably improves performance and stability is valuable. The gains shown on multiple benchmarks are convincing, suggesting that CE-GPPO is a useful addition to the RLHF toolkit. The identified weakness of PPO may also inspire other researchers to investigate the impact of token probabilities during RL training further, so there is an indirect theoretical impact as well.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of entropy instability in RL fine-tuning of LLMs.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis of gradient updates and their impact on entropy dynamics is insightful and well-explained. The categorization of tokens and their roles adds to the understanding of the algorithm.
    *   **Effective Algorithm:** CE-GPPO is presented as a straightforward modification of PPO, making it relatively easy to implement and integrate into existing workflows.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results convincingly demonstrate CE-GPPO's superior performance compared to strong baselines. The experiments also illustrate the algorithm's ability to balance exploration and exploitation.
    *   **Stable Optimization:** The paper explicitly addresses optimization stability (which is not always the case).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Parameter Tuning:** While the core idea is compelling, the performance of CE-GPPO depends on the appropriate selection of B1 and B2. The process of tuning these parameters is somewhat ad-hoc, which reduces the practical appeal of the algorithm. An adaptive or principled method for setting B1 and B2 would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** The paper focuses primarily on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are important, it would be valuable to discuss the potential generalizability of CE-GPPO to other RLHF scenarios.
    *   **Theoretical Depth:** While there is theoretical justification provided, the primary contribution rests on an *empirical* demonstration. A more rigorous theoretical framework would solidify the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more stable and effective RL algorithm for fine-tuning LLMs. The insights regarding the role of low-probability tokens may also guide future research in this area. The method is practically relevant.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** CE-GPPO represents a solid contribution to the field of RL for LLM fine-tuning. The paper clearly identifies a practical problem (entropy instability), provides an insightful analysis of its causes, and presents an effective solution. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating significant performance improvements. While the novelty is incremental (building on existing techniques), the specific combination of ideas and the focus on LLM reasoning tasks makes this a worthwhile contribution. The primary weaknesses are the sensitivity of the algorithm to parameter tuning and lack of discussion regarding the generalizability of the approach to other tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Difference-Guided Reasoning: A Temporal-Spatial Framework for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20713v1)
- **Authors**: Hong Su
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are important tools for reasoning and problem-solving, while they often operate passively, answering questions without actively discovering new ones. This limitation reduces their ability to simulate human-like thinking, where noticing differences is a key trigger for reasoning. Thus, in this paper we propose a difference-guided reasoning framework, which enables LLMs to identify and act upon changes across time and space. The model formalizes differences through feature extraction, prioritizes the most impactful and latest changes, and links them to appropriate actions. We further extend the framework with mechanisms for abnormal behavior detection and the integration of external information from users or sensors, ensuring more reliable and grounded reasoning. Verification results show that prompting LLMs with differences improves focus on critical issues, leading to higher alignment with desired reasoning outcomes compared to direct prompting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "difference-guided reasoning framework" for Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to explicitly prompt LLMs to identify differences across time and space, arguing that this mimics human reasoning more closely and allows LLMs to be more proactive in problem-solving, rather than simply responding to prompts.  The framework includes components for temporal difference reasoning (analyzing changes over time), spatial difference reasoning (analyzing differences between subcomponents of an object), abnormal behavior detection, and the integration of external information to improve the accuracy and grounding of the reasoning process.  The authors present experimental results using ChatGPT-5 on a temporal difference task (analyzing a driving scene) and a spatial difference task (analyzing the seating layout in a metro train). The results indicate that explicitly prompting for differences improves the LLM's ability to focus on critical changes and produce more accurate reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of prompting LLMs to identify differences is not entirely novel. Contrastive learning and prompting techniques already exist. However, the paper's systematic approach of framing difference detection as the *core* of the reasoning process, and building a framework around it with specific mechanisms for temporal/spatial analysis and abnormality detection, represents a contribution.  The paper also presents a clear, understandable structure for integrating external information in difference-detection scenarios.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a recognized limitation of LLMs: their passivity in problem-solving. The proposed framework has the potential to improve LLM performance in domains where noticing changes and anomalies is crucial (e.g., autonomous driving, security monitoring, fraud detection). The experimental verification, although limited in scope (two scenarios, one LLM), provides initial evidence that the approach is effective. The potential for better grounding of LLM reasonability with the addition of external inputs would be helpful in fields requiring human supervision, or those that require clear auditability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured framework.
    *   Addresses a relevant and important problem.
    *   Demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach with experimental results.
    *   Offers a structured approach that can allow better external observation of LLM decisionmaking.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope of experiments. Only ChatGPT-5 is used, and the scenarios are relatively simple.
    *   The improvement may be strongly influenced by the specific prompts, needing testing for robustness with a wider range of prompts.
    *   The 'reference' statements against which the results are compared may be influencing the evaluation.
    *   The paper needs to be more specific on *how* the impact function, crucial for filtering the detected differences, is defined and implemented.  This seems to be left entirely to the LLM.
    *   The approach relies heavily on prompt engineering, which is itself a fragile art. The authors should address how to make the difference-guided prompting more robust and less dependent on carefully crafted prompts.
    *   There is lack of a clear comparison against other related techniques that might already focus on feature extraction, change detection, or anomaly detection using LLMs.  The "Related Work" section touches on this, but a more direct comparison would be beneficial.
    *   The results are also limited to simple problems with obvious solutions, meaning that it's unknown if this method scales to more complex problems.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more proactive and adaptable LLMs. The framework provides a useful starting point for researchers interested in incorporating difference-based reasoning into LLMs. It could also inform the design of new prompt engineering techniques and evaluation metrics. The introduction of external inputs could be transformative to auditability.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper offers a valuable framework and demonstrates initial promise. However, the limited scope of the experiments and the reliance on specific prompts necessitate a lower score. The lack of clear comparison to competing solutions, and questions related to the impact function also reduce the impact of the overall study.

### Cryptographic Backdoor for Neural Networks: Boon and Bane
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20714v1)
- **Authors**: Anh Tu Ngo, Anupam Chattopadhyay, Subhamoy Maitra
- **Abstract**: In this paper we show that cryptographic backdoors in a neural network (NN) can be highly effective in two directions, namely mounting the attacks as well as in presenting the defenses as well. On the attack side, a carefully planted cryptographic backdoor enables powerful and invisible attack on the NN. Considering the defense, we present applications: first, a provably robust NN watermarking scheme; second, a protocol for guaranteeing user authentication; and third, a protocol for tracking unauthorized sharing of the NN intellectual property (IP). From a broader theoretical perspective, borrowing the ideas from Goldwasser et. al. [FOCS 2022], our main contribution is to show that all these instantiated practical protocol implementations are provably robust. The protocols for watermarking, authentication and IP tracking resist an adversary with black-box access to the NN, whereas the backdoor-enabled adversarial attack is impossible to prevent under the standard assumptions. While the theoretical tools used for our attack is mostly in line with the Goldwasser et. al. ideas, the proofs related to the defense need further studies. Finally, all these protocols are implemented on state-of-the-art NN architectures with empirical results corroborating the theoretical claims. Further, one can utilize post-quantum primitives for implementing the cryptographic backdoors, laying out foundations for quantum-era applications in machine learning (ML).
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of cryptographic backdoors in neural networks (NNs) for both offensive and defensive purposes.  Inspired by Goldwasser et al.'s work, it demonstrates how a carefully designed cryptographic backdoor can enable powerful and stealthy attacks on NNs.  On the defense side, the paper proposes using cryptographic backdoors for NN watermarking, user authentication, and intellectual property (IP) tracking. The core idea is to leverage digital signatures to either manipulate NN outputs maliciously or provide secure access and ownership verification. The paper presents theoretical proofs of robustness for the defensive schemes, along with experimental results that validate the effectiveness and computational overhead of the proposed applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon the work of Goldwasser et al. by extending the theoretical concept of cryptographic backdoors to practical applications in secure ML.  While Goldwasser et al. introduced the idea, this paper goes further by providing concrete implementations and demonstrating their applicability to real-world scenarios like watermarking, authentication, and IP protection. The combination of cryptographic backdoors with these specific defense mechanisms represents a novel contribution. The attack vector is a more direct link to adversarial examples via cryptographic manipulation. The empirical validation of the proposed schemes is also a significant step forward.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating the potential of cryptographic techniques to address security and privacy concerns in the context of machine learning.  The rise of MLaaS and the vulnerability of DNNs to attacks make the need for robust security mechanisms crucial.  The proposed schemes offer a provable, if computationally intensive, approach to watermarking, authentication, and IP tracking, all important aspects of deploying ML models securely. Addressing the limitations of existing DNN watermarking schemes by providing a provable secure framework is highly significant. The demonstration of the attack side also highlights the need for greater security awareness in deploying ML models. The discussion of potential future usage with post-quantum primitives gives it longevity as well.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Theoretical grounding: The paper provides theoretical proofs for the robustness of the proposed defensive schemes, which enhances their credibility.
    *   Practical implementations: The experimental results demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed schemes and provide insights into their computational overhead.
    *   Comprehensive applications: The paper explores multiple use cases for cryptographic backdoors, highlighting their versatility.
    *   Addresses a growing need: This work directly addresses the security of Machine Learning assets, specifically in a world where AI is becoming widely deployed and more prone to attack.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational overhead: The computational cost of signature verification and message decoding is a significant bottleneck, limiting the scalability of the proposed schemes. The paper mentions parallelization but doesn't delve into specific optimization strategies. The experimental comparison of different digital signature schemes, while highlighting the overhead, does not provide a clear direction towards a computationally lighter, yet secure, solution.
    *   Black-box assumptions: The security of the proposed schemes relies heavily on the black-box assumption, which may not always hold in practice. Future work should explore defenses against white-box attacks.
    *   Limited experimental evaluation: While the paper presents experimental results, a more thorough evaluation with diverse datasets and NN architectures would strengthen the findings. For instance, the reliance on MNIST as a trigger set for watermark seems limited. The choice of watermark/signature triggers and sizes requires more rigorous investigation to ensure that the changes are visually imperceptible.
    * The paper's attack scenario, while innovative, relies on LSB steganography for embedding the signature and message. While computationally inexpensive, this technique is vulnerable to more sophisticated steganalysis. The choice to use such a simple technique undermines the overall robustness of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research in the area of secure ML by highlighting the potential of cryptographic techniques. It could also inspire the development of more efficient and scalable schemes for NN watermarking, authentication, and IP tracking. The paper clearly illustrates that crypto methods may be beneficial for securing AI models in the future, and provides a starting point for future research, bridging the gap between ML and crypto.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper demonstrates a significant, novel extension of Goldwasser et al.’s cryptographic backdoors, making concrete use cases in the secure ML domain with the three novel applications. The strengths of proven robustness and real-world implementation balance with weaknesses around computational cost, simplistic steganography and black-box assumptions to result in a final rating of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Impact of Audio Watermarking on Audio Anti-Spoofing Countermeasures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20736v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenshan Zhang, Xueping Zhang, Yechen Wang, Liwei Jin, Ming Li
- **Abstract**: This paper presents the first study on the impact of audio watermarking on spoofing countermeasures. While anti-spoofing systems are essential for securing speech-based applications, the influence of widely used audio watermarking, originally designed for copyright protection, remains largely unexplored. We construct watermark-augmented training and evaluation datasets, named the Watermark-Spoofing dataset, by applying diverse handcrafted and neural watermarking methods to existing anti-spoofing datasets. Experiments show that watermarking consistently degrades anti-spoofing performance, with higher watermark density correlating with higher Equal Error Rates (EERs). To mitigate this, we propose the Knowledge-Preserving Watermark Learning (KPWL) framework, enabling models to adapt to watermark-induced shifts while preserving their original-domain spoofing detection capability. These findings reveal audio watermarking as a previously overlooked domain shift and establish the first benchmark for developing watermark-resilient anti-spoofing systems. All related protocols are publicly available at https://github.com/Alphawarheads/Watermark_Spoofing.git
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and will provide a summary and a critical evaluation with a score.

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the impact of audio watermarking on the performance of audio anti-spoofing systems. The authors find that watermarking, a technique designed for copyright protection, can significantly degrade the accuracy of anti-spoofing detectors. To address this, they construct a Watermark-Spoofing dataset by applying various watermarking methods to existing anti-spoofing datasets. They then propose a Knowledge-Preserving Watermark Learning (KPWL) framework to mitigate the performance degradation caused by watermarking while maintaining original-domain spoofing detection capabilities. The results suggest that audio watermarking introduces a previously overlooked domain shift, and the KPWL framework can help models adapt to this shift.  The paper also creates a benchmark for developing watermark-resilient antispoofing systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of this work lies in identifying and quantifying the impact of audio watermarking on anti-spoofing systems. This is a previously unexplored area, and the paper provides empirical evidence of the degradation in performance caused by watermarking. The Watermark-Spoofing dataset is also a novel contribution, providing a resource for future research in this area. The KPWL framework, while building on existing domain adaptation techniques, is tailored to address the specific challenges posed by watermark-induced domain shifts. While the specific watermarking and anti-spoofing methods used are not novel in themselves, their combination and analysis in this context are.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper stems from the increasing importance of both audio anti-spoofing and audio watermarking in real-world applications. The paper highlights a potential vulnerability in speech-based security systems, as the presence of watermarks can compromise their ability to detect spoofed audio. This is particularly relevant in scenarios where both copyright protection and security are critical, such as in financial transactions or access control systems. The proposed KPWL framework offers a potential solution to mitigate this vulnerability, and the Watermark-Spoofing dataset provides a valuable resource for developing more robust anti-spoofing systems. The findings also underscore the importance of considering domain shifts when developing and evaluating machine learning models for security-sensitive applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of watermark-induced performance degradation in anti-spoofing systems.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough experimental evaluation using diverse watermarking methods, anti-spoofing models, and datasets.
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The Watermark-Spoofing dataset is a valuable contribution to the field, enabling further research in this area.
    *   **Effective Mitigation Strategy:** The KPWL framework offers a promising approach to mitigate the performance degradation caused by watermarking.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Publicly available protocols and datasets increase the reproducibility and impact of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Watermarking Methods:** While the paper explores several watermarking methods, there are many other techniques available, and the results may not generalize to all types of watermarks.
    *   **Complexity of KPWL:** The KPWL framework, while effective, adds complexity to the training process, and it may require careful tuning to achieve optimal performance. Although, the complexity of the framework compared to the baseline is acceptable.
    *   **Real-world Watermark Variety:** The generation of Watermark-Spoofing datasets use simulated watermark data and may not be fully representative of watermarks found in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Limited Analysis on KPWL Effectiveness for Unseen Watermarks:** The paper did not provide sufficient data in assessing how KPWL is effective towards spoofing samples using unseen watermarks (i.e., watermarks not used during training).

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust anti-spoofing systems that are resilient to the presence of watermarks. It also highlights the importance of considering the interaction between different signal processing techniques when designing secure systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the problem, the significance of the findings, the comprehensive experimental evaluation, and the promising mitigation strategy, the paper makes a substantial contribution to the field. While there are some limitations, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. It opens up a new research direction and provides a valuable resource for future work.

Score: 7.5

- **Score**: 7/10

### Parallel Thinking, Sequential Answering: Bridging NAR and AR for Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20744v1)
- **Authors**: Qihang Ai, Haiyun Jiang
- **Abstract**: We study reasoning tasks through a framework that integrates auto-regressive (AR) and non-autoregressive (NAR) language models. AR models, which generate text sequentially, excel at producing coherent outputs but often suffer from slow inference, particularly in reasoning-intensive domains such as mathematics and code, where lengthy chains of thought are required. In contrast, NAR models, such as discrete diffusion models, allow parallel generation and offer substantial speedups, though typically at the cost of reduced output quality. To address these limitations, we introduce a new paradigm in which an NAR model efficiently produces intermediate reasoning traces, which subsequently guide an AR model to deliver precise final answers. Experiments demonstrate that our approach yields significant 26% improvements over strong baselines while substantially reducing inference cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a new reasoning paradigm that combines non-autoregressive (NAR) and autoregressive (AR) language models for improved efficiency and accuracy in complex reasoning tasks. The approach leverages an NAR model (Mercury) to generate compact, explicit intermediate reasoning traces (the "think" stage) and then utilizes an AR model (GPT-5) to produce the final answer. This division of labor aims to capitalize on the strengths of both model types: the parallel generation and global coherence capabilities of NAR models and the reliability and expressiveness of AR models. Experiments on mathematical and coding tasks demonstrate that this combined approach achieves significant improvements in performance while reducing inference costs compared to using AR models alone. The study explores different routing strategies, specifically using Mercury for the "think" stage and GPT-5 for the "answer" stage (NAR→AR) or using Mercury for both (NAR→NAR), with the NAR→AR configuration showing superior performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining NAR and AR models in a specific two-stage reasoning pipeline is reasonably novel. While hybrid approaches exist, the explicit focus on using an NAR model for generating intermediate reasoning traces to guide an AR model for final answer generation presents a new perspective on model collaboration.  The idea of decoupling the think and answer stages using different architectures is also innovative.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the field of large language models: balancing reasoning performance with computational efficiency.  The "overthinking" problem and the computational burden of long reasoning sequences are well-recognized issues. This paper's proposed solution offers a tangible way to mitigate these problems, potentially leading to more practical and scalable reasoning systems. Showing that NAR models can generate reasonable thinking traces to guide AR models significantly broadens the usability of NAR models beyond just text generation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of solely relying on AR models for reasoning-intensive tasks and highlights the need for more efficient approaches.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The proposed NAR+AR framework is clearly explained, along with the rationale for the chosen model combination.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across multiple reasoning domains (mathematics and coding), providing strong evidence of its potential.
    *   **Quantifiable Improvements:**  The paper reports significant performance gains over baseline methods, which strengthens the claims of improved accuracy and efficiency.
    *   **Division-of-Labor Justification:** The paper provides arguments supporting the separation of "thinking" and "answering" tasks and the benefit of using different architectures for each.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Variety:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on one specific NAR model (Mercury) and one AR model (GPT-5). While these are strong representatives, exploring a broader range of models and configurations would further strengthen the generalizability of the findings. The paper could have benefited from experimenting with other NAR models (e.g., more diffusion-based models) or using different prompting strategies.
    *   **Dataset Selection:** While the datasets used are relevant, they could benefit from more extensive, more complex benchmarks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  While the study compares NAR→NAR and NAR→AR configurations, more detailed ablation studies could provide a deeper understanding of the contribution of each component. For example, varying the length and quality of the NAR traces or explicitly measuring the reduction in inference time compared to a purely AR-based approach would be valuable.
    *   **No detailed cost/performance analysis:** Though the abstract mentions "substantial reduction in inference cost", a detailed evaluation of the actual inference time or computational cost savings would be welcome.
    *   **Limited generalization:** A lot of the improvements seem to come in cases that the AR model does not perform very well in. More challenging benchmarks where AR methods perform well would showcase the limitations of the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   **Encouraging Hybrid Reasoning Architectures:** The success of the NAR+AR framework may inspire other researchers to explore hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of different model types.
    *   **Rethinking Reasoning Pipelines:** The division-of-labor concept could lead to new designs for reasoning pipelines, where specific components are optimized for different stages of the reasoning process.
    *   **Improving the Efficiency of Reasoning Models:** The demonstrated reduction in inference costs could motivate further research into efficient reasoning techniques, making reasoning models more accessible and practical.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper presents a novel and promising approach to address the challenges of reasoning in large language models. The idea of dividing the reasoning process between NAR and AR models is well-motivated and supported by empirical results. The paper's potential influence on future research is also notable.

However, the paper's limitations, particularly the limited model variety, the lack of detailed ablation studies, and missing detailed cost/performance analysis, prevent it from achieving a higher score. The work could benefit from broader exploration and more detailed analysis to solidify the findings and fully demonstrate its potential impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### FreeInsert: Personalized Object Insertion with Geometric and Style Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20756v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhong Zhang, Han Wang, Yiwen Wang, Rong Xie, Li Song
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have made significant progress in image generation, allowing for effortless customized generation. However, existing image editing methods still face certain limitations when dealing with personalized image composition tasks. First, there is the issue of lack of geometric control over the inserted objects. Current methods are confined to 2D space and typically rely on textual instructions, making it challenging to maintain precise geometric control over the objects. Second, there is the challenge of style consistency. Existing methods often overlook the style consistency between the inserted object and the background, resulting in a lack of realism. In addition, the challenge of inserting objects into images without extensive training remains significant. To address these issues, we propose \textit{FreeInsert}, a novel training-free framework that customizes object insertion into arbitrary scenes by leveraging 3D geometric information. Benefiting from the advances in existing 3D generation models, we first convert the 2D object into 3D, perform interactive editing at the 3D level, and then re-render it into a 2D image from a specified view. This process introduces geometric controls such as shape or view. The rendered image, serving as geometric control, is combined with style and content control achieved through diffusion adapters, ultimately producing geometrically controlled, style-consistent edited images via the diffusion model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FreeInsert: Personalized Object Insertion with Geometric and Style Control":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FreeInsert, a novel training-free framework for personalized object insertion into images. It addresses limitations of existing methods by providing geometric control over inserted objects (shape, view, pose) and ensuring style consistency with the background. The core idea is a "2D→3D→2D" pipeline. First, the object is lifted into 3D using an image-to-3D model, enabling interactive 3D editing for pose and view manipulation.  The edited 3D model is then re-rendered into 2D.  Finally, this rendered image, combined with the original object and background images, is used as a condition for a diffusion model (SDXL) to generate the final image with desired geometric and style consistency. The method leverages diffusion adapters for style and content control, avoiding the need for extensive training or fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in combining 3D geometric control with diffusion-based image generation for personalized object insertion without requiring training.  While elements like image-to-3D and diffusion models are not new individually, the integrated pipeline and the emphasis on precise geometric manipulation are a notable contribution. The use of a "2D -> 3D -> 2D" approach is related to Image Sculpturing [35], but FreeInsert focuses on object insertion in a different image, and considers style consistency. This does improve upon related work, but the increment of novelty is modest.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a practical problem in image editing.  The ability to insert objects into scenes with specific poses, viewpoints, and consistent styles is highly desirable for various applications (e.g., creating visualizations, product placement, artistic expression). Avoiding per-object training is a major advantage, making the method more flexible and accessible. Potential for further development within e-commerce, artistic tools, and design.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-Free:** A major selling point is the training-free nature of the method. This simplifies the workflow and significantly reduces computational costs.
    *   **Geometric Control:**  The 3D editing pipeline offers precise control over object pose, viewpoint, and shape, which is a significant improvement over methods relying solely on textual prompts.
    *   **Style Consistency:** The use of diffusion adapters for style transfer helps to ensure a seamless integration of the object into the background.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides both qualitative and quantitative results, comparing FreeInsert to other methods. The ablation study helps to understand the contributions of individual components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Image-to-3D Quality:**  The pipeline's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the initial 3D reconstruction.  As the paper acknowledges, existing image-to-3D models can produce coarse geometry and artifacts, which can impact the final result, especially for detailed structures like human faces.
    *   **Limited to coarse geometry edits:** Due to the reliance of Image-to-3D models, this method is limited to edits that can be done on the entire mesh. Precise editing is still a challenge.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While training-free, the 3D reconstruction and rendering steps can still be computationally expensive, especially for complex scenes.
    *   **Potential for Style Leakage:** The diffusion adapters, while helpful for style transfer, may inadvertently alter the content of the original object.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach has the potential to influence future research in personalized image editing. It presents a compelling framework for combining 3D geometry with diffusion models and could inspire new methods for object insertion, scene composition, and content creation.
* **Rigorous rationale for the score:** While the paper presents a sound method, it suffers from limitations in the quality of 3D reconstruction and the reliance on external models. The method's contribution is not revolutionary but rather a clever integration of existing techniques.

**Score: 6**

**Reasoning:**

A score of 6 reflects a paper that makes a worthwhile contribution but has limitations. The "2D->3D->2D" pipeline combined with diffusion models for controlled object insertion is a novel approach, warranting acknowledgement. However, the significant reliance on the quality of image-to-3D models and the potential computational overhead temper the paper's overall impact. The training-free nature and geometric control are strong positives, but the method needs further refinement to address the limitations in detail and fidelity.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### SFT Doesn't Always Hurt General Capabilities: Revisiting Domain-Specific Fine-Tuning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20758v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Lin, Zhongruo Wang, Kun Qian, Tian Wang, Arvind Srinivasan, Hansi Zeng, Ruochen Jiao, Xie Zhou, Jiri Gesi, Dakuo Wang, Yufan Guo, Kai Zhong, Weiqi Zhang, Sujay Sanghavi, Changyou Chen, Hyokun Yun, Lihong Li
- **Abstract**: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on domain-specific datasets is a common approach to adapt Large Language Models (LLMs) to specialized tasks but is often believed to degrade their general capabilities. In this work, we revisit this trade-off and present both empirical and theoretical insights. First, we show that SFT does not always hurt: using a smaller learning rate can substantially mitigate general performance degradation while preserving comparable target-domain performance. We then provide a theoretical analysis that explains these phenomena and further motivates a new method, Token-Adaptive Loss Reweighting (TALR). Building on this, and recognizing that smaller learning rates alone do not fully eliminate general-performance degradation in all cases, we evaluate a range of strategies for reducing general capability loss, including L2 regularization, LoRA, model averaging, FLOW, and our proposed TALR. Experimental results demonstrate that while no method completely eliminates the trade-off, TALR consistently outperforms these baselines in balancing domain-specific gains and general capabilities. Finally, we distill our findings into practical guidelines for adapting LLMs to new domains: (i) using a small learning rate to achieve a favorable trade-off, and (ii) when a stronger balance is further desired, adopt TALR as an effective strategy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper draft:

**Summary:**

The paper revisits the common belief that domain-specific fine-tuning (SFT) of Large Language Models (LLMs) inevitably degrades their general capabilities. The authors challenge this assumption and present both empirical and theoretical evidence showing that this isn't always the case.  Specifically, they demonstrate that using a smaller learning rate during SFT can substantially mitigate the loss of general-purpose performance while maintaining comparable performance on the target domain. They provide a theoretical analysis to explain this phenomenon and propose a new method, Token-Adaptive Loss Reweighting (TALR), that adaptively adjusts token weights during training to balance domain-specific learning and general capability preservation. The paper evaluates various strategies for mitigating general capability loss, including L2 regularization, LoRA, model averaging, and FLOW, showing that TALR outperforms these baselines in balancing domain-specific gains and general capabilities. The work concludes with practical guidelines for adapting LLMs to new domains, advocating for the use of small learning rates and TALR for achieving a favorable trade-off.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its challenging of a widely accepted assumption within the LLM fine-tuning community. While the idea of using smaller learning rates isn't entirely new, the thorough empirical investigation and theoretical justification for why it works *particularly well* for LLMs is a novel and significant contribution. The introduction of TALR, a token-adaptive loss reweighting strategy, demonstrates a solid attempt to improve on existing methods for managing the trade-off between domain-specific adaptation and general performance. That being said, other data weighting or manipulation methodologies have been well explored in Machine learning, so the novelty in this aspect is lower.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant practical implications for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs.  The demonstration that general capability degradation isn't a foregone conclusion when fine-tuning allows for a more optimistic approach to domain adaptation. The practical guidelines provided (smaller learning rates, TALR) offer immediate value to those looking to improve LLM performance in specialized areas without sacrificing broader functionalities. The comprehensive empirical evaluation provides a good benchmark for the efficacy of different mitigations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a solid experimental setup across multiple models, datasets (MedCalc, ESCI, MetaMathQA), and general-purpose benchmarks (IFEval, GSM8K, HumanEval). This comprehensive evaluation strengthens the credibility of the findings.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The inclusion of a theoretical analysis, grounded in information theory and compression principles, provides a deeper understanding of the observed phenomena.  This helps move beyond purely empirical observations and allows for more informed method design.
    *   **Practical Guidelines:** The paper concludes with actionable guidelines for practitioners, making the research readily applicable to real-world scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper evaluates a range of techniques, it acknowledges that none completely eliminate the trade-off between domain-specific gains and general capability preservation. This suggests that further research into more sophisticated mitigation strategies is needed.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** The primary empirical focus on MedCalc and ESCI might raise concerns about the generalizability of the findings to other domains. While MetaMathQA is included, expanding the set of evaluated domains would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Theoretical Simplifications:** The theoretical analysis uses simplifying assumptions (e.g., exponential tilting) which might not perfectly capture the intricacies of SFT.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM fine-tuning, encouraging more careful consideration of learning rates and the development of more sophisticated mitigation strategies. The token-adaptive loss reweighting approach could serve as a foundation for further exploration of dynamic loss adjustment techniques. The work emphasizes the importance of balancing domain adaptation with general capability preservation and has the potential to lead to more robust and versatile LLMs.

**Overall:** The paper provides a valuable contribution to the understanding of LLM fine-tuning and offers practical solutions for mitigating the negative effects of domain-specific adaptation. The combination of strong empirical evidence and theoretical justification makes this a significant piece of work. However, the limitations in mitigation strategy scope and theoretical simplification prevent it from reaching the highest levels of impact. The empirical findings are convincing but the novelty is less so.

### Measuring LLM Sensitivity in Transformer-based Tabular Data Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20768v1)
- **Authors**: Maria F. Davila R, Azizjon Turaev, Wolfram Wingerath
- **Abstract**: Synthetic tabular data is used for privacy-preserving data sharing and data-driven model development. Its effectiveness, however, depends heavily on the used Tabular Data Synthesis (TDS) tool. Recent studies have shown that Transformer-based models outperform other state-of-the-art models such as Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and Diffusion models in terms of data quality. However, Transformer-based models also come with high computational costs, making them sometimes unfeasible for end users with prosumer hardware. This study presents a sensitivity assessment on how the choice of hyperparameters, such as number of layers or hidden dimension affects the quality of the resultant synthetic data and the computational performance. It is performed across two tools, GReaT and REaLTabFormer, evaluating 10 model setups that vary in architecture type and depth. We assess the sensitivity on three dimensions: runtime, machine learning (ML) utility, and similarity to real data distributions. Experiments were conducted on four real-world datasets. Our findings reveal that runtime is proportional to the number of hyperparameters, with shallower configurations completing faster. GReaT consistently achieves lower runtimes than REaLTabFormer, and only on the largest dataset they have comparable runtime. For small datasets, both tools achieve synthetic data with high utility and optimal similarity, but on larger datasets only REaLTabFormer sustains strong utility and similarity. As a result, REaLTabFormer with lightweight LLMs provides the best balance, since it preserves data quality while reducing computational requirements. Nonetheless, its runtime remains higher than that of GReaT and other TDS tools, suggesting that efficiency gains are possible but only up to a certain level.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the sensitivity of Transformer-based tabular data synthesis (TDS) tools (specifically GReaT and REaLTabFormer) to the choice of large language models (LLMs) and their hyperparameters, such as the number of layers and hidden dimensions. The study assesses the impact of these choices on runtime, machine learning (ML) utility, and data similarity across four real-world datasets. The key findings suggest that GReaT generally exhibits faster runtimes, but REaLTabFormer provides better ML utility and similarity, especially on larger datasets, making it a more robust option when balancing computational cost and data quality. The paper concludes that reducing the complexity of LLMs in REaLTabFormer can improve computational efficiency without significantly compromising data quality, but that improvements are limited.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic sensitivity analysis of LLM hyperparameters within Transformer-based TDS tools. While previous work has explored the general performance of these tools, the paper delves into the specific impact of architectural choices (layers, dimensions) on three key dimensions. This granular exploration is a valuable contribution, helping practitioners make informed decisions about LLM selection and configuration. Furthermore, the comparison of GReaT and REaLTabFormer provides practical insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because Transformer-based TDS tools are becoming increasingly popular for privacy-preserving data sharing and model development. The high computational cost associated with these tools, however, limits their accessibility. This study addresses this limitation by identifying strategies to reduce computational burden without sacrificing data quality. The findings can guide the development of more efficient and accessible TDS solutions. The systematic evaluation framework also serves as a model for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study evaluates performance along three relevant dimensions (runtime, ML utility, and similarity), providing a holistic perspective.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The systematic variation of LLM hyperparameters and the use of multiple datasets strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical recommendations for practitioners on selecting and configuring LLMs for TDS.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper clearly describes the experimental setup, datasets, and metrics, promoting reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to two specific TDS tools (GReaT and REaLTabFormer) and a selected set of LLM families. It is possible that the observed trade-offs do not generalize to other Transformer-based TDS architectures. Expanding the toolset for sensitivity assessment would enhance the impact of this work.
    *   **Lack of Privacy Analysis:** The study does not explicitly assess privacy preservation, which is a critical consideration in synthetic data generation. While ML utility and similarity are important, they should be considered in conjunction with privacy.
    *   **Dataset Diversity:** While the datasets cover classification and regression tasks, they may not represent the full spectrum of real-world tabular data. Including very large-scale and high-dimensional datasets would increase the robustness of the findings. Also, including imbalanced data sets would make the findings more practical for the audience.

*   **Impact:** The study has the potential to influence the design and deployment of Transformer-based TDS tools. The findings can guide developers in optimizing their tools for computational efficiency without compromising data quality. The paper can also inform practitioners on selecting appropriate LLM configurations for their specific needs and datasets.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed sensitivity analysis of LLM hyperparameters in Transformer-based TDS. The findings are practical and have the potential to influence the field. However, the limitations regarding the limited scope of tools and LLMs, the lack of privacy analysis, and the limited diversity of datasets somewhat constrain the overall impact. If the research were expanded to include more data sets with varying features and sizes, that would significantly increase its novelty and overall significance. Nevertheless, this research provides a solid foundation for future work in this area.
