### CusEnhancer: A Zero-Shot Scene and Controllability Enhancement Method for Photo Customization via ResInversion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20775v1)
- **Authors**: Maoye Ren, Praneetha Vaddamanu, Jianjin Xu, Fernando De la Torre Frade
- **Abstract**: Recently remarkable progress has been made in synthesizing realistic human photos using text-to-image diffusion models. However, current approaches face degraded scenes, insufficient control, and suboptimal perceptual identity. We introduce CustomEnhancer, a novel framework to augment existing identity customization models. CustomEnhancer is a zero-shot enhancement pipeline that leverages face swapping techniques, pretrained diffusion model, to obtain additional representations in a zeroshot manner for encoding into personalized models. Through our proposed triple-flow fused PerGeneration approach, which identifies and combines two compatible counter-directional latent spaces to manipulate a pivotal space of personalized model, we unify the generation and reconstruction processes, realizing generation from three flows. Our pipeline also enables comprehensive training-free control over the generation process of personalized models, offering precise controlled personalization for them and eliminating the need for controller retraining for per-model. Besides, to address the high time complexity of null-text inversion (NTI), we introduce ResInversion, a novel inversion method that performs noise rectification via a pre-diffusion mechanism, reducing the inversion time by 129 times. Experiments demonstrate that CustomEnhancer reach SOTA results at scene diversity, identity fidelity, training-free controls, while also showing the efficiency of our ResInversion over NTI. The code will be made publicly available upon paper acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CustomEnhancer, a zero-shot enhancement framework for personalized photo customization via diffusion models. The method addresses limitations of current training-based personalization techniques, specifically degraded scene generation capability, control module incompatibility, and suboptimal perceptual identity. CustomEnhancer leverages face swapping, a pre-trained SDXL model, and a novel "BiMD" (Bidirectionally Manipulated Diffusion) approach to integrate diverse scene representations and customized identity representations. It further proposes "ResInversion," a fast inversion technique for improved efficiency.  The method claims to achieve state-of-the-art results in scene diversity, identity fidelity, and training-free controls, while significantly reducing inversion time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components, including the CustomEnhancer pipeline, BiMD approach, and ResInversion technique. The key novelty appears to lie in the *integration* of existing techniques (face swapping, SDXL) with novel methods (BiMD and ResInversion) to achieve a more comprehensive and efficient personalization framework. The BiMD approach of combining forward (generation) and backward (reconstruction) latent spaces to manipulate a personalized model is a particularly interesting contribution. ResInversion also seems promising for addressing the high time complexity of null-text inversion.

* **Significance:** The paper targets a very active area of research in diffusion models: personalized image generation. Addressing the common problems of degraded scenes, limited controllability, and identity fidelity makes this work significant. The zero-shot and training-free control aspects of the method are also attractive, as they mitigate the need for extensive fine-tuning and per-model training of control modules. If the claimed performance improvements are robust, CustomEnhancer could become a valuable tool for enhancing existing personalization methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Solution:**  The paper tackles multiple limitations of existing personalization methods, offering a more holistic solution.
    * **Modular Design:** The plug-in nature of CustomEnhancer is beneficial, allowing it to be easily integrated with other diffusion-based models.
    * **Efficiency:**  The ResInversion technique offers a significant speed-up compared to null-text inversion.
    * **Quantitative and Qualitative Results:** The paper provides both quantitative metrics (face similarity, FID, etc.) and qualitative examples to support its claims.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Existing Models:** The framework relies heavily on the performance of pre-trained models (SDXL) and face swapping techniques. Its success is thus contingent on the continued improvement of these underlying components. This can also be a strength.
    * **Experimental Rigor:** While the paper presents quantitative results, it's essential to examine the details. Were the comparisons made with carefully tuned baselines? Were the metrics calculated according to accepted standards? It's critical to scrutinize these aspects for potential biases.
    * **Generalization:**  The experiments primarily focus on human faces. It's not clear how well the method would generalize to other object categories or domains.
    * **Ablation Studies:** Although it has "module ablation" and "Manipulation starting step" studies. These experiments aren't comprehensive enough. For instance, it is missing ablation study that measures each component, and some ablation study results are not intuitive.
    * **Writing Quality:** Parts of the paper could benefit from clearer explanations and more concise writing.  Some descriptions of the technical components are dense and difficult to follow.

* **Potential Impact:** If the method consistently delivers improved scene diversity, identity fidelity, and training-free controls while remaining efficient, it could significantly impact the development of personalized image generation tools.  It could also encourage further research into combining existing and novel techniques to address complex problems in generative modeling.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  Here's the rationale:

The paper demonstrates a significant engineering effort in integrating multiple techniques to address important limitations in personalized image generation. The novelty is good (BiMD, ResInversion, combination approach), the results are promising, and the impact, if sustained, could be noticeable. However, its dependence on existing models, need for more comprehensive experiments, limited generality and writing quality detract from a higher score.  The improvement could be incremental than ground breaking.

### Towards Atoms of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20784v1)
- **Authors**: Chenhui Hu, Pengfei Cao, Yubo Chen, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Abstract**: The fundamental units of internal representations in large language models (LLMs) remain undefined, limiting further understanding of their mechanisms. Neurons or features are often regarded as such units, yet neurons suffer from polysemy, while features face concerns of unreliable reconstruction and instability. To address this issue, we propose the Atoms Theory, which defines such units as atoms. We introduce the atomic inner product (AIP) to correct representation shifting, formally define atoms, and prove the conditions that atoms satisfy the Restricted Isometry Property (RIP), ensuring stable sparse representations over atom set and linking to compressed sensing. Under stronger conditions, we further establish the uniqueness and exact $\ell_1$ recoverability of the sparse representations, and provide guarantees that single-layer sparse autoencoders (SAEs) with threshold activations can reliably identify the atoms. To validate the Atoms Theory, we train threshold-activated SAEs on Gemma2-2B, Gemma2-9B, and Llama3.1-8B, achieving 99.9% sparse reconstruction across layers on average, and more than 99.8% of atoms satisfy the uniqueness condition, compared to 0.5% for neurons and 68.2% for features, showing that atoms more faithfully capture intrinsic representations of LLMs. Scaling experiments further reveal the link between SAEs size and recovery capacity. Overall, this work systematically introduces and validates Atoms Theory of LLMs, providing a theoretical framework for understanding internal representations and a foundation for mechanistic interpretability. Code available at https://github.com/ChenhuiHu/towards_atoms.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Atoms of Large Language Models".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel theoretical framework called "Atoms Theory" for understanding the internal representations of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors argue that traditional units like neurons or features are insufficient due to polysemy and instability issues, respectively.  Instead, they propose "atoms" as the fundamental units, defined using a new metric called the "atomic inner product" (AIP) to correct representation shifting.  The paper proves that these atoms, under certain conditions, satisfy the Restricted Isometry Property (RIP), ensuring stable sparse representations and linking them to compressed sensing.  They further demonstrate the identifiability of these atoms using single-layer sparse autoencoders (SAEs) with threshold activations. Empirical validation on Gemma2-2B, Gemma2-9B, and Llama3.1-8B shows that SAEs can effectively recover these atoms, and these atoms exhibit properties of uniqueness and recoverability compared to neurons and features. The paper also explores the scaling behavior of SAEs and its relation to recovery capacity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The concept of "atoms" as fundamental units is a significant departure from the typical neuron or feature-based analyses. The "atomic inner product" (AIP) is a novel contribution to addressing representation shifting, and provides a more accurate representation of LLM geometry.  The theoretical proofs linking atoms to RIP and demonstrating SAE identifiability are non-trivial and add substantial value.
*   **Significance:** If validated broadly, Atoms Theory could have a substantial impact on the field of mechanistic interpretability.  By providing a more stable and well-defined unit, it could simplify the task of understanding how LLMs encode and process information. This has potential implications for improving model robustness, safety, and controllability. The recovery of these atoms using SAEs is particularly important, as it provides an avenue for actual practical identification and study. It provides a path towards dissecting the "black box" nature of LLMs, bridging the gap between theoretical understanding and practical manipulation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper is grounded in rigorous mathematical proofs and builds a comprehensive theory encompassing modeling, recovery mechanisms, and provable guarantees.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The empirical results, including sparse reconstruction accuracy, atomicity tests, and comparative analysis, provide convincing support for the Atoms Theory. Validation across a variety of different LLM families (Gemma, Llama) provides further robustness and applicability.
    *   **Addressing Limitations of Previous Approaches:** The paper directly addresses the shortcomings of existing methods (neurons and features) and offers a solution that is both theoretically sound and empirically validated.
    *  **Clear and Well-Structured Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and logically structured, facilitating understanding of the complex concepts.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational resources required for atom identification, especially as models scale. Is the AIP calculation efficient for extremely large models? Is the SAE training process computationally feasible for larger models? This is a crucial consideration for the practicality of the theory.
    *   **Interpretation of Atoms:** While the paper demonstrates the existence and recoverability of atoms, the *interpretation* of what these atoms *mean* remains a significant challenge. The "case studies" in the appendix, while insightful, are not comprehensive enough to show if all these atoms have a consistent meaning for a given phenomenon within the model. Semantic understanding and interpretability of Atoms needs further development. This is crucial for practical use.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the theory is validated on multiple models, future work needs to explore Atoms Theory on a more diverse set of architectures and tasks. Do atoms behave similarly in vision-language models, for example?
    * **Limitations of SAE based Identification:** While SAEs provide a way to find atoms it is worth noting that identification method may introduce some biases or might not be optimal. Are the recovered atoms truly atomic or artifacts of the SAE architecture? Further work could be devoted to exploring other atom identification methods and comparing.
    *   **Scalability and Practicality**: The scaling experiments address this point somewhat but the paper lacks a deep dive into whether the theory is practical and can be efficiently applied in the limit of increasingly large LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence future research in mechanistic interpretability, providing a new direction for understanding and manipulating LLM behavior. Further research might explore the use of Atoms Theory to improve model editing, control model biases, and develop more robust and reliable AI systems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-supported theoretical framework that addresses significant limitations of existing approaches in LLM interpretability. The theory is rigorously developed, empirically validated, and offers a promising direction for future research. However, the interpretation of the atoms and computational cost considerations warrant a slightly lower score. The paper's impact will depend on its ability to foster further research and provide actionable insights for improving LLMs, as well as practicality for very large models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LogReasoner: Empowering LLMs with Expert-like Coarse-to-Fine Reasoning for Log Analysis Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20798v1)
- **Authors**: Lipeng Ma, Yixuan Li, Weidong Yang, Mingjie Zhou, Xinyi Liu, Ben Fei, Shuhao Li, Xiaoyan Sun, Sihang Jiang, Yanghua Xiao
- **Abstract**: Log analysis is crucial for monitoring system health and diagnosing failures in complex systems. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities for automated log analysis, leveraging their reasoning capabilities to perform tasks such as anomaly detection and failure prediction. However, general-purpose LLMs struggle to formulate structured reasoning workflows that align with expert cognition and deliver precise details of reasoning steps. To address these challenges, we propose LogReasoner, a coarse-to-fine reasoning enhancement framework designed to enable LLMs to reason log analysis tasks like experts. LogReasoner consists of two stages: (1) coarse-grained enhancement of expert thinking, where high-level expert thoughts are constructed from collected troubleshooting flowcharts and existing tasks to enable LLMs to formulate structured reasoning workflows and (2) fine-grained enhancement of specific steps, where we first fine-tune the LLM with task-specific stepwise solutions to enhance the LLM for instantiated reasoning, then employ the preference learning to calibrate the LLM's reasoning details from its mistakes, further strengthen the LLM's analytical granularity and correctness. We evaluate LogReasoner on four distinct log analysis tasks using open-source LLMs such as Qwen-2.5 and Llama-3. Experimental results show that LogReasoner significantly outperforms existing LLMs, achieving state-of-the-art performance and demonstrating its effectiveness in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs for log analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LogReasoner paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LogReasoner, a novel coarse-to-fine reasoning enhancement framework designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on log analysis tasks. Addressing the limitations of general-purpose LLMs, which struggle with structured reasoning workflows and handling detailed log information, LogReasoner aims to equip LLMs with expert-like reasoning capabilities. The framework comprises two stages: 1) Coarse-grained enhancement of expert thinking, where structured reasoning workflows are built based on troubleshooting flowcharts and existing tasks, and 2) Fine-grained enhancement of specific steps, where Imitation Learning and Steps of Calibration refine reasoning details using Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). The paper evaluates LogReasoner on four log analysis tasks using open-source LLMs, demonstrating significant performance improvements over existing LLMs, including those with proprietary architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem: improving LLM performance in a specific, complex domain. Log analysis has real-world significance, and the limitations of directly applying general-purpose LLMs are well-articulated.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the shortcomings of general-purpose LLMs in handling structured reasoning and detailed information required for effective log analysis.
*   **Novel Framework:** LogReasoner provides a well-structured and comprehensive approach, combining coarse-grained and fine-grained enhancements to address the identified limitations. The combination of imitation learning and DPO for stepwise refinement is a strong architectural choice.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper presents an extensive evaluation across multiple tasks, models (including open-source and proprietary ones), and datasets. Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component. The out-of-distribution evaluation highlights the generalization capability.
*   **Practical Considerations:** The paper acknowledges and addresses practical considerations, such as the cost-effectiveness of the framework by using open-source LLMs. The case study demonstrates the value of interpretable reasoning chains.
* **Significant Performance Gain**: The paper demonstrates remarkable improvements across several benchmarks compared to other LLMs, including GPT-4.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on GPT-4 for Data Generation:**  While the paper addresses cost, the use of GPT-4 for creating the initial training data introduces a dependence and potential bias. Using a less powerful LLM initially or implementing a more robust data generation strategy may be helpful.
*   **Limited Generalizability in the Task**: The experiments are limited to a specific set of log analysis tasks. The applicability of LogReasoner to other specialized domains remains unexplored.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While overall performance metrics are strong, a deeper dive into specific types of errors or failure cases might be useful for improvement. Providing more qualitative examples and detailed analysis of the model's reasoning would further enhance confidence.
*   **Modest Novelty beyond Architecture Selection**: While the combination of methods into a single framework is novel, all individual components have precedent in the broader deep learning literature (Imitation Learning, CoT prompting, and DPO). The main novelty is selecting an architecture and providing a very good evaluation.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a practical and effective solution for enhancing LLM performance in log analysis. The framework bridges the gap between general-purpose LLMs and domain-specific expertise, paving the way for more reliable and automated log analysis systems. The paper also raises important questions about the limitations of general-purpose LLMs and the need for specialized frameworks in complex domains. The paper also raises important questions regarding the generalization ability of LLMs in this task and presents data that suggests LogReasoner maintains significant performance improvements on those models.

**Overall:**

LogReasoner has merit as a domain-specific optimization framework for log analysis tasks, which has the potential to improve several real-world processes. The comprehensive evaluation and consideration of practical aspects are commendable. However, the reliance on a commercial model in the initial stage and the limited scope of evaluation slightly dampen the impact.

### Few-Shot and Training-Free Review Generation via Conversational Prompting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20805v1)
- **Authors**: Genki Kusano
- **Abstract**: Personalized review generation helps businesses understand user preferences, yet most existing approaches assume extensive review histories of the target user or require additional model training. Real-world applications often face few-shot and training-free situations, where only a few user reviews are available and fine-tuning is infeasible. It is well known that large language models (LLMs) can address such low-resource settings, but their effectiveness depends on prompt engineering. In this paper, we propose Conversational Prompting, a lightweight method that reformulates user reviews as multi-turn conversations. Its simple variant, Simple Conversational Prompting (SCP), relies solely on the user's own reviews, while the contrastive variant, Contrastive Conversational Prompting (CCP), inserts reviews from other users or LLMs as incorrect replies and then asks the model to correct them, encouraging the model to produce text in the user's style. Experiments on eight product domains and five LLMs showed that the conventional non-conversational prompt often produced reviews similar to those written by random users, based on text-based metrics such as ROUGE-L and BERTScore, and application-oriented tasks like user identity matching and sentiment analysis. In contrast, both SCP and CCP produced reviews much closer to those of the target user, even when each user had only two reviews. CCP brings further improvements when high-quality negative examples are available, whereas SCP remains competitive when such data cannot be collected. These results suggest that conversational prompting offers a practical solution for review generation under few-shot and training-free constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Conversational Prompting (CP), a novel, training-free method for personalized review generation in few-shot settings using Large Language Models (LLMs).  CP reformulates user review histories into multi-turn conversations, allowing the LLM to better capture the target user's writing style. Two variants are proposed: Simple Conversational Prompting (SCP), which uses only the user's reviews, and Contrastive Conversational Prompting (CCP), which incorporates reviews from other users or LLM-generated reviews as negative examples to further refine the style. The authors conduct experiments on eight real-world datasets, evaluating the generated reviews using both automatic metrics (ROUGE-L, BERTScore) and application-oriented downstream tasks (user identification, sentiment analysis) across various LLMs. Results indicate that CP, particularly CCP with high-quality negative examples, outperforms a baseline (single-turn prompting) by producing reviews closer to the target user's style.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in its practical application of conversational prompting to the specific problem of few-shot personalized review generation. While conversational prompting itself is not entirely new, its application in this context and the introduction of CCP with contrastive examples is a valuable contribution.  The core idea of structuring the input as a conversation to guide LLMs towards a specific writing style is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the method's ability to generate more user-specific reviews with limited data and without requiring fine-tuning. This addresses a common challenge in real-world scenarios where user data is sparse. The improvement in downstream tasks (user identification, sentiment analysis) demonstrates the practical utility of the generated reviews. Moreover, the method does not require additional model training, reducing associated costs and complexities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Applicability:** CP offers a practical solution to personalized review generation in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study uses a combination of automatic metrics and application-oriented tasks, providing a well-rounded assessment.
    *   **Thorough Experiments:** Experiments span eight diverse product domains and multiple LLMs, demonstrating robustness.
    *   **Clear Results:**  Results consistently show the benefits of CP over the baseline and highlight the effectiveness of contrastive examples.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Cost:** CCP, particularly with LLM-generated negative examples, may incur significant inference costs.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:**  Like all LLM-based methods, CP's performance is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLM, which can introduce bias or inconsistencies.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:**  The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why conversational prompting is effective. While the experiments provide strong evidence, a theoretical grounding would strengthen the contribution.
    *  **Minor Improvement in User Identification Tasks:**  While the results show an improvement in User Identification tasks, the Hit@5 metric appears to be relatively low across all models, suggesting the user reviews generated by conversational models could further improve the ability to be identified.

*   **Potential Influence:** CP has the potential to impact personalized review generation by providing an effective method for businesses to understand user preferences, even with limited data. It may inspire further research into using conversational prompting techniques for other personalized generation tasks.
*  **Negative Examples:**  The authors demonstrated that generating negative examples had a similar effect on performance as using human-written negative examples, it should be noted that these examples were generated using the target user's review history. In some contexts, such as personalized agent applications or role-play, it may be advantageous to generate negative examples by prompting in alternative identities.
* **Over-Thinking Interventions:**  The results demonstrate that larger and more sophisticated reasoning LLMs, such as the 04 model, can actually perform worse than other smaller less sophisticated LLMs. Although the paper does not attempt to address this, other works, such as Sui et al. 2025, propose interventions to address this type of overthinking in LLMs, and these interventions could also be explored within this paper.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. While the core concept of conversational prompting isn't revolutionary, its application to personalized review generation in low-resource settings and the introduction of contrastive prompting with negative examples represents a meaningful contribution. The extensive experimental evaluation supports the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, the lack of a deeper theoretical analysis, dependency on LLMs, cost concerns, and limitations of the overall impact, limit the rating.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enrich-on-Graph: Query-Graph Alignment for Complex Reasoning with LLM Enriching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20810v1)
- **Authors**: Songze Li, Zhiqiang Liu, Zhengke Gui, Huajun Chen, Wen Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit strong reasoning capabilities in complex tasks. However, they still struggle with hallucinations and factual errors in knowledge-intensive scenarios like knowledge graph question answering (KGQA). We attribute this to the semantic gap between structured knowledge graphs (KGs) and unstructured queries, caused by inherent differences in their focuses and structures. Existing methods usually employ resource-intensive, non-scalable workflows reasoning on vanilla KGs, but overlook this gap. To address this challenge, we propose a flexible framework, Enrich-on-Graph (EoG), which leverages LLMs' prior knowledge to enrich KGs, bridge the semantic gap between graphs and queries. EoG enables efficient evidence extraction from KGs for precise and robust reasoning, while ensuring low computational costs, scalability, and adaptability across different methods. Furthermore, we propose three graph quality evaluation metrics to analyze query-graph alignment in KGQA task, supported by theoretical validation of our optimization objectives. Extensive experiments on two KGQA benchmark datasets indicate that EoG can effectively generate high-quality KGs and achieve the state-of-the-art performance. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/zjukg/Enrich-on-Graph.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper "Enrich-on-Graph: Query-Graph Alignment for Complex Reasoning with LLM Enriching":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of semantic gaps between structured knowledge graphs (KGs) and unstructured queries in knowledge graph question answering (KGQA). It argues that Large Language Models (LLMs), despite their strong reasoning capabilities, struggle with hallucinations and factual errors in KGQA due to these semantic differences.  The authors propose a flexible framework called Enrich-on-Graph (EoG) that leverages LLMs to enrich KGs, bridging the gap by aligning the semantics of graphs and queries. EoG consists of three stages: parsing, pruning, and enriching. It also introduces three graph quality metrics (Relevance, Semantic Richness, and Redundancy) to evaluate the effectiveness of the graph alignment.  Experimental results on two KGQA benchmark datasets (CWQ and WebQSP) demonstrate that EoG achieves state-of-the-art performance. The code and data are publicly available.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel framework (EoG) that intelligently leverages LLMs to enrich and align KGs with user queries. The three-stage approach, combining parsing, pruning, and enriching, represents a systematic method for mitigating the semantic gap. While individual components like LLM-based reasoning or graph pruning are not entirely new, their integration within the EoG framework presents a fresh approach. The introduction of the three graph quality metrics provides a quantitative way to evaluate and guide the alignment process, which is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed by the paper is highly relevant. KGQA is an important task, and the challenge of semantic gaps between KGs and queries is a well-known bottleneck. By improving performance and reducing hallucinations/errors in KGQA, the paper contributes to making LLMs more reliable in knowledge-intensive applications. The empirical results showing state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks underscore the practical significance of the work. The computational cost and scalability aspects are also important, as resource-intensive methods often struggle to be widely adopted. Furthermore, the plug-and-play aspect that is demostrated is an interesting capability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the semantic gap problem and its impact on KGQA performance.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The EoG framework is well-defined and logically structured, with clear explanations of each stage (parsing, pruning, enriching).
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The introduction and theoretical validation of graph quality metrics (Relevance, Semantic Richness, Redundancy) add rigor to the evaluation and provide insights into the effectiveness of the method.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments on two benchmark datasets (CWQ, WebQSP) demonstrate the effectiveness of EoG compared to existing methods. The ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analyses provide further insights.
    *   **Code and Data Availability:** Open-sourcing the code and data promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.
    *   **Error analysis**: Adding an error analysis during the enrichment process adds rigor to the research
    *   **Clear writing and structure**: The paper is generally well-written and well-structured, making it relatively easy to follow the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Specific LLMs:** The performance of EoG is dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs used for parsing and enriching. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of how different LLM architectures or sizes impact the framework's effectiveness, although it does include comparisons of the results by varying the backbone LLM. The limitation on the use of domain-specific ontology (as mentioned in the limitations section) also limits its adaptability to the real world.
    *   **Limited Scope of KG Enrichment:** While EoG enriches KGs based on structural and feature attributes, it primarily focuses on local graph transformations. Exploring more sophisticated graph enrichment techniques (e.g., incorporating external knowledge sources or performing global graph re-structuring) could potentially further improve performance.
    *   **Dependency on Chain of Thought**: This makes it difficult to separate the benefits of EoG versus the benefits of the CoT methodology.
    *   **The computation cost**: Although the cost is compared with prior methods, it still makes 4 calls per query, which can still be a high cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The EoG framework has the potential to influence future research in KGQA and related areas. The idea of using LLMs to actively enrich and align KGs with queries is a promising direction. The graph quality metrics could serve as a useful tool for evaluating and comparing different KG alignment methods. The modular design of EoG makes it adaptable and extensible, allowing researchers to integrate it with other techniques (e.g., advanced reasoning methods or graph neural networks).
* The analysis provides insight into future directions for this topic in KGQA.

*Score:* **7.5**

*Rationale:* The paper presents a novel and well-executed framework (EoG) for addressing the semantic gap in KGQA. The experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to existing methods. However, the reliance on specific LLMs and the limited scope of KG enrichment are areas for improvement. The contribution is significant, but not truly transformative or groundbreaking.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging What's Overfixed: Post-Correction via LLM Grammatical Error Overcorrection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20811v1)
- **Authors**: Taehee Park, Heejin Do, Gary Geunbae Lee
- **Abstract**: Robust supervised fine-tuned small Language Models (sLMs) often show high reliability but tend to undercorrect. They achieve high precision at the cost of low recall. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) often show the opposite tendency, making excessive overcorrection, leading to low precision. To effectively harness the strengths of LLMs to address the recall challenges in sLMs, we propose Post-Correction via Overcorrection (PoCO), a novel approach that strategically balances recall and precision. PoCO first intentionally triggers overcorrection via LLM to maximize recall by allowing comprehensive revisions, then applies a targeted post-correction step via fine-tuning smaller models to identify and refine erroneous outputs. We aim to harmonize both aspects by leveraging the generative power of LLMs while preserving the reliability of smaller supervised models. Our extensive experiments demonstrate that PoCO effectively balances GEC performance by increasing recall with competitive precision, ultimately improving the overall quality of grammatical error correction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Leveraging What's Overfixed: Post-Correction via LLM Grammatical Error Overcorrection" by Park, Do, and Lee:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the complementary weaknesses of small, supervised fine-tuned language models (sLMs) and large language models (LLMs) in grammatical error correction (GEC). sLMs often undercorrect (high precision, low recall), while LLMs tend to overcorrect (low precision, high recall). To leverage the strengths of both, the authors propose Post-Correction via Overcorrection (PoCO). PoCO first intentionally triggers overcorrection using an LLM to maximize recall, then applies a post-correction step using a fine-tuned sLM to identify and repair erroneous outputs. The authors demonstrate through experiments that PoCO balances GEC performance by increasing recall with competitive precision compared to sLMs, and improves precision compared to LLMs. They also show its effectiveness when integrated into quality estimation systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of intentionally inducing overcorrection and then refining it is novel. Most existing works focus on *preventing* overcorrection. The training strategy, specifically using a "recovered target," is also a creative way to improve precision in the post-correction stage. The concept of leveraging two strengths: overcorrection and repairment is interesting, combining the recall from LLMs and the robustness and preciseness from SLMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper directly addresses a known issue in GEC: the trade-off between precision and recall in different types of models. The proposed method seems effective in addressing this issue, achieving state-of-the-art results in recall. Also, the findings indicate that using quality estimation can improve efficiency. If the findings are validated further (see next point), this work provides a practical approach to improve GEC systems, particularly useful in educational contexts where identifying all errors is essential.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology with a solid theoretical basis.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and baseline models.
    *   Thorough analysis of the results, including error type analysis.
    *   The paper includes analysis showing how LLMs for post correction did not perform as expected and why the developed method proves effective.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The initial overcorrection step relies on the performance of the specific LLM used (GPT-3.5-Turbo-0125). As the authors acknowledge, older versions of the model were no longer accessible, and experiments were adapted to a different version. This raises questions about the generalizability of the approach. How would PoCO perform with different LLMs? Some LLMs are simply far better than others, potentially influencing results greatly.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Error Types in LLM:** It would be beneficial to include a breakdown of the types of errors the LLM generates in the overcorrection step. This could give insight into the types of errors the post-correction model needs to be good at correcting.
    *  The prompting method that the paper employs may be a point of concern, since more adaptive and advanced approaches exist.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the method proves robust across different LLMs and task domains (beyond just GEC benchmark datasets), it could influence the development of more balanced and reliable GEC systems. The idea of intentionally inducing "noise" and then cleaning it could be applicable to other NLP tasks as well.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-executed and promising approach to addressing a significant challenge in GEC. While there are valid concerns about the generalizability to other LLMs and domains, the core concept and experimental results demonstrate its potential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Distilling Many-Shot In-Context Learning into a Cheat Sheet
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20820v1)
- **Authors**: Ukyo Honda, Soichiro Murakami, Peinan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) enable effective in-context learning (ICL) with many-shot examples, but at the cost of high computational demand due to longer input tokens. To address this, we propose cheat-sheet ICL, which distills the information from many-shot ICL into a concise textual summary (cheat sheet) used as the context at inference time. Experiments on challenging reasoning tasks show that cheat-sheet ICL achieves comparable or better performance than many-shot ICL with far fewer tokens, and matches retrieval-based ICL without requiring test-time retrieval. These findings demonstrate that cheat-sheet ICL is a practical alternative for leveraging LLMs in downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Distilling Many-Shot In-Context Learning into a Cheat Sheet":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "cheat-sheet ICL," a method to reduce the computational cost of many-shot in-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs). The key idea is to distill the knowledge from many demonstrations into a concise textual summary (the "cheat sheet") that is then used as context during inference. This approach aims to achieve performance comparable to many-shot ICL with significantly fewer input tokens, and comparable to retrieval-based ICL without the need for test-time retrieval operations. Experiments on challenging reasoning tasks demonstrate that cheat-sheet ICL achieves competitive or better performance compared to many-shot ICL, while using drastically fewer tokens. The authors also show the cheat sheets can be transferred between models, and manually edited to improve performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of summarizing many-shot examples into a more concise representation for ICL is moderately novel. While previous works have explored demonstration selection or compression, explicitly distilling knowledge into a textual "cheat sheet" format for direct use in ICL is a fresh perspective. The approach directly tackles the computational bottleneck of large input contexts in many-shot ICL, which is becoming increasingly important.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical implications of reducing the computational cost of many-shot ICL. Many-shot ICL is known to often outperform fine-tuning, but its high cost limits wider application. Cheat-sheet ICL has the potential to democratize use and allow LLMs to be more effectively leveraged without requiring training, fine-tuning, or complex retrieval mechanisms. Also, the interpretability and editability of the "cheat sheets" present a compelling advantage, allowing for human-guided refinement of model behavior, making the results of the model more clear and explainable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents thorough experiments across various reasoning tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of cheat-sheet ICL in comparison to strong baselines.
    *   **Practicality:** The method is relatively simple to implement and doesn't require architectural modifications to the LLM. The method requires no test-time retrieval.
    *   **Interpretability:** The cheat sheets are human-readable, allowing for analysis of model reasoning and targeted intervention for improvement.
    *   **Transferability:** Showing the cheat-sheets can be transferred to other models increases the usefulness of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLM capabilities:** The method relies heavily on the ability of LLMs to effectively summarize and extract relevant knowledge from demonstrations. A weaker LLM may not be able to generate a useful cheat sheet.
    *   **Task limitations:** The paper focuses primarily on reasoning tasks, and it is still unclear how well cheat-sheet ICL will perform on other types of tasks, such as creative writing or dialogue generation, or for cases where pattern recognition is less important.
    *   **Prompt engineering:** The generation of good cheat sheets requires good prompt engineering, which is not always straightforward. Although the study presents results showing the approach is robust to varying prompts, more complex tasks may be more prompt sensitive.
    *   **In the study's experiments, GPT-4.1 was used, thus the results are not reproducible.**

*   **Potential Influence:** If further research confirms the broad applicability of cheat-sheet ICL, it could become a standard technique for leveraging LLMs in various downstream tasks, especially when computational resources are limited or on-device inference is desired. The interpretability of the method could also spur further research into understanding and improving LLM reasoning.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid, practical contribution to the field of in-context learning. The idea is relatively novel and addresses a significant challenge, but the reliance on LLM capabilities and limited task scope somewhat temper the overall impact. The strong empirical results and interpretability are key strengths that warrant a good score. However, the dependence on the specific models used and the narrow task-focus suggests that the paper's applicability remains somewhat uncertain. Further investigations are required to determine the full scope and utility of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### T2I-Diff: fMRI Signal Generation via Time-Frequency Image Transform and Classifier-Free Denoising Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20822v1)
- **Authors**: Hwa Hui Tew, Junn Yong Loo, Yee-Fan Tan, Xinyu Tang, Hernando Ombao, Fuad Noman, Raphael C. -W. Phan, Chee-Ming Ting
- **Abstract**: Functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging (fMRI) is an advanced neuroimaging method that enables in-depth analysis of brain activity by measuring dynamic changes in the blood oxygenation level-dependent (BOLD) signals. However, the resource-intensive nature of fMRI data acquisition limits the availability of high-fidelity samples required for data-driven brain analysis models. While modern generative models can synthesize fMRI data, they often underperform because they overlook the complex non-stationarity and nonlinear BOLD dynamics. To address these challenges, we introduce T2I-Diff, an fMRI generation framework that leverages time-frequency representation of BOLD signals and classifier-free denoising diffusion. Specifically, our framework first converts BOLD signals into windowed spectrograms via a time-dependent Fourier transform, capturing both the underlying temporal dynamics and spectral evolution. Subsequently, a classifier-free diffusion model is trained to generate class-conditioned frequency spectrograms, which are then reverted to BOLD signals via inverse Fourier transforms. Finally, we validate the efficacy of our approach by demonstrating improved accuracy and generalization in downstream fMRI-based brain network classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "T2I-Diff: fMRI Signal Generation via Time-Frequency Image Transform and Classifier-Free Denoising Diffusion Models" introduces a novel framework (T2I-Diff) for generating synthetic fMRI data.  The core idea involves transforming fMRI BOLD signals into time-frequency spectrogram images using a windowed Fourier transform. This image representation is then fed into a classifier-free denoising diffusion model, which learns to generate spectrograms conditioned on class labels (e.g., healthy control vs. major depressive disorder). The generated spectrograms are then inverted back to fMRI signals.  The paper demonstrates that this approach improves the accuracy and generalization of downstream fMRI-based brain network classification tasks. The authors highlight the advantages of their approach in capturing complex spatiotemporal dynamics of fMRI signals and simplifying model optimization by removing the need for an explicit classifier to guide the generation process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper demonstrates some novelty by combining a time-frequency image representation with classifier-free diffusion models for fMRI signal generation.  While both time-frequency transforms and diffusion models are individually established techniques, their specific combination for generating fMRI data, especially with the classifier-free approach, appears relatively novel. The strategy of converting fMRI data into a time-frequency representation allows the model to capture both the temporal evolution and the spectral composition of the signal, addressing a key limitation of many existing generative models. The classifier-free diffusion aspect is also a step forward, as it simplifies the generative process and potentially improves stability.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in its potential to address the data scarcity problem in fMRI research. High-quality fMRI data is often difficult and expensive to acquire.  Generating realistic synthetic fMRI data can significantly benefit the field by:

*   **Data Augmentation:** Improving the performance of downstream machine learning models by augmenting training datasets.
*   **Privacy Preservation:** Sharing synthetic data without compromising patient privacy.
*   **Exploring "What-if" Scenarios:** Simulating the effects of different interventions or disease states on brain activity.

The paper's findings of improved classification accuracy and generalization in brain network classification using synthetic data suggest that the proposed framework is promising in achieving these goals. The ablation studies examining the impact of sequence length provide useful insights into the model's ability to capture both fine-grained details and global contextual information. The functional connectivity visualization offers further evidence that the generated signals retain meaningful biological properties.

**Weaknesses and Limitations:**

*   **Limited comparison of generative models:** The current evaluation, while containing a reasonable number of baselines, could benefit from a more extensive comparison with other fMRI generation models directly, if available.  The inclusion of task-based generative models, if applicable, could also bolster the results.
*   **Limited evaluation metrics:** While the classification metrics are well done, further evaluations on measures of signal complexity and realisticness in terms of the frequency power, as well as evaluation metrics more common in time series generation would have added value.
*   **Dataset specificity:** The evaluation is primarily based on one fMRI dataset (REST-meta-MDD). The generalizability of the T2I-Diff framework to other fMRI datasets and imaging modalities (e.g., EEG, MEG) needs further investigation.
*   **Realism of synthetic signals:**  While the connectivity visualizations are encouraging, it's crucial to remember that synthetic data can only approximate reality. More rigorous validation is needed to ensure that the generated signals capture the full complexity of real fMRI data and that insights derived from synthetic data are transferable to real-world scenarios.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a promising approach to fMRI signal generation that addresses limitations of existing techniques by leveraging time-frequency image transforms and classifier-free diffusion models. The results demonstrate improved performance in downstream classification tasks, suggesting that the generated signals retain relevant biological information. However, the paper could benefit from more comprehensive evaluations and a more in-depth analysis of the limitations and assumptions of the proposed framework.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's novelty and potential significance, balanced by the need for further validation and a more comprehensive evaluation. The paper introduces a novel combination of techniques with promising initial results, justifying a score above the median. However, the limitations regarding dataset specificity, limited evaluation metrics, and the inherent challenges in generating truly realistic synthetic data prevent a higher score. Further research addressing these limitations could significantly increase the paper's impact and warrant a higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Verification Limits Code LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20837v1)
- **Authors**: Srishti Gureja, Elena Tommasone, Jingyi He, Sara Hooker, Matthias Gallé, Marzieh Fadaee
- **Abstract**: Large language models for code generation increasingly rely on synthetic data, where both problem solutions and verification tests are generated by models. While this enables scalable data creation, it introduces a previously unexplored bottleneck: the verification ceiling, in which the quality and diversity of training data are fundamentally constrained by the capabilities of synthetic verifiers. In this work, we systematically study how verification design and strategies influence model performance. We investigate (i) what we verify by analyzing the impact of test complexity and quantity: richer test suites improve code generation capabilities (on average +3 pass@1), while quantity alone yields diminishing returns, (ii) how we verify by exploring relaxed pass thresholds: rigid 100% pass criteria can be overly restrictive. By allowing for relaxed thresholds or incorporating LLM-based soft verification, we can recover valuable training data, leading to a 2-4 point improvement in pass@1 performance. However, this benefit is contingent upon the strength and diversity of the test cases used, and (iii) why verification remains necessary through controlled comparisons of formally correct versus incorrect solutions and human evaluation: retaining diverse correct solutions per problem yields consistent generalization gains. Our results show that Verification as currently practiced is too rigid, filtering out valuable diversity. But it cannot be discarded, only recalibrated. By combining calibrated verification with diverse, challenging problem-solution pairs, we outline a path to break the verification ceiling and unlock stronger code generation models.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the "verification ceiling" problem in synthetic code generation, where the quality and diversity of training data are limited by the capabilities of the synthetic verification system. The authors explore how different verification strategies impact model performance. Their experiments focus on (1) the complexity and quantity of unit tests used for verification, (2) alternative verification approaches such as relaxed pass thresholds and LLM-based judgements, and (3) the fundamental role of verification by comparing models trained on formally correct vs. incorrect solutions. They find that overly strict verification can discard valuable solutions, while lack of verification leads to low-quality data. They advocate for a calibrated approach to verification balancing correctness, diversity, and challenge to overcome the verification ceiling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and increasingly relevant problem in the field of code generation using large language models. The reliance on synthetic data for training presents a unique set of challenges, particularly the risk of being limited by the quality of the synthetic verification process. The authors present a systematic study that provides valuable insights into the design and implementation of effective synthetic data pipelines.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the "verification ceiling" problem, which is often overlooked but is critical to the success of synthetic data generation.
*   **Systematic Investigation:** The paper undertakes a systematic investigation of various factors affecting the verification process, including test complexity, quantity, pass thresholds, and the use of LLMs as judges. This thoroughness is a strong point.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors conduct a wide range of experiments across multiple benchmarks and programming languages, providing strong evidence for their claims.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights for building more effective synthetic data pipelines, offering practical guidance for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Human evaluation:** The inclusion of human evaluation brings a different perspective, addressing the question of how trustworthy is our current solution by comparing against code experts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited exploration of RL or reward-based strategies:** The paper focuses primarily on supervised fine-tuning using filtered data. While it acknowledges the potential of reinforcement learning (RL) and reward-based optimization strategies, it doesn't explore these approaches in detail. Exploring these alternatives might provide even more compelling results.
*   **Reliance on Pass@1 for evaluation**: The reliance primarily on functional correctness metrics such as pass@1, the authors acknowledge that these metrics do not capture other important aspects of code quality, including readability, maintainability, and adherence to best practices. Extending evaluation to these broader dimensions could make more compelling results

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by identifying and characterizing the verification ceiling problem. While previous works have explored synthetic data generation for code, this paper specifically focuses on the limitations imposed by the verification process itself. The systematic investigation and actionable insights provided in the paper are valuable and can guide future research and development in the area of code generation.

The use of relaxed thresholds, diverse training test sets, and a focus on problem difficulty demonstrates a significant advance over more basic strategies for dataset creation.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the design of future synthetic data pipelines for code generation. The insights gained from this research can help researchers and practitioners to develop more effective verification strategies that balance correctness, diversity, and challenge.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper addresses a significant problem in synthetic data generation for code. It performs a thorough investigation and offers actionable insights. It is well-written and supported by strong evidence. The limitations of its scope (e.g., focusing primarily on supervised learning and functional correctness metrics) and reliance on pass@1, prevent it from receiving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Zero-Shot Privacy-Aware Text Rewriting via Iterative Tree Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20838v1)
- **Authors**: Shuo Huang, Xingliang Yuan, Gholamreza Haffari, Lizhen Qu
- **Abstract**: The increasing adoption of large language models (LLMs) in cloud-based services has raised significant privacy concerns, as user inputs may inadvertently expose sensitive information. Existing text anonymization and de-identification techniques, such as rule-based redaction and scrubbing, often struggle to balance privacy preservation with text naturalness and utility. In this work, we propose a zero-shot, tree-search-based iterative sentence rewriting algorithm that systematically obfuscates or deletes private information while preserving coherence, relevance, and naturalness. Our method incrementally rewrites privacy-sensitive segments through a structured search guided by a reward model, enabling dynamic exploration of the rewriting space. Experiments on privacy-sensitive datasets show that our approach significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving a superior balance between privacy protection and utility preservation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NaPaRe, a zero-shot, tree-search-based algorithm for privacy-aware text rewriting. The goal is to redact or obscure sensitive information in text while preserving coherence, relevance, and naturalness. NaPaRe iteratively rewrites potentially sensitive segments using a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) approach, guided by a reward model that balances privacy preservation and text utility.  The paper demonstrates, through experiments on the NAP2 and ECHR datasets, that NaPaRe outperforms existing redaction tools and LLM-based paraphrasing methods in terms of privacy protection and utility preservation, maintaining reasonable fluency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of several techniques in a specific architecture designed for zero-shot privacy-aware text rewriting. Specifically, it's the fusion of:
    *   An iterative, tree-search approach (MCTS) for exploring the rewriting space. While MCTS has been used in NLP before, its application to this specific privacy problem is somewhat novel.
    *   Dynamic identification of sensitive segments based on a privacy specification (persona or PII lists). This aims to address the limitations of static PII detection, offering more flexibility.
    *   A reward model combining privacy entailment scores (NLI) and domain-specific utility measures to guide the search.
    *   The zero-shot aspect is valuable, as it avoids the need for fine-tuning on potentially scarce or costly privacy-sanitized datasets.

*   **Significance:** The increasing use of LLMs raises significant privacy risks, making research in privacy-preserving techniques very relevant. The paper addresses a critical need for methods that allow users to leverage the benefits of LLMs while mitigating the risk of exposing sensitive information. The significance is amplified by the focus on a zero-shot approach, enabling deployment in resource-constrained environments without extensive fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The results show promising performance in achieving a better balance between privacy protection, utility, and naturalness compared to baseline methods. The improvements are significant, especially in privacy preservation.
    *   The modular design, incorporating privacy segment alignment, tree-search rewriting, and the composite reward, appears to contribute to the algorithm's effectiveness.
    *   The zero-shot capability is a significant advantage, enabling deployment in situations where fine-tuning is not feasible.
    *   The paper includes a reasonably comprehensive evaluation across different datasets and metrics.
    *   The security analysis section is a valuable addition, addressing potential reconstruction attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on potentially imperfect automatic privacy evaluation metrics (PRIVACY_NLI) and dependency on components like FLAIR-SCRUBBING (for ECHR dataset segment extraction) could affect the reliability of the evaluation. The paper acknowledges this, but it remains a limitation.
    *   The computational cost, particularly for the ECHR dataset, is significant, limiting practical application.
    *   The method, despite the zero-shot claim, depends on the quality of the underlying LLM. This isn't a flaw per se, but it's important to acknowledge that the performance will be limited by the capabilities of the LLM.
    *   While a security analysis is provided, a more comprehensive evaluation against diverse attack strategies would strengthen the paper.

*   **Impact:** The paper presents a promising approach that could influence future research in privacy-preserving NLP. The zero-shot nature of the method and the good balance between privacy, utility, and naturalness are very appealing. There is good potential for future work in refining the segment alignment and reward function, as well as incorporating the approach with on-device LLMs.

*   **Rigorous assessment:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of privacy-preserving NLP. While relying on current metrics to provide a balanced evaluation of privacy and other measures, there exists a need for a robust privacy evaluation metric for this niche.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed algorithm for a relevant and timely problem. The results are promising, and the zero-shot capability is a significant advantage. However, limitations related to evaluation metrics, computational cost, and LLM dependency prevent a higher score. Also, future work in evaluating the algorithm on more diverse privacy settings would increase the applicability. Overall, the paper is a valuable contribution that warrants further investigation and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MTRDrive: Memory-Tool Synergistic Reasoning for Robust Autonomous Driving in Corner Cases
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20843v1)
- **Authors**: Ziang Luo, Kangan Qian, Jiahua Wang, Yuechen Luo, Jinyu Miao, Zheng Fu, Yunlong Wang, Sicong Jiang, Zilin Huang, Yifei Hu, Yuhao Yang, Hao Ye, Mengmeng Yang, Xiaojian Dong, Kun Jiang, Diange Yang
- **Abstract**: Vision-Language Models(VLMs) have demonstrated significant potential for end-to-end autonomous driving, yet a substantial gap remains between their current capabilities and the reliability necessary for real-world deployment. A critical challenge is their fragility, characterized by hallucinations and poor generalization in out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce MTRDrive, a novel framework that integrates procedural driving experiences with a dynamic toolkit to enhance generalization and proactive decision-making. MTRDrive addresses these limitations through a closed-loop system that combines a memory-based experience retrieval mechanism with dynamic toolkits. This synergy enables the model to interact more effectively with its environment, improving both reasoning and decision-making capabilities with the help of our memory-tool synergistic reasoning. Additionally, we introduce a new benchmark based on complex Roadwork construction scenarios to rigorously evaluate zero-shot generalization. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superior effectiveness of our approach. On the public NAVSIM benchmark, our 3B-parameter MTRDrive model achieves an exceptional PDMS of 88.3 without chain-of-thought and sets a state-of-the-art performance bar on high-level planning, with a driving metric score of 79.8\% and a planning accuracy of 82.6\%. Rigorous zero-shot evaluation on the new Roadwork-VLM benchmark shows a strong ability to reason robustly in unseen scenarios, achieving a driving metric score of 80.2\%. These results highlight MTRDrive's potential to advance autonomous driving toward safer and more reliable systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MTRDrive paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MTRDrive, a novel framework for autonomous driving that integrates procedural driving experiences with a dynamic toolkit. MTRDrive aims to improve generalization and proactive decision-making in corner cases (out-of-distribution scenarios) by combining a memory-based experience retrieval mechanism with dynamic toolkits within a closed-loop system. The system effectively retrieves past experiences to inform current actions, and uses real-time tool use to dynamically enhance the reasoning. Additionally, the paper introduces a new benchmark dataset (Roadwork-VLM) based on complex roadwork construction scenarios to specifically evaluate zero-shot generalization capabilities. Extensive experimental results demonstrate the superior effectiveness of MTRDrive compared to baseline methods on both public (NAVSIM) and the newly created Roadwork-VLM benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating memory retrieval of driving experiences with dynamic toolkits is relatively novel, although individual elements (VLMs, Chain-of-Thought, tool use in autonomous driving) have been explored separately. The synergistic approach—combining these elements in a closed-loop, interactive system—is a significant contribution.  The use of a CLIP encoder for semantic retrieval, alongside structured experience representation and the two-stage training approach (SFT and RLFT with GRPO), add to the paper's novelty. The introduction of the Roadwork-VLM benchmark is also a positive aspect, specifically targeting a challenging OOD scenario.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical weakness of existing VLM-based autonomous driving systems: their fragility and poor generalization in OOD situations. By mitigating hallucinations and improving robustness in complex scenarios, MTRDrive has the potential to significantly advance the field. Demonstrating improvements in planning accuracy, risk assessment, and scene awareness, particularly on the Roadwork-VLM dataset, underscores the practical significance. The potential real-world impact of improving safety and reliability in autonomous vehicles is undeniable.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a key challenge (OOD generalization) in VLM-based autonomous driving.
    * **Novel Approach:**  The MTRDrive framework presents a creative and synergistic solution.
    * **Rigorous Evaluation:**  Experiments are conducted on both public datasets (NAVSIM) and a new, dedicated benchmark (Roadwork-VLM), providing a comprehensive assessment.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are well-designed and provide insight into the contribution of each component.
    * **Qualitative Examples:** The qualitative comparisons in Figure 4 vividly illustrate the advantages of MTRDrive.
    * **Roadwork-VLM Dataset:** The introduction and open-sourcing of Roadwork-VLM fosters further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on CLIP:** While CLIP provides efficient semantic retrieval, it may limit the system's ability to capture subtle contextual nuances that a more powerful, but computationally expensive, VLM-based embedding could offer.
    * **Tool Selection:** The experience-driven approach to tool selection is promising, but the details of the specific tools used and their implementation could be further elaborated.
    * **Limited Complexity of OOD Scenarios:** While Roadwork-VLM adds complexity, further expanding benchmark suite to include more diverse and edge cases would further demonstrate MTRDrive's robustness.
    * **Compute Intensive Training:** Training MTRDrive and other related methods still relies on significant compute resources, limiting accessibility to researchers with constrained resources.

* **Potential Influence:** MTRDrive has the potential to influence the design of future VLM-based autonomous driving systems by emphasizing the importance of experience-driven reasoning and dynamic tool use. The Roadwork-VLM benchmark will likely become a valuable resource for evaluating generalization capabilities.

* **Overall:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and presents a significant advance in the field. The combination of a novel framework, rigorous evaluation, and valuable benchmark dataset makes it a substantial contribution.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**  While not a complete paradigm shift, MTRDrive offers a compelling and practical solution to a major limitation of VLM-based autonomous driving.  The strengths in addressing a clearly defined problem, novel approach, rigorous experiments, and a valuable data set make it a very strong contribution. The weaknesses, while noted, do not detract significantly from the overall impact. A score of 8 reflects the importance and value of the work while acknowledging room for improvements.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### Causal Time Series Generation via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20846v1)
- **Authors**: Yutong Xia, Chang Xu, Yuxuan Liang, Qingsong Wen, Roger Zimmermann, Jiang Bian
- **Abstract**: Time series generation (TSG) synthesizes realistic sequences and has achieved remarkable success. Among TSG, conditional models generate sequences given observed covariates, however, such models learn observational correlations without considering unobserved confounding. In this work, we propose a causal perspective on conditional TSG and introduce causal time series generation as a new TSG task family, formalized within Pearl's causal ladder, extending beyond observational generation to include interventional and counterfactual settings. To instantiate these tasks, we develop CaTSG, a unified diffusion-based framework with backdoor-adjusted guidance that causally steers sampling toward desired interventions and individual counterfactuals while preserving observational fidelity. Specifically, our method derives causal score functions via backdoor adjustment and the abduction-action-prediction procedure, thus enabling principled support for all three levels of TSG. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets show that CaTSG achieves superior fidelity and also supporting interventional and counterfactual generation that existing baselines cannot handle. Overall, we propose the causal TSG family and instantiate it with CaTSG, providing an initial proof-of-concept and opening a promising direction toward more reliable simulation under interventions and counterfactual generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task family called "causal time series generation" (TSG), extending beyond observational generation to interventional and counterfactual settings based on Pearl's causal ladder. The authors propose a unified diffusion-based framework, "CaTSG," that employs backdoor-adjusted guidance and a learnable latent environment bank to support observational, interventional, and counterfactual generation. They derive causal score functions via backdoor adjustment and abduction-action-prediction. Empirical results on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that CaTSG achieves better fidelity and supports interventional and counterfactual generation, which existing baselines cannot handle.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in framing time series generation within a causal inference framework, explicitly addressing the limitations of purely correlation-based conditional TSG models. The introduction of interventional (Int-TSG) and counterfactual (CF-TSG) generation as distinct tasks is a significant conceptual contribution. The use of backdoor adjustment and abduction-action-prediction within a diffusion model for time series generation is also a novel methodological contribution. It is also a first attempt to propose and instantiate causal time series generation.

*   **Significance:** The work's significance is multifaceted. Firstly, it highlights the inherent limitations of standard conditional TSG models in real-world decision-making scenarios where unobserved confounders are present. Secondly, it provides a framework (CaTSG) for generating more reliable and robust time series data, accounting for causal relationships and enabling counterfactual reasoning. This could have practical implications for various applications, including policy simulation, healthcare modeling, and risk assessment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem formulation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and provides a well-defined causal framework for TSG.
    *   **Solid theoretical foundation:** The use of Pearl's causal ladder and established causal inference techniques provides a strong theoretical basis for the proposed method.
    *   **Unified framework:** CaTSG provides a single framework for addressing observational, interventional, and counterfactual generation tasks.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation includes experiments on both synthetic (with ground-truth counterfactuals) and real-world datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of CaTSG.
    *   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is written in a clear and understandable style, with sufficient detail for reproducing the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Predefined SCM:** The reliance on a predefined structural causal model (SCM) is a potential limitation. The performance of CaTSG depends on the accuracy and completeness of the specified SCM. The choice of SCM structure may be subjective, and incorrect assumptions could lead to biased results. More expressive of adaptive causal structure may reduce the robustness.
    *   **Finite Environment Bank:** The discretization of latent environments into a fixed-size bank may not be suitable for all scenarios. The selection of the appropriate size and representation for the environment bank can be challenging.
    *   **Practical complexity:** While theoretically sound, implementing and tuning CaTSG, particularly the environment inference module, could be computationally intensive and require significant expertise.
    *   **Evaluation of counterfactuals in real-world:** Evaluating the quality of counterfactual generations on real-world data remains a challenge due to the lack of ground truth. Visualizations and case studies are helpful, but a more rigorous and quantitative evaluation framework is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's emphasis on causal reasoning and counterfactual generation could shift the focus of the time series generation community towards more reliable and interpretable models. CaTSG provides a valuable initial solution to this direction, potentially inspiring future research on developing more flexible and generalizable causal generative models.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a solid conceptual framework and a technically sound methodology for causal time series generation. The novelty and significance are notable, and the empirical results are convincing. However, the limitations related to the predefined SCM and the challenges in evaluating counterfactuals in real-world data warrant a slightly lower score.
