    The theoretical result providing conditions when whitening improves sample quality is potentially impactful. However, the practicality and tightness of this condition needs further scrutiny.

*   **Significance:** The paper's potential significance stems from its ability to improve the accuracy and robustness of probabilistic time series forecasting. By explicitly accounting for non-stationarity, inter-variable dependencies, and distribution shifts, CW-Gen offers a potentially valuable tool for applications in finance, healthcare, environmental science, and transportation. The empirical results presented seem to support these claims; CW-Gen appears to consistently outperform several state-of-the-art baselines. The method's integration with both diffusion models and flow matching adds to its significance, suggesting broad applicability within the generative modeling landscape. Mitigating distribution shift effects is a crucial concern in many time series applications, giving the CW-Gen approach added value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Solid theoretical justification for conditional whitening.
    *   Novel JMCE module for estimating conditional means and covariances.
    *   Consistent empirical improvements across various datasets and generative model architectures.
    *   Demonstrated ability to mitigate distribution shifts.
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical conditions in Theorem 1, while insightful, may not always be easily verifiable in practice or may not be tight enough.
    *   The computational cost associated with JMCE and, particularly, the Cholesky decomposition of high-dimensional covariance matrices, could be a limitation in certain applications (although the paper mentions mitigation via Flow matching). However this is a common complexity that other models that estimate covariance matrices would also face.
    *   The paper may benefit from an explicit discussion of the limitations of JMCE and CW-Gen, as well as directions for future research. It could further justify the design choices that were made, instead of other potential designs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in probabilistic time series forecasting by establishing conditional whitening as a viable technique for improving generative model performance. It also opens up avenues for exploring other ways to incorporate prior information into generative models. The JMCE module could serve as a valuable building block for future research.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to probabilistic time series forecasting. The theoretical analysis, the JMCE module, and the empirical results all contribute to a significant advance in the field. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale for the Score:** The score of 7.5 reflects the paper's significant contributions to probabilistic time series forecasting. The novel concept of conditional whitening, supported by theoretical analysis and implemented in the JMCE module, constitutes a valuable advance in the field. The consistent empirical improvements and the demonstrated ability to mitigate distribution shifts further bolster the paper's significance. The limitations, particularly related to the practical verification of theoretical conditions and the potential computational cost of covariance estimation, prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### GALAX: Graph-Augmented Language Model for Explainable Reinforcement-Guided Subgraph Reasoning in Precision Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20935v1)
- **Authors**: Heming Zhang, Di Huang, Wenyu Li, Michael Province, Yixin Chen, Philip Payne, Fuhai Li
- **Abstract**: In precision medicine, quantitative multi-omic features, topological context, and textual biological knowledge play vital roles in identifying disease-critical signaling pathways and targets. Existing pipelines capture only part of these-numerical omics ignore topological context, text-centric LLMs lack quantitative grounded reasoning, and graph-only models underuse node semantics and the generalization of LLMs-limiting mechanistic interpretability. Although Process Reward Models (PRMs) aim to guide reasoning in LLMs, they remain limited by unreliable intermediate evaluation, and vulnerability to reward hacking with computational cost. These gaps motivate integrating quantitative multi-omic signals, topological structure with node annotations, and literature-scale text via LLMs, using subgraph reasoning as the principle bridge linking numeric evidence, topological knowledge and language context. Therefore, we propose GALAX (Graph Augmented LAnguage model with eXplainability), an innovative framework that integrates pretrained Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) into Large Language Models (LLMs) via reinforcement guided by a Graph Process Reward Model (GPRM), which generates disease-relevant subgraphs in a step-wise manner initiated by an LLM and iteratively evaluated by a pretrained GNN, enabling process-level supervision without explicit intermediate reasoning annotations. As an application, we also introduced Target-QA, a benchmark combining CRISPR-identified targets, multi-omic profiles, and biomedical graph knowledge across diverse cancer cell lines, which enables GNN pretraining for supervising step-wise graph construction and supports long-context reasoning over text-numeric graphs (TNGs), providing a scalable and biologically grounded framework for explainable, reinforcement-guided subgraph reasoning toward reliable and interpretable target and pathway discovery in precision medicine.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GALAX, a novel framework for precision medicine that integrates graph neural networks (GNNs) into large language models (LLMs). GALAX aims to improve target identification and mechanism discovery by combining quantitative multi-omic data, topological context from biological knowledge graphs, and the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. It uses reinforcement learning guided by a Graph Process Reward Model (GPRM) to generate disease-relevant subgraphs in a stepwise manner. The GPRM utilizes a pre-trained GNN to evaluate intermediate subgraphs, providing process-level supervision without requiring explicit intermediate reasoning annotations. The authors also introduce a new benchmark dataset, Target-QA, combining CRISPR-identified targets, multi-omic profiles, and biomedical graph knowledge to facilitate GNN pretraining and long-context reasoning. The Target-QA dataset will be publicly available at Huggingface and the GALAX code will be on GitHub.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the architecture's holistic integration of different data modalities and modeling techniques for precision medicine. Combining GNNs and LLMs is not entirely new, but the specific implementation with reinforcement learning and a GPRM for step-wise subgraph refinement offers a significant advance. The use of a GNN as a process reward function, replacing the need for manual annotations, is a key innovation.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of GALAX is significant. Existing methods in precision medicine often focus on single data types or lack mechanistic interpretability. GALAX addresses these limitations by creating explainable, patient-specific subnetworks that link numerical evidence to language context and topological knowledge. The Target-QA dataset is also a valuable contribution, as it provides a challenging benchmark for evaluating models in this domain. However, more details should be included on how representative it is of real-world problems in the biomedical space.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integrative Approach:** The ability to combine multi-omic data, knowledge graphs, and LLMs is a major strength.
    *   **Explainability:** The subgraph generation process provides a transparent rationale for target prioritization.
    *   **Reinforcement Learning:** The GPRM allows for process-level supervision without requiring explicit intermediate annotations.
    *   **New Benchmark:** The Target-QA dataset enables standardized evaluation of models for target identification.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The framework is complex, involving multiple pre-trained models and a reinforcement learning component, which could increase computational costs and training difficulties.
    *   **Dependence on GNN pretraining**: The GPRM and final output relies heavily on the initial quality of the pre-trained GNN.
    *   **Limited comparison**: While ablation studies are performed, it is still difficult to compare GALAX to other models. More complex models that take advantage of other LLM architectures should be used in future studies.
    *   **Scope of Target-QA**: The Target-QA dataset is limited to cancerous cell lines which may not generalize to real-world data in other disease.

*   **Potential Influence:** GALAX has the potential to significantly influence target and pathway discovery in precision medicine. The framework's ability to provide explainable, patient-specific insights could guide the development of novel therapeutics and effective treatment strategies. In the future, it can be expanded to other areas in precision medicine outside of oncology to provide even wider reach.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

While GALAX presents a strong foundation for future research, some details on data pre-processing and integration are missing. The complexity and reliance on several pre-trained models also add complexity to the framework.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Noise-Resistant Vision: Key Architectural Secrets for Robust Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20939v1)
- **Authors**: Bum Jun Kim, Makoto Kawano, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
- **Abstract**: While the robustness of vision models is often measured, their dependence on specific architectural design choices is rarely dissected. We investigate why certain vision architectures are inherently more robust to additive Gaussian noise and convert these empirical insights into simple, actionable design rules. Specifically, we performed extensive evaluations on 1,174 pretrained vision models, empirically identifying four consistent design patterns for improved robustness against Gaussian noise: larger stem kernels, smaller input resolutions, average pooling, and supervised vision transformers (ViTs) rather than CLIP ViTs, which yield up to 506 rank improvements and 21.6\%p accuracy gains. We then develop a theoretical analysis that explains these findings, converting observed correlations into causal mechanisms. First, we prove that low-pass stem kernels attenuate noise with a gain that decreases quadratically with kernel size and that anti-aliased downsampling reduces noise energy roughly in proportion to the square of the downsampling factor. Second, we demonstrate that average pooling is unbiased and suppresses noise in proportion to the pooling window area, whereas max pooling incurs a positive bias that grows slowly with window size and yields a relatively higher mean-squared error and greater worst-case sensitivity. Third, we reveal and explain the vulnerability of CLIP ViTs via a pixel-space Lipschitz bound: The smaller normalization standard deviations used in CLIP preprocessing amplify worst-case sensitivity by up to 1.91 times relative to the Inception-style preprocessing common in supervised ViTs. Our results collectively disentangle robustness into interpretable modules, provide a theory that explains the observed trends, and build practical, plug-and-play guidelines for designing vision models more robust against Gaussian noise.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates architectural design choices in vision models and their impact on robustness against additive Gaussian noise. Through extensive empirical evaluation of 1,174 pre-trained vision models, the authors identify four key design patterns that improve robustness: larger stem kernels, smaller input resolutions, average pooling instead of max pooling, and using supervised ViTs rather than CLIP ViTs.  The authors provide theoretical analysis to support these findings. This analysis includes demonstrating how larger stem kernels and anti-aliased downsampling attenuate noise, showing the unbiased nature of average pooling (compared to the bias of max pooling), and revealing that the sensitivity of CLIP ViTs to noise is amplified due to their preprocessing normalization. The paper concludes by offering practical design guidelines for creating more robust vision models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the systematic dissection of architectural choices within vision models to understand their specific contributions to robustness against Gaussian noise. While some individual aspects (e.g., anti-aliasing, pooling analysis) have been studied before, the comprehensive empirical meta-analysis and the effort to connect empirical findings with clear theoretical explanations distinguishes this work. The discovery of the specific role of mean-std normalization in CLIP ViTs' vulnerability is also notable. The systematic identification of design rules is also a key novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Actionable Design Rules:** The paper translates empirical observations into practical, plug-and-play design guidelines that engineers can use to build more robust vision models.
    *   **Interpretable Modules:** The paper disentangles robustness into interpretable modules, providing a clearer understanding of which architectural choices contribute to which aspects of robustness.
    *   **Theoretical grounding:** Provides a starting point for other theoretical analyses of noise robustness.
    *   **Practical Implications:** Robust vision models are critical for safety-critical applications (e.g., autonomous driving, medical imaging).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-Scale Empirical Study:** The evaluation across a vast number of pretrained models is a major strength, lending credibility to the empirical findings.
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides theoretical justifications for the observed patterns, converting correlations into causal mechanisms. The analysis is generally sound.
    *   **Clear Design Guidelines:** The translation of findings into clear design guidelines enhances the paper's practical value.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Focus on Gaussian Noise:** The exclusive focus on Gaussian noise, while justified by its theoretical properties and prevalence, limits the generalizability of the results to other types of noise or corruptions.
    *   **Reliance on Pretrained Models:** The empirical study relies on pretrained models, potentially inheriting biases from their training data and procedures. Though the controlled ResNet experiments address this concern in part, it's not a complete solution.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** More extensive ablation studies on specific architectural choices could further strengthen the causal claims. Swapping pretrained weights of each block partially works but isn't complete.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the design of future vision models, especially in applications where robustness is paramount. It also provides a valuable framework for analyzing the robustness of other deep learning architectures.

* **Other Considerations**: It would have been great if the authors showed other failure cases where their analysis fails.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by systematically investigating the architectural factors that influence the robustness of vision models against Gaussian noise. The large-scale empirical study, rigorous theoretical analysis, and actionable design guidelines are all significant strengths. The main limitations are primarily due to the restricted focus on a specific noise type and the reliance on pre-trained models. Though the controlled ResNet study addresses these in part, the paper isn't perfect. Despite these weaknesses, the paper provides significant insights and lays a foundation for future work in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Why Attention Fails: The Degeneration of Transformers into MLPs in Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20942v1)
- **Authors**: Zida Liang, Jiayi Zhu, Weiqiang Sun
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based architectures achieved high performance in natural language processing and computer vision, yet many studies have shown that they have not demonstrated a clear advantage in time series forecasting and even underperform simple linear baselines in some cases. However, most of these studies have not thoroughly explored the reasons behind the failure of transformers. To better understand time-series transformers(TST), we designed a series of experiments, progressively modifying transformers into MLPs to investigate the impact of the attention mechanism. Surprisingly, transformer blocks often degenerate into simple MLPs in existing time-series transformers. We designed a interpretable dataset to investigate the reasons behind the failure of the attention mechanism and revealed that the attention mechanism is not working in the expected way. We theoretically analyzed the reasons behind this phenomenon, demonstrating that the current embedding methods fail to allow transformers to function in a well-structured latent space, and further analyzed the deeper underlying causes of the failure of embedding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Why Attention Fails: The Degeneration of Transformers into MLPs in Time Series Forecasting" investigates the underperformance of Transformer-based architectures compared to simpler models like MLPs in time series forecasting.  Through a series of experiments, including ablation studies, perturbation analysis, and manipulation of patch lengths, the authors demonstrate that the attention mechanism in time series transformers often degenerates into a nearly useless component, effectively turning the model into an MLP. They further investigate the reasons behind this, focusing on the ineffectiveness of current linear embedding methods in creating a well-structured latent space for the attention mechanism to operate on.  The authors design a toy dataset to better understand the attention mechanism and demonstrate that it does not work as expected.  They argue that the linear embedding layer is a bottleneck and is neither effective nor necessary.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The observation that transformers can degenerate into MLPs in time series forecasting is a valuable and novel insight. The paper goes beyond simply noting the underperformance of transformers, diving deep into the reasons *why* this occurs, and specifically focusing on the role and limitations of the attention mechanism and the embedding layer. It challenges the indiscriminate adoption of transformer architectures from NLP and vision to time series without careful consideration of the data characteristics. The toy dataset creation is also a novel approach to investigating the attention mechanism more directly.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to be quite significant. By identifying the limitations of current time series transformer architectures, it provides valuable direction for future research. If confirmed and built upon, it could lead to more effective transformer-based models for time series forecasting by emphasizing representation learning and alternative embedding techniques. It could also lead to a re-evaluation of existing models and practices within the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Thorough experimentation:** The paper presents a comprehensive suite of experiments, methodically investigating the attention mechanism from multiple angles. The ablation studies, perturbation analysis, and patch length manipulation are all well-designed and executed.
    *   **Clear explanations:** The authors provide clear and detailed explanations of their experimental setup, results, and theoretical analyses.
    *   **Interpretable toy dataset:** The use of the toy dataset is a strong point, enabling a more direct and interpretable examination of the attention mechanism's behavior.
    *   **Theoretical justification:** The paper attempts to provide theoretical backing for its empirical observations, offering a plausible explanation for the limitations of linear embeddings.
    *   **Discussion of related work:** The paper attempts to differentiate itself from related work by emphasizing the depth and scope of the investigation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited theoretical depth:** While the paper provides a theoretical analysis, it could benefit from a more rigorous mathematical treatment of the limitations of linear embeddings in capturing the complexities of time series data. It is plausible the "isomorphism" argument might be too simplistic for such complex datasets.
    *   **Dependency on specific models:** The core experiments primarily use PatchTST and iTransformer. Generalizing the findings to *all* time series transformers requires additional validation across a wider range of architectures. However, the results from the foundation models add confidence.
    *   **Oversimplification of ViT success:** The paper quickly addresses why ViT works using a linear embedding (via attention smoothing). This explanation feels cursory and could be strengthened by deeper analysis.
    *   **Lack of concrete solutions:** While the paper identifies a problem and proposes directions for future research (improved representation learning), it doesn't offer any specific, immediately implementable solutions.
    *   **Clarity on "degeneration":** While the term "degeneration" is used, it is a bit too strong. The attention may not be as useful as intended, but it is still performing *some* mixing of tokens. Quantifying what is being mixed and how would add more precision.

*   **Overall:** The paper is a valuable contribution to the field. It offers a compelling analysis of the limitations of the attention mechanism in time series transformers and provides a strong argument for the need for improved representation learning techniques. While the theoretical analysis could be strengthened, the empirical evidence is convincing. The weaknesses are outweighed by the paper's novelty and potential impact.

### Flow Matching in the Low-Noise Regime: Pathologies and a Contrastive Remedy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20952v1)
- **Authors**: Weili Zeng, Yichao Yan
- **Abstract**: Flow matching has recently emerged as a powerful alternative to diffusion models, providing a continuous-time formulation for generative modeling and representation learning. Yet, we show that this framework suffers from a fundamental instability in the low-noise regime. As noise levels approach zero, arbitrarily small perturbations in the input can induce large variations in the velocity target, causing the condition number of the learning problem to diverge. This ill-conditioning not only slows optimization but also forces the encoder to reallocate its limited Jacobian capacity toward noise directions, thereby degrading semantic representations. We provide the first theoretical analysis of this phenomenon, which we term the low-noise pathology, establishing its intrinsic link to the structure of the flow matching objective. Building on these insights, we propose Local Contrastive Flow (LCF), a hybrid training protocol that replaces direct velocity regression with contrastive feature alignment at small noise levels, while retaining standard flow matching at moderate and high noise. Empirically, LCF not only improves convergence speed but also stabilizes representation quality. Our findings highlight the critical importance of addressing low-noise pathologies to unlock the full potential of flow matching for both generation and representation learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a "low-noise pathology" in flow matching models, a type of generative model. It argues that as noise levels approach zero during training, the models become unstable, leading to poor convergence and degraded representation quality. The paper provides theoretical analysis to show that this instability stems from an ill-conditioned learning problem where small input perturbations cause large variations in the target velocity field. To address this, the authors propose "Local Contrastive Flow (LCF)", a hybrid training strategy that combines direct velocity regression at moderate/high noise levels with contrastive feature alignment at low noise levels. The LCF method aims to stabilize training and improve representation learning by leveraging more robust representations obtained at moderate noise. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and Tiny-ImageNet demonstrate that LCF improves convergence speed and representation quality compared to standard flow matching.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification and theoretical analysis of the "low-noise pathology" in flow matching is a significant contribution. While empirical observations of representation degradation at low noise levels in generative models exist, the paper provides a specific, theoretically grounded explanation for this phenomenon in the context of flow matching. This explanation, linked to the diverging condition number, is a novel insight. The proposed LCF method, while drawing inspiration from contrastive learning, is tailored to address the specific pathology, making it a novel intervention strategy within the flow matching paradigm.
*   **Significance:** Addressing instabilities in generative models, especially concerning representation learning, is crucial for enabling their broader application as universal tools for both synthesis and understanding. By clearly articulating the issue of low-noise pathology and proposing a remedy, the paper makes a tangible step toward more robust and reliable flow-matching models. The experimental results provide a compelling case that LCF solves key issues, while ablation experiments indicate it is necessary to combine flow-matching and contrastive representation learning. Improvements in convergence and representation quality translates to better downstream task performance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of low-noise pathology.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The paper offers a formal theoretical explanation of the phenomenon, linking it to operator ill-conditioning.
    *   **Practical Solution:** The LCF method provides a practical and implementable approach to mitigate the pathology.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results comprehensively demonstrate the effectiveness of LCF on standard benchmarks.
    *   **Ablation Studies**: The ablation studies, including comparisons with other approaches, provides further evidence for the importance of both flow-matching and contrastive components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are confined to relatively small datasets (CIFAR-10 and Tiny-ImageNet). While these are standard benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of LCF on larger, more complex datasets would strengthen the case.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of contrastive learning-based techniques can be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters (temperature `tau`, `lambda`, etc.). The paper provides values, but an exploration of the sensitivity to these parameters would improve the analysis.
    *   **Theoretical analysis assumes well-behaved Jacobians**: The theoretical analysis relies on assumptions about the Jacobians in the architecture, which in certain instances (particularly with Transformers), may not be entirely well-founded. Although the authors acknowledge this in the discussion after Proposition 3, more attention could be paid to investigating the validity of these claims empirically and via further theoretical analyses.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development and application of flow-matching models. It provides a more robust foundation for representation learning and opens avenues for further research into stabilizing training and improving the quality of learned representations in generative models.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel problem, provides a coherent theoretical explanation, proposes a workable solution, and validates it through thorough experimentation. The limitations are relatively minor. While larger-scale validation would strengthen the case, the current work is a solid contribution to the field of generative modeling. The clear articulation of a potentially fundamental issue, along with a validated solution, deserves a high rating.

### Beyond Stars: Bridging the Gap Between Ratings and Review Sentiment with LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20953v1)
- **Authors**: Najla Zuhir, Amna Mohammad Salim, Parvathy Premkumar, Moshiur Farazi
- **Abstract**: We present an advanced approach to mobile app review analysis aimed at addressing limitations inherent in traditional star-rating systems. Star ratings, although intuitive and popular among users, often fail to capture the nuanced feedback present in detailed review texts. Traditional NLP techniques -- such as lexicon-based methods and classical machine learning classifiers -- struggle to interpret contextual nuances, domain-specific terminology, and subtle linguistic features like sarcasm. To overcome these limitations, we propose a modular framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) enhanced by structured prompting techniques. Our method quantifies discrepancies between numerical ratings and textual sentiment, extracts detailed, feature-level insights, and supports interactive exploration of reviews through retrieval-augmented conversational question answering (RAG-QA). Comprehensive experiments conducted on three diverse datasets (AWARE, Google Play, and Spotify) demonstrate that our LLM-driven approach significantly surpasses baseline methods, yielding improved accuracy, robustness, and actionable insights in challenging and context-rich review scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a modular framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to analyze mobile app reviews, aiming to overcome the limitations of traditional star ratings and NLP techniques. The proposed framework quantifies discrepancies between numerical ratings and textual sentiment, extracts detailed feature-level insights, and supports interactive review exploration through retrieval-augmented conversational question answering (RAG-QA). The system employs structured prompting techniques and is validated on three diverse datasets (AWARE, Google Play, and Spotify). The experiments demonstrate improved accuracy, robustness, and actionable insights compared to baseline methods. A key aspect is the automated prompt optimization and inclusion of consistency checks to reduce brittleness and hallucination.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of several established techniques (LLMs, structured prompting, RAG-QA, lexicon-based baselines, and rule-based heuristics) into a cohesive framework specifically tailored for mobile app review analysis.  The *automated prompt optimization* and the *hybrid approach* of combining LLMs with lightweight consistency checks are significant contributors to the paper's novelty. While each individual component may not be groundbreaking, the combination and adaptation to the specific problem domain of app review analysis is novel. The quantification of the discrepancy between ratings and sentiment using VADER as a baseline is also a useful contribution. *However*, the paper relies heavily on established LLM techniques.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to provide developers with more actionable insights from user reviews, leading to better feature prioritization, bug fixes, and overall product strategy.  The ability to automatically extract aspect-sentiment-recommendation triplets directly addresses a key need in the industry. Demonstrating improved performance over traditional methods is significant, especially in capturing contextual nuances that simpler techniques often miss. *However*, the real-world impact remains to be seen. While the experiments show promising results, deploying such a system in a production environment and measuring its actual effect on app development cycles and user satisfaction would strengthen the significance.  The consistent use of the GPT-4 model limits the generalizability of the framework to more accessible or open-source LLMs.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Well-structured modular framework.
    * Effective use of structured prompting and automated prompt optimization.
    * Comprehensive experimental evaluation on diverse datasets.
    * Demonstrated improvement over baseline methods.
    * Addresses the issues of brittleness and hallucination in LLMs via consistency checks.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * Relies heavily on a closed-source, high-cost LLM (GPT-4), limiting accessibility and reproducibility, as well as generalization of the claims to more widely available models.
    * While it integrates multiple approaches, the *individual contributions* are not fundamentally new.
    * The evaluation focuses primarily on offline metrics (accuracy, F1-score, coherence).  A user study or real-world deployment would provide stronger evidence of its practical value.
    * The improvements over baseline may not justify the increased computational cost, especially when considering alternatives that are more cost-effective or easier to integrate.
    * The novelty is incremental and dependent on the use of GPT-4, making it vulnerable to rapid changes in LLM technology and access.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable integration of existing LLM techniques for a practical application (mobile app review analysis).  The automated prompt optimization and consistency checks address real challenges in using LLMs for this task. The experimental results support the claim of improved performance compared to baselines. *However*, the dependence on GPT-4, the incremental nature of the innovation, and the lack of real-world validation limit its overall impact. Furthermore, the paper uses VADER, a known weak baseline which inflates the perceived improvements by GPT-4.

### Tool Calling for Arabic LLMs: Data Strategies and Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20957v1)
- **Authors**: Asim Ersoy, Enes Altinisik, Husrev Taha Sencar, Kareem Darwish
- **Abstract**: Tool calling is a critical capability that allows Large Language Models (LLMs) to interact with external systems, significantly expanding their utility. However, research and resources for tool calling are predominantly English-centric, leaving a gap in our understanding of how to enable this functionality for other languages, such as Arabic. This paper investigates three key research questions: (1) the necessity of in-language (Arabic) tool-calling data versus relying on cross-lingual transfer, (2) the effect of general-purpose instruction tuning on tool-calling performance, and (3) the value of fine-tuning on specific, high-priority tools. To address these questions, we conduct extensive experiments using base and post-trained variants of an open-weight Arabic LLM. To enable this study, we bridge the resource gap by translating and adapting two open-source tool-calling datasets into Arabic. Our findings provide crucial insights into the optimal strategies for developing robust tool-augmented agents for Arabic.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the feasibility and best practices for enabling tool calling in Arabic Large Language Models (LLMs). Tool calling allows LLMs to interact with external systems, expanding their capabilities. The authors explore three key questions: the necessity of Arabic-specific tool-calling data, the impact of general-purpose instruction tuning, and the value of fine-tuning on specific, high-priority tools.  They translate existing English tool-calling datasets (Glaive and XLAM) into Arabic and create two new datasets: CustomTools and IslamicRAGTool. They conduct experiments using the Fanar Arabic LLM, analyzing performance with different training strategies. The results emphasize the importance of bilingual datasets, instruction tuning, and, crucially, fine-tuning on tool-specific data for optimal performance, especially for domain-specific tools. The paper also presents an error analysis of argument population accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in addressing tool calling specifically for the Arabic language, a relatively under-explored area. While tool calling itself isn't a new concept, the application to a non-English, low-resource language like Arabic provides a valuable contribution. The creation of the Arabic tool-calling datasets also constitutes a novel resource.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for developing Arabic-based LLM agents and applications. The study provides evidence-based recommendations for training strategies, helping researchers and developers build more effective tool-augmented Arabic LLMs. The detailed error analysis further identifies specific areas for improvement. The findings contribute to the broader understanding of cross-lingual transfer and adaptation of LLM capabilities.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Research Questions:** The paper clearly articulates the research questions it aims to answer.
    * **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The experimental setup is thorough, comparing multiple training configurations and evaluation metrics.
    * **New Datasets:** The translated and newly created datasets address a resource gap in Arabic tool calling.
    * **Detailed Error Analysis:**  The error analysis provides insights into the specific challenges of argument population accuracy.
    * **Clear Findings:** The paper presents the findings in a clear and concise manner, drawing practical conclusions.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Datasets:** The study is primarily based on two translated datasets (Glaive and XLAM) and two relatively small custom-built datasets (CustomTools and IslamicRAGTool). While translated datasets are useful, there may be subtle differences in how an Arabic speaker would formulate queries and functions. Creating native datasets would be more ideal.
    * **Model Dependency:** The findings are based on a single Arabic LLM, Fanar. While Fanar is a significant contribution itself, the results might not generalize perfectly to other Arabic LLMs.
    * **Evaluation Metric Focus:** The evaluation focuses mainly on function name detection and argument population accuracy. It lacks evaluation regarding downstream utility or correctness of the tool execution, which is crucial to assess the value of tool-calling capabilities. The reliance on recall-based metrics without considering real-world application scenarios is questionable.
    * **Over-Reliance on Paraphrasing as Error:** While paraphrasing is identified as a main issue for `ArgA` errors, its presence may indicate creative language use or a non-rigid understanding of argument population, thus being misinterpreted in the analysis.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field, providing empirical evidence and practical guidance for enabling tool calling in Arabic LLMs. However, the limited datasets, model dependency, and the absence of more comprehensive real-world application evaluations prevent it from receiving a higher score.

### Unlocking Financial Insights: An advanced Multimodal Summarization with Multimodal Output Framework for Financial Advisory Videos
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20961v1)
- **Authors**: Sarmistha Das, R E Zera Marveen Lyngkhoi, Sriparna Saha, Alka Maurya
- **Abstract**: The dynamic propagation of social media has broadened the reach of financial advisory content through podcast videos, yet extracting insights from lengthy, multimodal segments (30-40 minutes) remains challenging. We introduce FASTER (Financial Advisory Summariser with Textual Embedded Relevant images), a modular framework that tackles three key challenges: (1) extracting modality-specific features, (2) producing optimized, concise summaries, and (3) aligning visual keyframes with associated textual points. FASTER employs BLIP for semantic visual descriptions, OCR for textual patterns, and Whisper-based transcription with Speaker diarization as BOS features. A modified Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)-based loss function, equipped with BOS-specific fact-checking, ensures precision, relevance, and factual consistency against the human-aligned summary. A ranker-based retrieval mechanism further aligns keyframes with summarized content, enhancing interpretability and cross-modal coherence. To acknowledge data resource scarcity, we introduce Fin-APT, a dataset comprising 470 publicly accessible financial advisory pep-talk videos for robust multimodal research. Comprehensive cross-domain experiments confirm FASTER's strong performance, robustness, and generalizability when compared to Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs). By establishing a new standard for multimodal summarization, FASTER makes financial advisory content more accessible and actionable, thereby opening new avenues for research. The dataset and code are available at: https://github.com/sarmistha-D/FASTER
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Unlocking Financial Insights: An advanced Multimodal Summarization with Multimodal Output Framework for Financial Advisory Videos" introduces FASTER (Financial Advisory Summariser with Textual Embedded Relevant images), a novel framework for summarizing financial advisory videos.  FASTER leverages visual (BLIP-2, OCR), acoustic, and textual modalities to extract relevant information, generate concise summaries, and align visual keyframes with the textual content. It utilizes a modified Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) loss function, incorporates fact-checking, and employs a ranking mechanism for keyframe retrieval.  A key contribution is the introduction of Fin-APT, a new dataset comprising 470 annotated financial advisory videos, aimed at addressing the scarcity of such resources for multimodal research.  The authors conduct extensive experiments demonstrating FASTER's superior performance compared to existing Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in terms of accuracy, robustness, and generalizability. The paper claims that FASTER makes financial advisory content more accessible and actionable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Novelty of the Framework:** FASTER presents a modular approach that integrates multiple modalities (visual, acoustic, textual) in a structured and potentially effective way for financial video summarization. The use of curriculum learning with a modified DPO is a good step toward improving the quality of generated summaries.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The introduction of Fin-APT addresses a significant gap in available resources. The annotation process appears thorough, including expert input and a multi-phase annotator selection process.  The dataset's categorization by domain and voice tone adds value for targeted analysis.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive set of experiments comparing FASTER to a variety of established baselines. The use of ROUGE, BLEU, BERTSCORE, SSIM, and RMSE provides a relatively comprehensive view of the summary quality and alignment of visual frames.
*   **Focus on Factuality and Reliability:** The emphasis on fact-checking and reliable summaries is particularly crucial in the financial domain, where misinformation can have serious consequences.
*   **Clarity and Structure:** The paper is generally well-structured and clearly presents the proposed approach, experiments, and results.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** While the overall framework is novel, many of the individual components (BLIP-2, OCR, Whisper, BERT) are pre-existing models. The novelty lies primarily in their integration and the DPO modification.  The impact of the *modification* to DPO is not thoroughly explained, especially concerning which biases are tackled through BOS.
*   **Modality Significance:** The paper acknowledges that visual features are less significant than textual features. It would have been beneficial to dive deeper into the reasons for this, or try to leverage other strategies for greater visual feature inclusion. Perhaps investigating strategies to include charts, diagrams, or key financial metrics would benefit the overall outcome.
*   **Generalizability Claims:** While the authors claim generalizability, the experiments are primarily focused on financial advisory videos. Further testing on different types of videos or tasks is needed to substantiate this claim more robustly.
*   **Dataset Limitations:** The dataset predominantly features static backgrounds, which might limit the model's reliance on the visual modality. A more diverse dataset with more dynamic visual elements could further enhance the model's capabilities. Although the use of LLMs and VLMs highlights the need for aligned and privacy-focused summaries, they do not discuss how this model can offer those benefits.
*   **Computational Resources:** The paper mentions using a NVIDIA-A100 GPU, which shows the use of high resources for training and fine tuning, but they do not discuss how the memory requirements or training time can impact widespread implementation.

*Significance:*

The paper contributes to the growing field of multimodal summarization, particularly within the financial domain. The Fin-APT dataset is a valuable resource for future research.  The focus on factuality and reliability makes this work relevant for practical applications in financial advisory services.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has strengths in its framework design, dataset creation, and comprehensive evaluation, it also has limitations in the novelty of its individual components and the generalizability of its claims.  The marginal improvement provided by the addition of BOS features also diminishes the impact of visual components.

I believe that FASTER presents a solid step forward in multimodal summarization but does not represent a groundbreaking contribution.

### Knowledgeable Language Models as Black-Box Optimizers for Personalized Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20975v1)
- **Authors**: Michael S. Yao, Osbert Bastani, Alma Andersson, Tommaso Biancalani, Aïcha Bentaieb, Claudia Iriondo
- **Abstract**: The goal of personalized medicine is to discover a treatment regimen that optimizes a patient's clinical outcome based on their personal genetic and environmental factors. However, candidate treatments cannot be arbitrarily administered to the patient to assess their efficacy; we often instead have access to an in silico surrogate model that approximates the true fitness of a proposed treatment. Unfortunately, such surrogate models have been shown to fail to generalize to previously unseen patient-treatment combinations. We hypothesize that domain-specific prior knowledge - such as medical textbooks and biomedical knowledge graphs - can provide a meaningful alternative signal of the fitness of proposed treatments. To this end, we introduce LLM-based Entropy-guided Optimization with kNowledgeable priors (LEON), a mathematically principled approach to leverage large language models (LLMs) as black-box optimizers without any task-specific fine-tuning, taking advantage of their ability to contextualize unstructured domain knowledge to propose personalized treatment plans in natural language. In practice, we implement LEON via 'optimization by prompting,' which uses LLMs as stochastic engines for proposing treatment designs. Experiments on real-world optimization tasks show LEON outperforms both traditional and LLM-based methods in proposing individualized treatments for patients.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper you provided:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of personalized medicine, where treatment plans are tailored to individual patient characteristics but are difficult to optimize due to limited real-world feedback.  The authors introduce LEON (LLM-based Entropy-guided Optimization with knowledgeable priors), a method that leverages large language models (LLMs) as black-box optimizers, using their ability to contextualize unstructured domain knowledge (medical textbooks, biomedical knowledge graphs) to propose personalized treatment plans.  LEON uses 'optimization by prompting,' where LLMs stochastically propose treatment designs. The method constrains the optimization process by incorporating domain-specific knowledge, analyzing the distribution of proposed designs, and using an adversarial source critic.  Experiments on real-world optimization tasks demonstrate that LEON outperforms both traditional and other LLM-based methods in proposing individualized treatments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the specific *combination* of techniques to address a well-defined problem. The key is the integrated use of LLMs as optimizers with domain-specific knowledge, constrained optimization, and adversarial critic within the personalized medicine context. While LLMs are being explored for optimization in various fields, the tailored constraints and leveraging of medical knowledge in this way is the original aspect.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in personalized medicine: overcoming the limitations of imperfect surrogate models and out-of-distribution data by incorporating domain knowledge. The potential impact is in improving treatment recommendations in situations where traditional methods falter. Success could lead to a shift in how treatment strategies are proposed when real patient data is scarce.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a very important and relevant challenge in medicine with significant societal implications.
    *   **Methodological Soundness:** The approach is mathematically principled, with clear justifications for the constraints and optimization strategy.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments on a range of real-world optimization tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of LEON against relevant baselines.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and explains the proposed method clearly, making it accessible to researchers in different areas.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The method's performance is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the chosen LLM.  While the authors used a state-of-the-art model, future improvements or regressions in LLM performance could directly affect the results. Also, the cost effectiveness and privacy concern regarding using close-source LLM APIs need to be discussed.
    *   **Complexity:** The proposed method involves multiple components (LLM prompting, adversarial critic, statistical analysis of designs). Each component adds complexity that requires careful tuning. It might limit the usability in other fields.
    *   **Generalizability of Findings:** While tested on several tasks, the generalizability of the specific constraints and prompts to *all* personalized medicine scenarios is not fully established.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** While they mention potential bias in LLMs, a deeper discussion of ethical considerations related to autonomous treatment suggestions and potential impact on healthcare disparities is warranted.
    *   **Evaluation Setting:** The experiments are conducted within a simulated environment. Real-world evaluation would involve integration into clinical workflows and evaluation with human clinicians, which would yield richer insights and identify unforeseen challenges.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in the following ways:

    *   Inspire new methods for incorporating domain knowledge into LLM-based optimization.
    *   Provide a framework for addressing distribution shift in personalized medicine.
    *   Motivate further investigation into the use of LLMs in clinical decision support.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by combining several well-established techniques into a novel approach for a significant problem. The method shows promising empirical results and has the potential to improve treatment planning. However, the paper's weaknesses, particularly the reliance on a specific LLM and the need for further validation in real-world settings, temper its significance. The impact will depend on how readily the ideas are adopted, adapted, and validated in clinical settings.

### Toward Robust and Efficient ML-Based GPU Caching for Modern Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.20979v1)
- **Authors**: Peng Chen, Jiaji Zhang, Hailiang Zhao, Yirong Zhang, Jiahong Yu, Xueyan Tang, Yixuan Wang, Hao Li, Jianping Zou, Gang Xiong, Kingsum Chow, Shuibing He, Shuiguang Deng
- **Abstract**: In modern GPU inference, cache efficiency remains a major bottleneck. In recommendation models, embedding hit rates largely determine throughput, while in large language models, KV-cache misses substantially increase time-to-first-token (TTFT). Heuristic policies such as \textsc{LRU} often struggle under structured access patterns. Learning-based approaches are promising, but in practice face two major limitations: they degrade sharply when predictions are inaccurate, or they gain little even with accurate predictions due to conservative designs. Some also incur high overhead, further limiting practicality. We present \textsc{LCR}, a practical framework for learning-based GPU caching that delivers performance gains while ensuring robustness and efficiency. Its core algorithm, \textsc{LARU}, enhances \textsc{LRU} with machine-learned predictions and dynamically adapts to prediction accuracy through online error estimation. When predictions are accurate, \textsc{LARU} achieves near-optimal performance. With inaccurate predictions, it degrades gracefully to near-\textsc{LRU} performance. With \textsc{LCR}, we bridge the gap between empirical progress and theoretical advances in learning-based caching. Experiments show that \textsc{LCR} delivers consistent gains under realistic conditions. In DLRM and LLM scenarios, it improves throughput by up to 24.2\% and reduces P99 TTFT by up to 28.3\%, outperforming widely used inference systems. Even under poor predictions, its performance remains stable, demonstrating practical robustness.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty and significance score.

**Summary**

The paper introduces LCR, a learning-based GPU caching framework designed to improve performance and robustness in modern inference workloads, particularly for DLRMs and LLMs. The core of LCR is a new caching algorithm called LARU (Learning-Augmented LRU). LARU enhances LRU with machine-learned predictions and dynamically adapts to prediction accuracy through online error estimation. When predictions are accurate, LARU achieves near-optimal performance; with inaccurate predictions, it gracefully degrades to near-LRU performance. The paper details LCR's architecture, including the use of LightGBM as a predictor and flexible index structures for HBM and DRAM. Experimental results on DLRM (using HugeCTR) and LLM (using SGLang) scenarios demonstrate that LCR can improve throughput and reduce tail latency, outperforming widely used inference systems and maintaining stability even under poor predictions. The paper also provides theoretical analysis, proving that LARU achieves ideal consistency and bounded robustness.

**Critical Evaluation**

The paper tackles a critical problem in modern GPU inference: the limitations of traditional caching policies when dealing with complex and dynamic access patterns in DLRMs and LLMs. While learning-based caching is a promising area, existing approaches often suffer from fragility (poor performance under inaccurate predictions) or high overhead, limiting their practical applicability.

*Strengths:*

*   **Practical Robustness:** The key strength of this work lies in its emphasis on practical robustness. The LARU algorithm's ability to degrade gracefully to near-LRU performance when predictions are inaccurate is a significant advantage over naive learning-based approaches that blindly follow predictions. This is directly addressed with clear theoretical contributions on bounded robustness with competitive analysis.
*   **Efficiency:** The design of LCR, including the use of LightGBM as a low-overhead predictor and the exploitation of structural properties of cache indices, contributes to efficiency.  The paper clearly articulates the cost metrics and compares it against others.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating consistent gains in both DLRM and LLM scenarios. The use of real-world datasets (AD-CTR-User, QB-video, Company A's internal QA platform) and a synthetic LLM dataset increases the credibility of the results.
*   **Theoretical Grounding:** The paper provides formal proofs of LARU's consistency and robustness, strengthening the theoretical foundation of the approach.
*   **Systems Integration:** The integration of LCR into existing frameworks like HugeCTR and SGLang demonstrates the practicality and adaptability of the system.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Predictor Reliance:** While LightGBM seems like a good choice, it is still a predictor. The work doesn't deeply explore alternative prediction strategies and their impact on performance. The authors also claim a low memory footprint for the predictor, but do not explicitly provide numbers.
*   **Generality to Other Workloads:** The paper focuses on DLRM and LLM workloads. While LCR's design principles may generalize to other scenarios, the paper lacks concrete evidence of its applicability beyond these specific use cases.
*   **Limited Comparison with Theoretical Algorithms:** While the paper positions the work against theoretical algorithms, the evaluation mainly focuses on LRU and other heuristics. A direct comparison with a practical implementation of a robust theoretical algorithm would be beneficial.  The evaluation is limited by the number of related works due to constraints.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in the design of LARU, which balances the benefits of learning-based caching with the crucial requirement of robustness. The theoretical analysis and the practical integration into widely used inference frameworks contribute to the significance of the work. It addresses a crucial gap in the field of learning-based caching by providing a system that is both performant and reliable in real-world deployment scenarios. The work also effectively connects theoretical advancements in algorithms-with-predictions to real-world GPU memory management challenges.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a strong contribution to the field by addressing the critical problem of robustness in learning-based GPU caching. The LARU algorithm's design, coupled with the comprehensive evaluation and theoretical analysis, demonstrates significant value. The emphasis on practical deployment and integration into existing frameworks is commendable. While the reliance on the LightGBM predictor and the limited generalization beyond DLRM and LLM workloads are minor weaknesses, the paper's overall contribution warrants a high score. It provides a solid foundation for future research in learning-based caching and opens up opportunities for deploying such techniques in production environments.

Score: 7.5
