### SupCLAP: Controlling Optimization Trajectory Drift in Audio-Text Contrastive Learning with Support Vector Regularization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21033v1)
- **Authors**: Jiehui Luo, Yuguo Yin, Yuxin Xie, Jinghan Ru, Xianwei Zhuang, Minghua He, Aofan Liu, Zihan Xiong, Dongchao Yang
- **Abstract**: Contrastive language-audio pretraining, which aims to unify multimodal representations in a shared embedding space, serves as a cornerstone for building a wide range of applications, from cross-modal retrieval to cutting-edge multimodal large language models. However, we find that the perpendicular component of the pushing force from negative samples in contrastive learning is a double-edged sword: it contains rich supplementary information from negative samples, yet its unconstrained nature causes optimization trajectory drift and training instability. To address this, we propose Support Vector Regularization (SVR), a method that introduces an auxiliary support vector to control this perpendicular component, aiming to harness its rich information while mitigating the associated trajectory drift. The efficacy of SVR is critically governed by its semantic radius, for which we explore two unsupervised modeling strategies: direct parameterization and an adaptive radius predictor module enhanced with constraints to improve its predicting accuracy. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that our method surpasses widely used baselines like InfoNCE and SigLIP loss across classification, monolingual retrieval, and multilingual retrieval on standard audio-text datasets. Both the theoretical analysis and the experimental results on optimizing trajectory drift validate the correctness and effectiveness of our SVR method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a problem called "optimization trajectory drift" in contrastive audio-text learning. This drift arises from the perpendicular component of the pushing force from negative samples. While this component contains useful information, its unconstrained nature can destabilize training. The authors propose Support Vector Regularization (SVR) to control this component. SVR introduces a support vector to guide optimization, with the degree of control governed by a semantic radius. They explore two unsupervised methods for determining this radius: static parameterization and an adaptive radius predictor. Experiments on standard datasets show that their method (SupCLAP) outperforms InfoNCE and SigLIP baselines. They analyze the optimization trajectory to validate their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification and formalization of "optimization trajectory drift" as a distinct problem in contrastive learning is a valuable contribution. While the idea of pulling positive examples together and pushing negative examples apart is fundamental, explicitly decomposing the pushing force and recognizing the perpendicular component as both beneficial and detrimental *is* a novel insight. Using a "support vector" concept to regularize this is a creative solution. The explorations of static versus dynamic radius modeling add another layer of novelty. However, the connection to SVM is rather loose. The "support vector" is more of a regularizer than a true SVM-based classification boundary.

*   **Significance:** The improvements demonstrated in the experiments (particularly in multilingual settings) suggest that SupCLAP can lead to more robust and accurate audio-text representation learning. This is important because these representations are used in a wide range of applications, including retrieval and multimodal LLMs.  The results, though incremental, show a clear and consistent advantage over strong baselines. The theoretical analysis (though not completely revolutionary) provides a good framework for understanding the dynamics of contrastive learning. If the proposed SVR proves to be extensible to other cross-modal learning tasks, its significance would be amplified.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of optimization trajectory drift.
    *   **Sound solution:** The SVR approach is well-motivated and intuitively makes sense.
    *   **Thorough experiments:** The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering several datasets, tasks, and ablation studies.
    *   **Theoretical analysis:** The gradient space analysis provides insights into how SVR works.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are positive, the performance gains over the baselines are not dramatically large.
    *   **Limited Generality:**  The paper focuses primarily on audio-text learning. It would be stronger if the authors provided evidence or theoretical arguments suggesting the approach is applicable to other modalities.
    *   **"Support Vector" Misnomer:** The term "support vector" is used loosely and may be misleading, as the method doesn't directly implement an SVM. A more accurate name might be "Regularized Trajectory Control"

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research by encouraging researchers to think more critically about the dynamics of contrastive learning and the role of negative samples. The SVR concept could be adapted and applied to other contrastive learning problems. The analysis provides a structured view of the gradient landscape.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution, presenting a novel perspective on a fundamental problem in contrastive learning. It provides a well-motivated solution and demonstrates its effectiveness through thorough experiments. However, the improvements are incremental, and the connection to SVM is somewhat weak. The broader impact remains to be seen, but the identified issue and proposed framework do offer meaningful advancements to the field.

### Generative AI for FFRDCs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21040v1)
- **Authors**: Arun S. Maiya
- **Abstract**: Federally funded research and development centers (FFRDCs) face text-heavy workloads, from policy documents to scientific and engineering papers, that are slow to analyze manually. We show how large language models can accelerate summarization, classification, extraction, and sense-making with only a few input-output examples. To enable use in sensitive government contexts, we apply OnPrem$.$LLM, an open-source framework for secure and flexible application of generative AI. Case studies on defense policy documents and scientific corpora, including the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) and National Science Foundation (NSF) Awards, demonstrate how this approach enhances oversight and strategic analysis while maintaining auditability and data sovereignty.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OnPrem.LLM, an open-source Python toolkit designed to facilitate the secure and flexible application of generative AI, especially within Federally Funded Research and Development Centers (FFRDCs). It addresses the challenge of analyzing text-heavy workloads in sensitive government contexts by leveraging large language models (LLMs) for tasks like summarization, classification, extraction, and sense-making. The paper outlines the architecture of OnPrem.LLM, which consists of four primary modules: LLM, Ingest, Pipelines, and App.  It presents example use cases with publicly available data, mitigation strategies, and quickstart instructions. It highlights the importance of auditability, a human-in-the-loop approach, and sovereign deployment as strategies to minimize risks and misuse of generative AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper is somewhat limited.  While it presents a cohesive system called OnPrem.LLM, it builds upon existing open-source tools and techniques. The core ideas of using LLMs for document intelligence, integrating with various LLM backends, and employing RAG are well-established in the field. The implementation focuses on facilitating the use of these techniques in FFRDCs, which is valuable but not radically innovative. The integration of dense, sparse and dual stores is interesting, but its novelty is dampened by existing hybrid search solutions. The attempt_fix functionality is promising, but more detail on its performance would be beneficial.

**Significance:**  The significance lies in the practical application and focus on the specific needs and constraints of FFRDCs.  The emphasis on security, data sovereignty, and auditability is crucial in this context, making the toolkit potentially valuable for government analysts and researchers. The paper's strength is that it translates complex concepts into accessible workflows, targeting users with limited coding skills. The inclusion of mitigation strategies for hallucination and data exposure demonstrates awareness of the challenges associated with deploying LLMs in sensitive environments. However, a more in-depth analysis of the performance, accuracy, and scalability of OnPrem.LLM on real-world FFRDC datasets would significantly strengthen the paper's claims.  The web-based UI is a good addition, and will encourage wider adoption by non-technical users.

**Strengths:**

*   Provides a practical, open-source solution for applying generative AI in FFRDCs.
*   Addresses critical security and data sovereignty concerns.
*   Includes pre-built workflows and a web UI to ease adoption.
*   Emphasizes auditability and human-in-the-loop approaches.
*   Comprehensive description of each module's functionality.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited novelty, building on existing tools and techniques.
*   Lacks detailed performance evaluation on real-world FFRDC datasets.
*   More detailed exploration of the "attempt_fix" functionality would be beneficial.
*   Could include more concrete examples of specific use cases and their impact.
*   Limited discussion of scalability and resource requirements.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the adoption of generative AI in FFRDCs by providing a user-friendly and secure toolkit. The open-source nature of OnPrem.LLM allows for customization and extension, making it adaptable to different needs. However, the lack of comprehensive performance data and the limited novelty might hinder its widespread adoption unless further research and validation are conducted.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and practical tool, but its lack of substantial novelty prevents it from achieving a higher score. While OnPrem.LLM addresses a real need and provides a user-friendly interface, its reliance on existing techniques and limited performance evaluation dampen its overall impact. The emphasis on security and auditability is commendable, but more rigorous validation and in-depth analysis are required to establish its effectiveness and justify a higher score. Further research focusing on performance metrics, scalability, and real-world applications would significantly enhance the paper's significance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Behind RoPE: How Does Causal Mask Encode Positional Information?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21042v1)
- **Authors**: Junu Kim, Xiao Liu, Zhenghao Lin, Lei Ji, Yeyun Gong, Edward Choi
- **Abstract**: While explicit positional encodings such as RoPE are a primary source of positional information in Transformer decoders, the causal mask also provides positional information. In this work, we prove that the causal mask can induce position-dependent patterns in attention scores, even without parameters or causal dependency in the input. Our theoretical analysis indicates that the induced attention pattern tends to favor nearby query-key pairs, mirroring the behavior of common positional encodings. Empirical analysis confirms that trained models exhibit the same behavior, with learned parameters further amplifying these patterns. Notably, we found that the interaction of causal mask and RoPE distorts RoPE's relative attention score patterns into non-relative ones. We consistently observed this effect in modern large language models, suggesting the importance of considering the causal mask as a source of positional information alongside explicit positional encodings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the causal mask, a standard component of Transformer decoders, encodes positional information.  The authors theoretically prove that the causal mask, even without explicit positional encodings (like RoPE), parameters, or causal dependencies in the input, can induce position-dependent patterns in attention scores. This induced pattern favors nearby query-key pairs.  They empirically verify this finding by simulating a Transformer decoder without parameters or explicit positional encodings, showing that the attention patterns align with their theoretical analysis. Furthermore, the paper analyzes how the causal mask interacts with RoPE in modern large language models (LLMs) and finds that the causal mask distorts RoPE's relative attention pattern into a non-relative one. This effect is observed in various LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea that the causal mask itself encodes positional information is a contribution. Prior works have noted this, but this paper provides a mathematical analysis and empirical confirmation of this phenomenon, particularly how the mask induces position-dependent patterns *without* other mechanisms. The observation of the interaction between the causal mask and RoPE, and its effect on the relative positional encoding property, is also novel and potentially significant.
*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they challenge the conventional understanding of Transformer decoders where explicit positional encodings are regarded as the primary source of positional information. Understanding the contribution of the causal mask is crucial for better model design, especially concerning length generalization and optimization.  The identified interaction between the causal mask and RoPE has practical implications for tuning and improving LLMs. This could influence future research aimed at improving positional encoding schemes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Rigor:** The paper provides a mathematical proof to support its claim, giving the argument a solid foundation.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Simulations and experiments on real LLMs corroborate the theoretical analysis and demonstrate the real-world relevance of the findings.
    *   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper presents its argument in a clear and structured manner.
    *   **Relevance to Current Research:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant issue in the field of LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Analysis of Feedforward Network Effects:** The theoretical analysis simplifies the architecture by excluding the feedforward network layers. These layers likely play a crucial role in shaping the learned positional representations, and their absence weakens the completeness of the analysis. It would be stronger if the paper included discussion around how it could be extended to cover the FFN layers.
    *   **Lack of Direct Performance Impact Study:**  The paper analyzes the attention patterns but doesn't directly quantify the impact of the causal mask's positional encoding on downstream tasks like perplexity or accuracy. A more direct performance impact is crucial for confirming the paper's contribution to real-world LLM scenarios.
    *   **Limited exploration of alternative explanations**: While the paper provides a compelling explanation for the observed behavior, it would be useful to explore alternative explanations and provide a more rigorous comparison with existing methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by prompting a more holistic view of positional information in Transformer decoders.  It could also inspire new methods for jointly leveraging the causal mask and explicit positional encodings. Furthermore, the interaction analysis of RoPE and the causal mask might help improve LLM performance and length generalization.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper makes a novel contribution to the understanding of positional information in Transformer decoders, particularly concerning the role of the causal mask and its interaction with RoPE. However, the limited analysis of feedforward networks, and most critically, the absence of a quantitative performance evaluation on downstream tasks weakens the argument for its real-world significance. It provides a solid theoretical foundation and empirical evidence for its claims, but the implications for practical model performance need further investigation to justify a higher score.  The finding of the interaction between causal mask and RoPE is interesting, but needs more validation in different settings to justify a higher score. It has potential to influence research in LLM optimization, but the precise impact remains unclear.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Combinatorial Creativity: A New Frontier in Generalization Abilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21043v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Schapiro, Sumuk Shashidhar, Alexi Gladstone, Jonah Black, Royce Moon, Dilek Hakkani-Tur, Lav R. Varshney
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) systems, and large language models (LLMs) in particular, are increasingly employed for creative tasks like scientific idea generation, constituting a form of generalization from training data unaddressed by existing conceptual frameworks. Though in many ways similar to forms of compositional generalization (CG), combinatorial creativity (CC) is an open-ended ability. Instead of evaluating for accuracy or correctness against fixed targets, which would contradict the open-ended nature of CC, we propose a theoretical framework and algorithmic task for evaluating outputs by their degrees of novelty and utility. From here, we make several important empirical contributions: (1) We obtain the first insights into the scaling behavior of creativity for LLMs. (2) We discover that, for fixed compute budgets, there exist optimal model depths and widths for creative ability. (3) We find that the ideation-execution gap, whereby LLMs excel at generating novel scientific ideas but struggle to ensure their practical feasibility, may be explained by a more fundamental novelty-utility tradeoff characteristic of creativity algorithms in general. Importantly, this tradeoff remains persistent even at scale, casting doubt on the long-term creative potential of LLMs in their current form. Together, our conceptual framework and empirical findings provide a foundation for understanding and improving creativity in modern AI models, marking a new frontier in generalization abilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a theoretical framework and an algorithmic task to evaluate combinatorial creativity (CC) in AI systems, particularly large language models (LLMs). Unlike traditional compositional generalization, CC focuses on open-ended generation of novel and useful artifacts. The framework models creativity within a conceptual space represented as a graph, where models find novel paths between concepts while adhering to logical constraints. Through extensive experiments on decoder-only Transformers, the authors investigate the scaling behavior of creativity with varying model size, depth, and width. Key findings include: (1) a predictable improvement in creative performance with increased model size; (2) the existence of optimal model depths and widths for creative ability, achieving a sweet spot; and (3) a fundamental novelty-utility tradeoff that persists even at scale, suggesting limitations in the creative potential of LLMs in their current form. The paper connects the novelty-utility tradeoff to the observed "ideation-execution gap" in LLMs generating scientific ideas.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper offers several novel aspects:

*   **Formalized Framework for CC Evaluation:** The explicit mathematical framework for assessing creativity (novelty and utility) is a significant step forward. Prior work has often lacked a quantitative measure.
*   **Algorithmic Task for Open-Ended Evaluation:** The designed task, with inclusion/exclusion constraints in a graph-based conceptual space, provides a controlled environment to study creative generation, something missing from prior literature.
*   **Architectural Insights:** The findings regarding optimal depth and width for LLMs concerning creativity are interesting. Showing how there's a sweet spot rather than just "bigger is always better" adds a new layer to the literature.
*   **Empirical Evidence of the Novelty-Utility Tradeoff:** While the concept is known, demonstrating it *empirically* and at scale in LLMs is a valuable contribution.
*   **Connection to Ideation-Execution Gap:** Linking the novelty-utility tradeoff directly to the documented problems LLMs have with actually implementing creative ideas provides a tangible interpretation to the theoretical finding.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical area: the potential of AI systems for *truly* creative tasks. Here's how it's significant:

*   **Moves Beyond Task Accuracy:** CC moves past standard accuracy-based evaluations to assess genuine novelty and utility, aligning more closely with how humans judge creativity.
*   **Architectural Implications:**  The discovery of optimal architectural ratios offers practical guidance for designing LLMs specifically for creative applications.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The work provides a theoretical basis lacking in much of the research on applying LLMs to creative problems like scientific discovery. This aids in interpreting empirical observations and designing future models.
*   **Highlighting Limitations:** The persistent novelty-utility tradeoff is a sobering finding, implying that simply scaling current LLM architectures may not overcome inherent limitations in creative potential. This suggests a need for new architectural or training approaches.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data:** The experiments rely entirely on synthetic data.  While this offers control, it raises questions about the generalizability of findings to real-world creative domains. Would the sweet spot for model depth/width shift when applied to, say, creative writing or scientific hypothesis generation?
*   **Limited Model Scale:** While exploring multiple architectures, the largest model size is still only 100M parameters, small in today's LLM landscape. It's unclear if the trends (novelty-utility tradeoff, optimal architectures) hold for models in the billion+ parameter range.
*   **Scope of Creativity:** It focuses solely on combinatorial creativity, neglecting other important aspects like exploratory and transformational creativity as discussed by Boden. This makes the framework only partially representative of "creativity" as a whole.
*   **Abstract Task:** While cleverly designed, the graph pathfinding task is a very abstract proxy for real-world creative endeavors. The ecological validity is uncertain.

**Justification of Score:**

Despite its weaknesses, the paper makes significant contributions to the field. The formalized framework and the rigorous empirical investigation of architectural choices and limitations are valuable. The discovery of a novelty-utility tradeoff and its connection to the ideation-execution gap is particularly insightful. However, the use of synthetic data and the limited scale of the models restrain the impact of the findings.
A score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with high potential, yet constrained by methodological choices.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforcement Learning Fine-Tuning Enhances Activation Intensity and Diversity in the Internal Circuitry of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21044v1)
- **Authors**: Honglin Zhang, Qianyue Hao, Fengli Xu, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) acquire extensive prior knowledge through large-scale pretraining and can be further enhanced via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training. A growing body of evidence has shown that RL fine-tuning improves the capability of LLMs beyond what SFT alone achieves. However, the underlying mechanisms why RL fine-tuning is able to enhance the capability of various LLMs with distinct intrinsic characteristics remain underexplored. In this study, we draw inspiration from prior work on edge attribution patching (EAP) to investigate the internal differences of LLMs before and after RL fine-tuning. Our analysis across multiple model families shows two robust effects of online RL post-training: (i) an overall increase in activation intensity, indicating that more internal pathways are engaged and their signals become stronger, and (ii) greater diversity in activation patterns, reflected by higher entropy and less concentrated edge distributions. These changes suggest that RL reshapes information flow to be both more redundant and more flexible, which may explain its advantage in generalization. Notably, models fine-tuned with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) deviate from these trends, exhibiting substantially weaker or inconsistent internal changes compared to PPO- and GRPO-based training. Together, our findings provide a unified view of how RL fine-tuning systematically alters the internal circuitry of LLMs and highlight the methodological distinctions between online RL and preference-based approaches. Our code is open source at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/llm_rl_probing_analysis-F673.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the internal mechanisms of large language models (LLMs) before and after reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning. Using an edge attribution patching (EAP) framework, the authors analyze how RL fine-tuning affects the internal circuitry of LLMs. They find that RL fine-tuning leads to two key effects: (1) increased activation intensity, indicating more pathways are engaged with stronger signals, and (2) greater diversity in activation patterns, suggesting more redundant and flexible information flow.  A notable exception to these findings arises with models trained using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which shows weaker or inconsistent changes compared to PPO- and GRPO-based methods. The paper argues that these internal changes induced by online RL methods contribute to the improved generalization capabilities observed in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Addresses an Important Question:**  The paper tackles a fundamental question in the field of LLMs: Why does RL fine-tuning often outperform supervised fine-tuning (SFT)? Understanding the internal changes is crucial for developing better training methods and more robust models.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of EAP to probe internal circuitry offers a good compromise between computational feasibility and the ability to analyze information flow. The methodology is well-described and grounded in prior work.
*   **Interesting and Consistent Findings:** The observations of increased activation intensity and greater diversity in activation patterns after online RL fine-tuning are consistent across multiple model families and provide a compelling explanation for RL's benefits.
*   **Important Counterpoint with DPO:**  The observation that DPO doesn't consistently exhibit the same internal changes as PPO/GRPO is significant. It highlights the methodological differences between preference-based and online RL approaches and might explain some of the debates about DPO's effectiveness.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it relatively easy to follow the analysis and understand the conclusions.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide source code for their work

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Computational Cost:** While EAP is efficient compared to full ablation, it still involves substantial computation. The analysis could potentially be expanded to even larger models, but this is limited by resources.
*   **Interpretation Challenges:** Interpreting the meaning of specific edge activations within these models remains challenging. While the paper identifies overall trends, it doesn't necessarily pinpoint specific circuits responsible for specific improved capabilities.
*   **Generalization to other architectures:** The study utilizes a specific class of Transformer-based LLMs.  It's not entirely clear how well the findings generalize to other model architectures or training paradigms (e.g., mixture-of-experts models, different pre-training objectives).
*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the study analyzes mathematical problem-solving, it would be valuable to see if the findings hold across a wider variety of tasks (e.g., creative writing, code generation).

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic analysis of the internal changes induced by RL fine-tuning in LLMs, linking these changes to improved generalization ability.  While some prior work has investigated LLM internals, this study is significant in its focus on the *effects of RL* and its distinction between online RL and preference-based methods.  It provides a unified view that bridges empirical performance gains with interpretable shifts in information flow. It represents a valuable step towards understanding how RL transforms LLMs at a mechanistic level.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing empirical evidence for how RL fine-tuning affects the internal circuitry of LLMs, and relating those changes to improved task performance. The robust results across different LLM families and the observation around DPO training are novel and significant contributions. However, the challenges in directly interpreting the edge activations limit the depth of understanding.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeoRef: Referring Expressions in Geometry via Task Formulation, Synthetic Supervision, and Reinforced MLLM-based Solutions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21050v1)
- **Authors**: Bing Liu, Wenqiang Yv, Xuzheng Yang, Shichang Wang, Junzhuo Liu, Peng Wang, Guoqing Wang, Yang Yang, Heng Tao Shen
- **Abstract**: AI-driven geometric problem solving is a complex vision-language task that requires accurate diagram interpretation, mathematical reasoning, and robust cross-modal grounding. A foundational yet underexplored capability for this task is the ability to identify and interpret geometric elements based on natural language queries. To address this, we introduce the task of Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) for geometric problems, which evaluates whether models can localize points, shapes, and spatial relations in diagrams in response to textual prompts. We present GeoRef, a benchmark dataset constructed from existing geometric problem corpora, featuring diverse, high-quality annotations and queries. Due to the lack of annotated data for this task, we generate a large-scale synthetic training dataset using a structured geometric formal language, enabling broad coverage of geometric concepts and facilitating model adaptation. We explore two fine-tuning approaches: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Our results show that GRPO significantly outperforms SFT by better aligning model behavior with task-specific rewards. Furthermore, we propose a verify-and-regenerate mechanism that detects incorrect predictions and re-infers answers using contextual reasoning history, further boosting accuracy. Notably, even state-of-the-art Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with this task, underscoring the necessity of explicitly evaluating and strengthening geometric grounding as a prerequisite for robust geometric problem solving. Moreover, models trained on GeoRef demonstrate measurable improvements on downstream geometric reasoning tasks, highlighting the broader value of REC as a foundation for multimodal mathematical understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GeoRef paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the task of Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) for geometric problems, a diagnostic capability for evaluating how well AI models identify and interpret geometric elements in diagrams based on natural language queries.  To support this task, the authors present GeoRef, a benchmark dataset constructed from existing geometric problem corpora and augmented with synthetically generated training data using a structured geometric formal language. They explore supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to adapt models to the task, also proposing a verify-and-regenerate mechanism to refine predictions.  The results show that even state-of-the-art MLLMs struggle with geometric grounding, but training on GeoRef improves performance on downstream geometric reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core contribution – introducing REC for geometric problems and creating the GeoRef dataset – represents a meaningful advancement. While REC exists for general images, its application to geometric diagrams is novel and fills a gap in evaluating multimodal mathematical reasoning. The synthetic data generation approach is also a notable contribution, allowing for scalable and controlled dataset creation, addressing the data scarcity issue. The GRPO and verification mechanism add incremental improvements. However, the core innovation centers around the task and the dataset, while the methods are adaptations of existing techniques.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in its explicit focus on geometric grounding, a component often bypassed by end-to-end MLLM approaches. By isolating and evaluating this capability, the paper highlights a key limitation of current models and provides a valuable tool for future research. The improvements seen on downstream geometric reasoning tasks further demonstrate the practical importance of this grounding ability. GeoRef dataset acts as a crucial tool for enabling this type of targeted evaluation and improvement.  The experiments are comprehensive, benchmarking a variety of models and exploring different training strategies.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and task formulation.
    * Well-constructed and diverse dataset (GeoRef), combining existing and synthetic data.
    * Comprehensive experimental evaluation with various models and training techniques.
    * Demonstrated transferability to downstream tasks, highlighting the broader value of REC.
    * Rigorous annotation and evaluation methodology that addresses the ambiguities inherent in geometric figures.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The GRPO and verification mechanisms, while effective, build upon existing techniques and are not particularly innovative on their own. They serve to augment rather than fundamentally revolutionize the approach.
    * The paper acknowledges that models still struggle with complex geometric images and hallucinate. Further investigation into these limitations would be beneficial.
    * The heavy reliance on synthetic data raises questions regarding how well models trained solely on GeoRef would generalize to unseen *real-world* geometric diagrams, which may exhibit complexities and variations not captured in the synthetic data.
    * While the work addresses the issue of limited annotations in existing geometric datasets, the question generation process, albeit guided by prompts, could potentially introduce biases in the dataset that are not fully accounted for.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research in geometric problem solving and multimodal mathematical reasoning. GeoRef dataset is expected to become a standard benchmark for evaluating geometric grounding, and the insights gained from this work can inform the development of more robust and interpretable AI models for mathematical tasks. The paper also highlights the importance of targeted training and evaluation for specific capabilities, rather than relying solely on end-to-end performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by introducing a novel diagnostic task and dataset for geometric grounding.  While the methodologies are not entirely groundbreaking, the overall impact of the paper is significant. GeoRef fills a critical gap and the work serves as an important stepping stone for building more comprehensive and interpretable AI systems for solving geometry problems.

### When Instructions Multiply: Measuring and Estimating LLM Capabilities of Multiple Instructions Following
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21051v1)
- **Authors**: Keno Harada, Yudai Yamazaki, Masachika Taniguchi, Edison Marrese-Taylor, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to real-world scenarios, it becomes crucial to understand their ability to follow multiple instructions simultaneously. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce two specialized benchmarks for fundamental domains where multiple instructions following is important: Many Instruction-Following Eval (ManyIFEval) for text generation with up to ten instructions, and Style-aware Mostly Basic Programming Problems (StyleMBPP) for code generation with up to six instructions. Our experiments with the created benchmarks across ten LLMs reveal that performance consistently degrades as the number of instructions increases. Furthermore, given the fact that evaluating all the possible combinations of multiple instructions is computationally impractical in actual use cases, we developed three types of regression models that can estimate performance on both unseen instruction combinations and different numbers of instructions which are not used during training. We demonstrate that a logistic regression model using instruction count as an explanatory variable can predict performance of following multiple instructions with approximately 10% error, even for unseen instruction combinations. We show that relatively modest sample sizes (500 for ManyIFEval and 300 for StyleMBPP) are sufficient for performance estimation, enabling efficient evaluation of LLMs under various instruction combinations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the crucial need to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) regarding their ability to follow multiple instructions simultaneously, which is vital for real-world applications. To achieve this, the authors introduce two novel benchmarks: ManyIFEval (for text generation with up to ten instructions) and StyleMBPP (for code generation with up to six instructions). These benchmarks are designed to maintain consistent task descriptions while varying the number of instructions, enabling a focused analysis of the impact of instruction count on model performance. The authors conduct experiments across ten LLMs, revealing performance degradation as the number of instructions increases. They also propose regression models to estimate performance on unseen instruction combinations, demonstrating that a logistic regression model using instruction count as an explanatory variable can predict performance with reasonable accuracy. The study concludes that relatively modest sample sizes are sufficient for accurate performance estimation, facilitating efficient LLM evaluation under various instruction combinations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant and timely problem: the rigorous evaluation of LLMs' ability to handle multiple, simultaneous instructions. This capability is often overlooked in standard benchmarks, which typically focus on single-instruction scenarios. The authors' work addresses a gap in the existing literature by providing specialized benchmarks and methodologies for a more comprehensive assessment.

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty lies in the creation of the ManyIFEval and StyleMBPP benchmarks. These benchmarks are designed with a controlled experimental setup, ensuring that the core task description remains consistent while varying the number of instructions. This allows for a focused analysis of the effect of instruction count, which is a significant improvement over existing benchmarks that often conflate instruction count with task complexity. The proposed regression models for performance estimation on unseen instruction combinations add further value by providing a way to efficiently evaluate LLMs without exhaustively testing all possibilities.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the evaluation and development of LLMs for real-world applications. By providing benchmarks and methodologies for assessing multiple-instruction-following capabilities, the authors enable researchers and practitioners to identify areas where LLMs need improvement and to develop models that are better equipped to handle complex, multi-faceted tasks. The finding that modest sample sizes are sufficient for performance estimation has practical implications for reducing the computational cost of LLM evaluation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of evaluating multiple-instruction-following capabilities and its importance for real-world applications.
*   **Controlled Experimental Design:** The benchmarks are designed with a controlled experimental setup, allowing for a focused analysis of the effect of instruction count.
*   **Objective Evaluation:** The use of programmatic rule-based verification ensures objective and reliable assessment of model performance.
*   **Efficient Performance Estimation:** The proposed regression models provide a way to efficiently evaluate LLMs without exhaustively testing all possibilities.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across ten LLMs, providing strong empirical evidence for the claims made.
*   **Generalizability:** The study examines the relationship between training sample size and estimation error showing that the estimation method also generalizes across unseen instruction count.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Instruction Types:** The benchmarks primarily focus on relatively simple instructions that can be objectively evaluated, such as keyword inclusion, character counts, and formatting rules. More complex instruction types involving semantic understanding, conditional logic, or multi-step procedures are not included.
*   **Empirical Focus:** The paper primarily focuses on empirical observation and modeling, with limited investigation into the underlying mechanisms behind the observed performance degradation.
*   **Limited number of models**: While 10 models were explored, some models not publicly available could have been added to the benchmark.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a significant problem and providing novel benchmarks and methodologies for evaluating LLMs' ability to handle multiple instructions. The controlled experimental design, objective evaluation, and efficient performance estimation are key strengths. While the limitations regarding instruction types and mechanistic understanding are acknowledged, the paper's overall impact and potential to influence future research warrant a high score. The paper fills an important gap in the LLM evaluation landscape, providing a more rigorous and realistic assessment of model capabilities for real-world applications.

### Disagreements in Reasoning: How a Model's Thinking Process Dictates Persuasion in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21054v1)
- **Authors**: Haodong Zhao, Jidong Li, Zhaomin Wu, Tianjie Ju, Zhuosheng Zhang, Bingsheng He, Gongshen Liu
- **Abstract**: The rapid proliferation of recent Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), where Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) usually collaborate to solve complex problems, necessitates a deep understanding of the persuasion dynamics that govern their interactions. This paper challenges the prevailing hypothesis that persuasive efficacy is primarily a function of model scale. We propose instead that these dynamics are fundamentally dictated by a model's underlying cognitive process, especially its capacity for explicit reasoning. Through a series of multi-agent persuasion experiments, we uncover a fundamental trade-off we term the Persuasion Duality. Our findings reveal that the reasoning process in LRMs exhibits significantly greater resistance to persuasion, maintaining their initial beliefs more robustly. Conversely, making this reasoning process transparent by sharing the "thinking content" dramatically increases their ability to persuade others. We further consider more complex transmission persuasion situations and reveal complex dynamics of influence propagation and decay within multi-hop persuasion between multiple agent networks. This research provides systematic evidence linking a model's internal processing architecture to its external persuasive behavior, offering a novel explanation for the susceptibility of advanced models and highlighting critical implications for the safety, robustness, and design of future MAS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates persuasion dynamics within Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) composed of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). It challenges the prevalent belief that persuasion efficacy is solely determined by model scale, proposing instead that a model's underlying cognitive process, specifically its capacity for explicit reasoning, is the primary driver. Through multi-agent persuasion experiments, the paper identifies a "Persuasion Duality" trade-off: LRMs, while more resistant to persuasion due to their reasoning capabilities, become dramatically more persuasive when their "thinking content" is shared. The paper further explores complex multi-hop persuasion scenarios, revealing influence propagation and decay patterns. It also identifies that attention mechanisms within the models can be manipulated by confident rhetorical cues instead of logical arguments, demonstrating how explicit reasoning can be overridden. Finally, the paper introduces a prompt-based mitigation strategy to improve model robustness against manipulation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central premise – that a model's reasoning process is a crucial determinant of persuasion dynamics, rather than simply model scale – presents a valuable shift in perspective. This is a significant departure from existing research that often focuses solely on model size and datasets. The introduction of the "Persuasion Duality" concept is also a unique and thought-provoking contribution.
*   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The experiments are reasonably comprehensive, covering a variety of models (both open and closed source) and tasks (objective and subjective). The use of multiple models to promote generalizability is a good strength.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The discovery that making reasoning transparent enhances persuasiveness is counterintuitive and intriguing. The analysis of attention mechanisms and the identification of a susceptibility to confident language are also valuable insights. The multi-hop persuasion analysis also adds complexity and realism.
*   **Practical Implications:** The study offers actionable insights for designing safer and more robust MAS, including the importance of explicit reasoning mechanisms and the prompt-based mitigation strategies.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper touches upon attention mechanisms, it lacks a deeper, more granular analysis of *how* reasoning processes influence persuasion. A more in-depth investigation into the specific neurons or network structures involved in reasoning and their relation to persuasion would significantly strengthen the paper.
*   **Scope of Multi-Hop Experimentation:** The multi-hop experiments, while interesting, could benefit from further exploration. Analyzing the structural properties of networks that lead to amplification or attenuation would provide more actionable insights. More advanced causal inference methods could strengthen the analysis.
*   **Simplified Persuasion Scenarios:** The persuasion scenarios used are relatively simple, consisting of direct statements and rebuttals. Real-world persuasion is a more nuanced process involving framing, storytelling, and emotional appeals. The generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world settings could be questioned.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases**: The paper highlights successful persuasion attempts and mitigation strategies, but a more comprehensive analysis of *why* certain strategies fail would be beneficial.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the growing field of AI safety, particularly in the context of increasingly autonomous MAS. The emphasis on reasoning processes, and the discovery of the Persuasion Duality trade-off, offer new avenues for research and development in designing more robust and aligned AI systems. The paper's findings highlight the importance of transparency and critical thinking in AI, as well as the need for mitigation strategies to protect against manipulation. It's clear that this research has the potential to influence the direction of future research in LLMs and their applications.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper is strong in its novelty and experimental design, it could be improved by delving deeper into the mechanistic aspects of reasoning and persuasion. The use of relatively simplistic persuasion scenarios and analysis of failure cases also represent areas for improvement. Its practical implications and contributions to the field of AI safety and AI alignment make it a significant contribution, but the paper falls short of being truly transformative.

### PMark: Towards Robust and Distortion-free Semantic-level Watermarking with Channel Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21057v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Huo, Shuliang Liu, Bin Wang, Junyan Zhang, Yibo Yan, Aiwei Liu, Xuming Hu, Mingxun Zhou
- **Abstract**: Semantic-level watermarking (SWM) for large language models (LLMs) enhances watermarking robustness against text modifications and paraphrasing attacks by treating the sentence as the fundamental unit. However, existing methods still lack strong theoretical guarantees of robustness, and reject-sampling-based generation often introduces significant distribution distortions compared with unwatermarked outputs. In this work, we introduce a new theoretical framework on SWM through the concept of proxy functions (PFs) $\unicode{x2013}$ functions that map sentences to scalar values. Building on this framework, we propose PMark, a simple yet powerful SWM method that estimates the PF median for the next sentence dynamically through sampling while enforcing multiple PF constraints (which we call channels) to strengthen watermark evidence. Equipped with solid theoretical guarantees, PMark achieves the desired distortion-free property and improves the robustness against paraphrasing-style attacks. We also provide an empirically optimized version that further removes the requirement for dynamical median estimation for better sampling efficiency. Experimental results show that PMark consistently outperforms existing SWM baselines in both text quality and robustness, offering a more effective paradigm for detecting machine-generated text. Our code will be released at [this URL](https://github.com/PMark-repo/PMark).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PMARK, a novel approach to semantic-level watermarking (SWM) for large language models (LLMs).  PMARK aims to improve robustness against paraphrasing attacks and mitigate distribution distortions common in existing SWM methods. It proposes a theoretical framework based on proxy functions (PFs) which map sentences to scalar values.  PMARK dynamically estimates the PF median for the next sentence generation step, enforcing multiple PF constraints ("channels") to strengthen watermark evidence. The authors present both an online and offline version of PMARK, providing theoretical guarantees of distortion-freeness and demonstrating through experiments that PMARK outperforms existing SWM baselines in text quality and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Theoretical Framework:** The paper's primary strength lies in establishing a theoretical framework for analyzing SWMs using the concept of proxy functions. This provides a more rigorous foundation for understanding and evaluating different SWM methods.  It allows for a unified lens through which to view and compare existing techniques.
*   **Multi-Channel Constraints:** The idea of using multiple channel constraints to enhance watermark evidence is a significant step towards improving robustness against paraphrasing attacks. This addresses a key weakness of previous SWM schemes that relied on relatively sparse evidence.  The theoretical grounding on orthogonal vectors adds weight to this contribution.
*   **Distortion-Free Guarantees:** The paper claims and provides theoretical justification for a distortion-free property, which is a desirable feature that differentiates PMARK from biased token-level watermarking methods and some prior SWM schemes.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results demonstrate that PMARK consistently outperforms existing SWM baselines in both text quality (Perplexity) and robustness (TPR under paraphrase attacks).  The comparison is comprehensive, including both token-level and semantic-level methods.  The presentation of both online and offline versions provides practical options for different deployment scenarios.
*   **Well-Structured and Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and organized, making it relatively easy to follow the theoretical arguments and experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Median Estimation and Prior Assumption:** While the paper claims distortion-freeness, the practical implementation involves estimating the median PF dynamically. The reliance on this estimation, and the assumption of a zero median in the offline version, introduces potential approximation errors. The paper acknowledges this and leaves a strict theoretical error analysis for future work.  This undermines the strength of the distortion-free claim.
*   **Limited Novelty in Basic Components:**  While the overall architecture is novel, some underlying techniques such as the cosine similarity proxy function and using the QR decomposition for vectors may be seen as incremental in the machine learning literature. The novelty derives more from how these are combined and justified within the theoretical framework.
*   **Dataset and attack limitations:** While a reasonable range of attacks have been employed, testing on more sophisticated and context-aware adversarial techniques could demonstrate an increase in overall robustness of the model.
*   **Limited detail about baseline implementation and model comparability:** Not all parameter specifications have been noted for the models involved in this study.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper exhibits substantial novelty in its theoretical framework and the multi-channel constrained approach. While individual components may have been used before, the combination and justification within the context of SWM is novel.  The significance lies in providing a more principled approach to designing and analyzing SWM methods and in demonstrating improved performance in a challenging domain.

**Potential Influence:**

PMARK's theoretical framework could influence future research in SWM by providing a common language and set of tools for analysis. The multi-channel constrained approach could inspire new techniques for improving robustness. The distortion-free guarantees, even with the caveats, could motivate further research into methods that minimize distribution shifts.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 7.5. The theoretical framework and multi-channel approach are substantial contributions. However, the reliance on potentially noisy median estimation and approximations in the offline version, and limitation of current attack scope, somewhat temper the strength of the distortion-free claim. The lack of details in baselines is a bit of a weakness. While incremental in some aspects, the overall contribution is significant and likely to influence future work in the field of semantic-level watermarking.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Designing for Novice Debuggers: A Pilot Study on an AI-Assisted Debugging Tool
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21067v1)
- **Authors**: Oka Kurniawan, Erick Chandra, Christopher M. Poskitt, Yannic Noller, Kenny Tsu Wei Choo, Cyrille Jegourel
- **Abstract**: Debugging is a fundamental skill that novice programmers must develop. Numerous tools have been created to assist novice programmers in this process. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been integrated with automated program repair techniques to generate fixes for students' buggy code. However, many of these tools foster an over-reliance on AI and do not actively engage students in the debugging process. In this work, we aim to design an intuitive debugging assistant, CodeHinter, that combines traditional debugging tools with LLM-based techniques to help novice debuggers fix semantic errors while promoting active engagement in the debugging process. We present findings from our second design iteration, which we tested with a group of undergraduate students. Our results indicate that the students found the tool highly effective in resolving semantic errors and significantly easier to use than the first version. Consistent with our previous study, error localization was the most valuable feature. Finally, we conclude that any AI-assisted debugging tool should be personalized based on user profiles to optimize their interactions with students.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Designing for Novice Debuggers: A Pilot Study on an AI-Assisted Debugging Tool":

**Summary:**

The paper presents CodeHinter, an interactive debugging tool designed to help novice programmers overcome semantic errors. It combines traditional debugging tools with techniques powered by large language models (LLMs) within the Visual Studio Code IDE. Unlike tools that simply provide solutions, CodeHinter guides students through the debugging process by integrating fault localization with LLM-powered hints. A pilot study involving undergraduate students evaluates the tool's usability and effectiveness. The results show that students find CodeHinter useful, especially in resolving semantic errors and easier to use than an earlier version, particularly highlighting the value of error localization. The paper suggests personalizing AI-assisted debugging tools based on user profiles to optimize interactions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Focus on Active Learning:** The paper's primary strength lies in its emphasis on actively engaging novice programmers in the debugging process. It moves away from complete, auto-generated solutions towards a guided, step-by-step approach. This aligns with pedagogical principles that emphasize the importance of active learning for skill development.
    * **Integration of LLM and Traditional Techniques:** Combining LLM capabilities with established fault localization techniques like FauxPy is a sensible approach. It leverages the strengths of both: LLMs offer contextual hints and explanations, while fault localization provides a data-driven starting point, mitigating the risk of LLM hallucinations.
    * **Usability Evaluation:** The pilot study provides evidence for the tool's usability. The SUS scores and qualitative feedback suggest that students found CodeHinter effective and easy to use compared to a prior version of the tool. The data regarding frequency of feature use provides interesting insights on student preferences.
    * **Design Iteration:** The paper clearly describes how the current design of CodeHinter evolved from an earlier version (SID), highlighting the importance of iterative design and user feedback in tool development.
    * **Identifies Areas for Further Improvement:** The authors acknowledge limitations like the small sample size and offer concrete suggestions for future work, such as incorporating real-time assistance and code quality analysis.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Pilot Study:**  The sample size (n=10) is small, which limits the generalizability of the findings. It's also limited to one institution. A larger, multi-institutional study would strengthen the conclusions.
    * **Lack of Comparative Evaluation Against Baseline:** While comparison is made to an earlier version of the tool (SID), a comparison with a standard debugging environment (e.g., Visual Studio Code's debugger without CodeHinter) would add more value to demonstrate the tool's effectiveness.
    * **Limited Assessment of Learning Outcomes:** The study focuses primarily on usability and perceived helpfulness.  It doesn't directly assess how CodeHinter affects students' actual debugging skills or long-term learning. A more rigorous assessment would involve pre/post tests of debugging ability.
    * **Specificity of Task Domain:** The two LeetCode questions used may not be representative of the diverse range of errors encountered in introductory programming courses. It focuses on algorithmically oriented tasks, which might not directly address issues in other programming domains (e.g., GUI development or data analysis).
    * **Dependence on LLM Performance:** The tool's effectiveness hinges on the performance and reliability of the underlying LLM (GPT-4o).  The paper does not address scenarios where the LLM provides inaccurate or misleading hints.
    * **Limited information on what problems the participants could actually resolve.** The paper measures usability and perspectives, however it is unclear how the tool affects the ability for students to solve the programming tasks.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The novelty lies in the specific combination of LLM techniques with a user-guided debugging process that emphasizes active learning, coupled with concrete interface implementations.  While LLMs have been used in debugging tools, CodeHinter's design philosophy and user interface seem relatively unique.
    * The significance is moderate. The paper has the potential to influence the design of future AI-assisted debugging tools by advocating for a more active and engaging approach. However, the limited scope of the study and lack of strong learning outcome data dampen its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **6**.  The paper makes a valuable contribution by emphasizing the importance of active learning in AI-assisted debugging and by integrating LLM techniques in a user-friendly way.  The usability evaluation provides initial evidence for the tool's potential. However, the small sample size, lack of comparative baseline, and lack of rigorous learning outcome assessment significantly limit the impact of the study. Further research with a larger and more diverse sample, a comparative evaluation, and a focus on learning outcomes is needed to fully demonstrate the tool's effectiveness and solidify its contribution to the field. The work is a solid step but requires more rigorous evaluation to have substantial influence on the field of programming education.

### Normalizing Flows are Capable Visuomotor Policy Learning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21073v1)
- **Authors**: Simon Kristoffersson Lind, Jialong Li, Maj Stenmark, Volker Krüger
- **Abstract**: The field of general purpose robotics has recently embraced powerful probabilistic models, such as diffusion models, to model and learn complex behaviors. However, these models often come with significant trade-offs, namely high computational costs for inference and a fundamental inability to quantify output uncertainty. We argue that a model's trustworthiness, a critical factor for reliable, general-purpose robotics, is inherently linked to its ability to provide confidence measures. In this work, we introduce Normalizing Flows Policy, a novel visuomotor policy learning model based on Normalizing Flows. We show that Normalizing Flows are a natural and powerful alternative to diffusion models, providing both a statistically sound measure of confidence and a highly efficient inference process. Through comprehensive experiments across four distinct simulated robotic tasks, we demonstrate that Normalizing Flows Policy achieves performance comparable to, and often surpassing, Diffusion Policy, and it does so not only with improved sample efficiency but also with up to 30 times faster inference. Additionally, our ablation study validates several key architectural and training techniques that enable Normalizing Flows to perform well in this domain.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper "Normalizing Flows are Capable Visuomotor Policy Learning Models."

**Summary**

The paper introduces Normalizing Flows Policy (NF-P), a novel visuomotor policy learning model based on Normalizing Flows (NFs). It argues that NFs offer a compelling alternative to Diffusion Policies (DP) by providing a measure of output confidence, faster inference times, and competitive performance.  The authors perform experiments on four simulated robotic tasks within the RoboTwin 2.0 framework, demonstrating that NF-P achieves performance comparable to, and sometimes exceeding, DP, especially when trained on limited data. They validate key architectural and training techniques, including visual and action conditioning, action-sequence generation with stride, and likelihood optimization.  An ablation study explores the impact of various design choices on NF-P's performance. The core argument is that NFs are a practical and efficient solution for visuomotor policy learning, addressing some of the limitations of diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in applying NFs to the specific domain of visuomotor policy learning and explicitly contrasting them with the widely adopted diffusion model approach. While NFs themselves are not new, their application in this area, alongside specific architectural and training adaptations for robotic manipulation, adds a layer of novelty. The paper highlights the advantages of NFs (speed, confidence measures) over DPs, which is a useful contribution to the field. However, using NF for policy learning has been explored to some extent already, for example in [19], which the authors cite.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The experimental results demonstrate that NFs can achieve competitive performance with DPs, which is a notable achievement. The faster inference times of NFs are a significant advantage, particularly for real-time robotics applications. Furthermore, the investigation of key architectural and training techniques offers valuable insights for researchers working with NFs in this domain. The claim of providing confidence measures is promising, even though this aspect is not fully explored in the experiments provided in this paper. The success of NF-P, particularly in data-scarce environments, is also significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of DPs in terms of inference time and lack of confidence measures.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:**  The NF-P model is well-defined, and the implementation details are sufficient.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover a range of robotic tasks and varying amounts of training data, allowing for a thorough comparison with DPs.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into the importance of different design choices.
    *   **Faster Inference:** Significant speed improvements are achieved.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Confidence Measure Exploration:** The paper claims that NFs provide confidence measures, but the experimental validation does not explicitly demonstrate or utilize these confidence measures to improve policy performance or reliability.  This aspect is not thoroughly explored.  It is mentioned, but no experiments showcase the value of the confidence information provided by the model.

    *   **Simulated Environment:**  The experiments are conducted in a simulated environment, which raises questions about the transferability of the results to real-world robotic systems.

    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The gains in performance are not earth-shattering over DP, and in some cases, DP does better. While competitive performance with faster inference is good, this doesn't constitute a dramatic change for the field.
    *   **Lack of Novel Architectures** The neural spline flow used is known, and the structure in which it is embedded is rather basic, meaning the novelty is mostly in applying the NF in the described robotic context.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence researchers working on visuomotor policy learning by providing a practical alternative to DPs. The faster inference times and the potential for incorporating confidence measures could make NFs an attractive option for real-world robotic applications. However, the need for further validation in real-world settings and more detailed investigation of confidence measures limits the immediate impact.

**Justification for Score**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by demonstrating the feasibility and benefits of using NFs for visuomotor policy learning. The competitive performance, faster inference times, and potential for incorporating confidence measures make NFs a promising alternative to DPs. The strengths of the paper are that it clearly establishes the problem and defines the proposed solution. However, the limited exploration of confidence measures, the simulated environment, and the incremental nature of the performance gains prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution. Also, using NF for policy learning has been explored before. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate novelty, practical significance, and potential impact on the field, while acknowledging its limitations and incremental contributions.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### RePro: Leveraging Large Language Models for Semi-Automated Reproduction of Networking Research Results
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21074v1)
- **Authors**: Yining Jiang, Wenyun Xu, Qingyu Song, Yuling Lin, Xuanhao Liu, Xiaoqiang Zheng, Qiang Su, Lizhao You, Lu Tang, Wangjian Feng, Linghe Kong, Qiao Xiang, Jiwu Shu
- **Abstract**: Reproducing networking research is a critical but challenging task due to the scarcity of open-source code. While Large Language Models (LLMs) can automate code generation, current approaches lack the generalizability required for the diverse networking field. To address this, we propose RePro, a semi-automated reproduction framework that leverages advanced prompt engineering to reproduce network systems from their research papers. RePro combines few-shot in-context learning with Structured and Semantic Chain of Thought (SCoT/SeCoT) techniques to systematically translate a paper's description into an optimized, executable implementation. The framework operates through a three-stage pipeline: system description extraction, structural code generation, and code optimization. Our evaluation with five state-of-the-art LLMs across diverse network sub-domains demonstrates that RePro significantly reduces reproduction time compared to manual efforts while achieving comparable system performance, validating its effectiveness and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RePro: Leveraging Large Language Models for Semi-Automated Reproduction of Networking Research Results":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RePro, a semi-automated framework that utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) to reproduce networking research results from academic papers. RePro aims to address the challenge of limited open-source code availability in networking research by automating the process of re-implementing network systems based on paper descriptions. It employs a three-stage pipeline: (1) system description extraction, (2) structural code generation, and (3) code optimization. RePro uses prompt engineering techniques like few-shot in-context learning with Structured and Semantic Chain of Thought (SCOT/SeCoT) to translate paper descriptions into executable code. The paper demonstrates the framework's effectiveness and efficiency by evaluating it with different LLMs on various network sub-domains, showing significant reduction in reproduction time compared to manual efforts while achieving comparable system performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** Reproducibility in networking research is a well-known bottleneck. The scarcity of open-source code and the complexity of network systems make it difficult for researchers to verify and build upon existing work.
    *   **Novel approach:** The application of LLMs, particularly with structured prompting techniques like SCOT/SeCoT, to automate the reproduction process is a promising and novel approach.
    *   **Comprehensive framework:** RePro provides a structured workflow that covers essential steps from paper analysis to code generation and optimization, offering a practical solution to a complex problem.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The evaluation using different LLMs and diverse network systems demonstrates the effectiveness and generalizability of the framework. The results showing a significant reduction in reproduction time and comparable system performance are compelling.
    *   **Rigorous Error Analysis:** The error analysis highlighting semantic vs. syntactic errors, and the relative time and prompt counts is valuable for understanding the bottlenecks and limitations of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Semi-automated nature:** RePro requires manual interaction, especially for exception handling and error correction. This limits the overall automation and can still require significant expertise from the user.
    *   **Reliance on paper quality:** The framework's performance depends heavily on the clarity and completeness of the original research paper. Ambiguous or incomplete descriptions in the paper can hinder the accuracy and efficiency of the reproduction process.
    *   **Generalizability challenges:** While evaluated on diverse network sub-domains, the framework's generalizability to highly complex or novel network architectures might be limited. Some level of domain expertise is likely still needed to validate code produced.
    *   **Lack of open-source implementation:** A key weakness is that the current prototype of RePro is not open-source. This makes it difficult for other researchers to independently verify the results and build upon the work.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** While 9 systems were used for evaluation, further detailed analysis on a wider range of systems would further solidify the contributions made by this framework.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper introduces a novel application of LLMs for automating the reproduction of networking research, which can have a significant impact on the field by improving reproducibility and accelerating research progress. The integration of structured prompt engineering techniques like SCOT/SeCoT with LLMs is also a notable contribution.
    *   However, the semi-automated nature and reliance on paper quality limit the framework's overall impact. Furthermore, the lack of publicly available source code restricts wider adoption and independent verification.
    *   The paper also identifies key bottlenecks in applying LLMs to networking, particularly error handling and semantic understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   RePro can inspire future research on developing more automated and robust tools for reproducing networking research, potentially leading to a more collaborative and transparent research environment.
    *   The framework's structured prompt engineering techniques can be adopted and extended by other researchers working on code generation and automation in various domains.

*   **Score Rationale:**

    *The paper demonstrates a practical solution to a pertinent issue using modern LLM tools and techniques. While it isn't perfect, its structure, analysis, and demonstration are very impressive for a single paper. It has solid potential to inspire related work.*

Score: 7.5
