### Communication Bias in Large Language Models: A Regulatory Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21075v1)
- **Authors**: Adrian Kuenzler, Stefan Schmid
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly central to many applications, raising concerns about bias, fairness, and regulatory compliance. This paper reviews risks of biased outputs and their societal impact, focusing on frameworks like the EU's AI Act and the Digital Services Act. We argue that beyond constant regulation, stronger attention to competition and design governance is needed to ensure fair, trustworthy AI. This is a preprint of the Communications of the ACM article of the same title.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Communication Bias in Large Language Models: A Regulatory Perspective" explores the risks associated with communication bias in Large Language Models (LLMs) and how emerging regulatory frameworks, specifically the EU's AI Act and Digital Services Act (DSA), attempt to address these challenges. It argues that LLMs, functioning as primary interfaces between users and AI, can subtly influence opinions and potentially exacerbate societal polarization. While current regulations address AI-generated content, they often overlook the fundamental impact LLMs might have on worldviews and voting decisions. The paper advocates for a comprehensive approach that combines value chain regulation, content moderation, competition, and technology design governance to foster fair and trustworthy AI systems. It analyzes the limitations of current legal frameworks, especially the AI Act and DSA, and suggests promoting competition and self-governance to ensure diversity and representativity in technology design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper makes a timely and relevant contribution by highlighting the often-overlooked risk of communication bias in LLMs. Its focus on the regulatory landscape, particularly the EU's AI Act and DSA, is practical and provides a tangible context for the discussion. The paper effectively articulates how LLMs' role as primary interfaces amplifies their potential influence on user opinions. The call for a multifaceted approach involving value chain regulation, content moderation, competition, and technology design governance is a strong recommendation. Furthermore, this paper draws from a variety of disciplines, including law and computer science, to produce its conclusions.

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core argument, the potential for LLMs to subtly influence worldviews and voting decisions, is a significant insight and somewhat novel, however there exist prior works which have documented these biases. The emphasis on competition and self-governance as complementary to existing regulations is somewhat less explored.

*   **Significance:** This paper contributes to ongoing discussions about AI ethics, regulation, and governance. Its practical focus on how emerging regulatory frameworks can address communication bias in LLMs makes it valuable for policymakers and AI developers. The discussion on the limitations of current legal frameworks is particularly helpful. The discussion of "moral hazard" is a valuable contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper does not offer a concrete technical solutions to the issues discussed, instead primarily focuses on the broader regulatory landscape. While the paper advocates for competition, the strategies for fostering competition in the AI market could be more thoroughly developed. The paper could include more discussion of existing work in bias detection and mitigation in LLMs, and how these can be integrated with the proposed governance frameworks.

*   **Overall:** The paper effectively bridges the gap between legal and technical discussions surrounding LLMs. While it would benefit from more detailed technical solutions and a deeper exploration of competition strategies, it makes a timely and important contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**
**Rationale:** The paper is novel and presents an important aspect of LLM regulation, the risk of communication bias. The policy recommendations are sound. However, the paper doesn't include substantial technical analysis, the competition strategies could be more detailed, and the coverage of existing bias detection methods is limited, and the content relies heavily on the EU context. The moral hazard framing provides significant value.
- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### SoM-1K: A Thousand-Problem Benchmark Dataset for Strength of Materials
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21079v1)
- **Authors**: Qixin Wan, Zilong Wang, Jingwen Zhou, Wanting Wang, Ziheng Geng, Jiachen Liu, Ran Cao, Minghui Cheng, Lu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Foundation models have shown remarkable capabilities in various domains, but their performance on complex, multimodal engineering problems remains largely unexplored. We introduce SoM-1K, the first large-scale multimodal benchmark dataset dedicated to evaluating foundation models on problems in the strength of materials (SoM). The dataset, which contains 1,065 annotated SoM problems, mirrors real-world engineering tasks by including both textual problem statements and schematic diagrams. Due to the limited capabilities of current foundation models in understanding complicated visual information, we propose a novel prompting strategy called Descriptions of Images (DoI), which provides rigorous expert-generated text descriptions of the visual diagrams as the context. We evaluate eight representative foundation models, including both large language models (LLMs) and vision language models (VLMs). Our results show that current foundation models struggle significantly with these engineering problems, with the best-performing model achieving only 56.6% accuracy. Interestingly, we found that LLMs, when provided with DoI, often outperform VLMs provided with visual diagrams. A detailed error analysis reveals that DoI plays a crucial role in mitigating visual misinterpretation errors, suggesting that accurate text-based descriptions can be more effective than direct image input for current foundation models. This work establishes a rigorous benchmark for engineering AI and highlights a critical need for developing more robust multimodal reasoning capabilities in foundation models, particularly in scientific and engineering contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SoM-1K: A Thousand-Problem Benchmark Dataset for Strength of Materials":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SoM-1K, a new multimodal benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the performance of foundation models (LLMs and VLMs) on problems related to the strength of materials (SoM). The dataset contains over a thousand annotated problems, each consisting of a textual problem statement, a schematic diagram, and the corresponding ground truth solution. Recognizing the limitations of current VLMs in understanding engineering diagrams, the authors propose a novel prompting strategy called Descriptions of Images (DoI), which provides expert-generated text descriptions of the visual diagrams. The paper evaluates eight representative foundation models using different prompting strategies, highlighting their struggles with these engineering problems and demonstrating the effectiveness of the DoI strategy in mitigating visual misinterpretation errors. The authors conclude by emphasizing the need for more robust multimodal reasoning capabilities in foundation models, particularly in scientific and engineering contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of SoM-1K is a significant contribution in itself. There is a clear lack of publicly available, large-scale, multimodal datasets tailored to engineering domains. The inclusion of both textual problem descriptions and schematic diagrams, along with expert-validated DoI, significantly enhances the dataset's value for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of foundation models. The proposed DoI prompting strategy is also a novel approach to address the limitations of current VLMs in understanding visual engineering information.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important gap in the evaluation of AI models for engineering applications. The results highlight the significant challenges faced by existing foundation models in solving SoM problems, despite their impressive performance in other domains. This underscores the importance of developing more specialized and robust AI systems that can effectively integrate textual and visual information for complex engineering tasks. The finding that text-based descriptions (DoI) often outperform direct image input for current foundation models is a noteworthy insight, suggesting a potential direction for improving the visual reasoning capabilities of these models. The development of a benchmark tailored to a practical engineering domain could stimulate the development of models that are more applicable in real-world scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Contribution:**  The SoM-1K dataset provides a valuable resource for researchers working on AI for engineering.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The DoI strategy is well-motivated and demonstrably improves performance.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes a diverse set of foundation models and prompting strategies.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The detailed error analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of current models and areas for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Performance:** The relatively low accuracy achieved by even the best-performing models (56.6%) suggests that the current foundation models are still far from being able to reliably solve SoM problems. While this highlights the challenges, it might also raise questions about the complexity or inherent ambiguity of the problems in the dataset.
    *   **DoI limitations:** While DoI improves results, it is also limited in the sense that the manual effort to generate these prompts does not scale to more dynamic environments where, for example, the diagrams themselves might need to be created by the model.
    *   **Limited scope:**  The benchmark is limited to a single domain (SoM). While this allows for in-depth analysis, it may not generalize well to other engineering disciplines.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SoM-1K dataset and the associated findings are likely to stimulate further research in AI for engineering, particularly in the development of more robust multimodal reasoning capabilities and specialized models for engineering domains. The work can also serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated educational tools and AI assistants for engineers.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by providing a new, domain-specific benchmark for evaluating foundation models in engineering. The SoM-1K dataset and the DoI prompting strategy address an important gap in the evaluation of AI for engineering applications.  The paper highlights real limitations of current VLMs in practical contexts and proposes a reasonable technique for improving results. However, the scope is still limited to a specific domain and the model performance results expose the gap that still remains. Thus, the paper represents a significant and novel contribution but with ample room for improvement and future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Which Cultural Lens Do Models Adopt? On Cultural Positioning Bias and Agentic Mitigation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21080v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Wan, Xingrun Chen, Kai-Wei Chang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have unlocked a wide range of downstream generative applications. However, we found that they also risk perpetuating subtle fairness issues tied to culture, positioning their generations from the perspectives of the mainstream US culture while demonstrating salient externality towards non-mainstream ones. In this work, we identify and systematically investigate this novel culture positioning bias, in which an LLM's default generative stance aligns with a mainstream view and treats other cultures as outsiders. We propose the CultureLens benchmark with 4000 generation prompts and 3 evaluation metrics for quantifying this bias through the lens of a culturally situated interview script generation task, in which an LLM is positioned as an onsite reporter interviewing local people across 10 diverse cultures. Empirical evaluation on 5 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a stark pattern: while models adopt insider tones in over 88 percent of US-contexted scripts on average, they disproportionately adopt mainly outsider stances for less dominant cultures. To resolve these biases, we propose 2 inference-time mitigation methods: a baseline prompt-based Fairness Intervention Pillars (FIP) method, and a structured Mitigation via Fairness Agents (MFA) framework consisting of 2 pipelines: (1) MFA-SA (Single-Agent) introduces a self-reflection and rewriting loop based on fairness guidelines. (2) MFA-MA (Multi-Agent) structures the process into a hierarchy of specialized agents: a Planner Agent(initial script generation), a Critique Agent (evaluates initial script against fairness pillars), and a Refinement Agent (incorporates feedback to produce a polished, unbiased script). Empirical results showcase the effectiveness of agent-based methods as a promising direction for mitigating biases in generative LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates a novel "culture positioning bias" in large language models (LLMs).  This bias occurs when LLMs, acting as reporters generating interview scripts, adopt an "insider" perspective for dominant cultures (particularly US culture) but default to an "outsider" perspective when interviewing about less dominant cultures.  The authors introduce the CULTURELENS benchmark with 4,000 prompts across 10 diverse cultures and propose metrics (CEP, CPD, CAG) to quantify this bias. Empirical evaluation reveals a significant bias. To mitigate this, they propose two inference-time methods: a prompt-based Fairness Intervention Pillars (FIP) and a more structured Mitigation via Fairness Agents (MFA) framework, consisting of single-agent (MFA-SA) and multi-agent (MFA-MA) pipelines.  Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the MFA methods, especially the multi-agent approach, in reducing the identified cultural positioning bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of "culture positioning bias" is a valuable contribution.  While prior work has addressed cultural bias in LLMs, this paper hones in on the implicit viewpoint adopted by the model, which is a subtler and arguably more insidious form of bias than explicit stereotypes. The CULTURELENS benchmark also represents a useful resource for evaluating this specific type of bias.

*   **Significance:**  The findings highlight a critical issue concerning cultural hegemony in LLMs.  As LLMs are increasingly used for content generation and interaction across diverse user bases, this bias can perpetuate and amplify existing cultural power imbalances. The proposed mitigation strategies, particularly the agent-based approaches, provide a promising direction for addressing this challenge.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly defines and frames the culture positioning bias.
    *   **Systematic evaluation:**  The CULTURELENS benchmark provides a structured and comprehensive way to evaluate this bias.
    *   **Rigorous experimentation:** The paper includes thorough empirical evaluations of several state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   **Effective mitigation strategies:** The agent-based MFA methods demonstrate significant improvements in reducing the identified bias.
    *   **Clear writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, despite the complexity of the topic.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope of cultures:** While 10 cultures provide good diversity, the paper might benefit from including even more, with a more elaborate justification on how these cultures were selected based on metrics beyond general global continental location.
    *   **Simplified insider/outsider classification:** The automated classification of "insider" vs. "outsider" perspectives, while using an LLM judge, simplifies a complex phenomenon. It would strengthen the paper to discuss the limitations of this binary classification and explore potential nuances within these categories.  There may be degrees of insider/outsider perspective, and an LLM might simultaneously exhibit both.
    *   **Reliance on US-centric view as baseline:** While the authors acknowledge and address the models' preference for the US, they still use the US context as a baseline for calculating the CAG metric. This might inherently skew the results towards identifying US-centric biases more readily than biases aligned with other cultures.
    *   **Limited Analysis on failure cases:** There could be more detailed qualitative analyses on the circumstances where the agentic methods failed, providing insights into their limitations.
    *   **Computational resources:** There's a caveat about limited experiments due to computational restrictions. This impacts the mitigation results.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Raising awareness of the culture positioning bias in LLMs.
    *   Providing a benchmark and metrics for evaluating this bias.
    *   Inspiring further research into agent-based mitigation strategies for cultural bias.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution, clearly identifying and systematically investigating a subtle but important form of cultural bias in LLMs. The CULTURELENS benchmark and the agent-based mitigation strategies offer valuable tools for addressing this issue. However, the simplifying assumptions in classifying perspectives, the reliance on US-centric baseline, limitations due to computational resources and the need for more granular qualitative analysis, prevent it from achieving the highest impact.

### Vision Transformers: the threat of realistic adversarial patches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21084v1)
- **Authors**: Kasper Cools, Clara Maathuis, Alexander M. van Oers, Claudia S. Hübner, Nikos Deligiannis, Marijke Vandewal, Geert De Cubber
- **Abstract**: The increasing reliance on machine learning systems has made their security a critical concern. Evasion attacks enable adversaries to manipulate the decision-making processes of AI systems, potentially causing security breaches or misclassification of targets. Vision Transformers (ViTs) have gained significant traction in modern machine learning due to increased 1) performance compared to Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and 2) robustness against adversarial perturbations. However, ViTs remain vulnerable to evasion attacks, particularly to adversarial patches, unique patterns designed to manipulate AI classification systems. These vulnerabilities are investigated by designing realistic adversarial patches to cause misclassification in person vs. non-person classification tasks using the Creases Transformation (CT) technique, which adds subtle geometric distortions similar to those occurring naturally when wearing clothing. This study investigates the transferability of adversarial attack techniques used in CNNs when applied to ViT classification models. Experimental evaluation across four fine-tuned ViT models on a binary person classification task reveals significant vulnerability variations: attack success rates ranged from 40.04% (google/vit-base-patch16-224-in21k) to 99.97% (facebook/dino-vitb16), with google/vit-base-patch16-224 achieving 66.40% and facebook/dinov3-vitb16 reaching 65.17%. These results confirm the cross-architectural transferability of adversarial patches from CNNs to ViTs, with pre-training dataset scale and methodology strongly influencing model resilience to adversarial attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the vulnerability of Vision Transformers (ViTs) to realistic adversarial patches, specifically focusing on person vs. non-person classification tasks. It adapts an existing method used for CNNs (the Dynamic Adversarial Patches framework with Creases Transformation) to generate patches that can cause ViTs to misclassify images. The study examines the transferability of adversarial attack techniques from CNNs to ViTs by fine-tuning different ViT models and evaluating their attack success rates. The results demonstrate that ViTs are indeed vulnerable to such attacks, with significant variations in resilience depending on the pre-training methodology and the scale of the pre-training dataset. The paper highlights the need for robust defense mechanisms tailored to ViTs in security-sensitive applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a moderate level of novelty. While the concept of adversarial patches is well-established, the application and adaptation of the Creases Transformation technique to ViTs for generating *realistic* adversarial patches adds a tangible value. Also, the evaluation of cross-architectural transferability (from CNN-targeted patches to ViTs) with a specific focus on person detection and realistic deformations is a relatively new area of investigation. The investigation into how pre-training strategies and dataset size affects ViT model robustness is also valuable. Prior work on ViT security has largely focused on more abstract attacks or less realistic perturbations, while this paper grounds the research in a tangible security scenario.  However, the core DAP framework is existing, and the changes may be not significant enough.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its practical implications for security-sensitive applications like border control and law enforcement. Demonstrating the vulnerability of ViTs, which are increasingly deployed in these areas, to realistic, physically realizable attacks is an important contribution. By highlighting the varying levels of resilience among different ViT architectures based on their pre-training, the paper provides valuable insights for selecting more robust models in such deployments. The paper also contributes by developing a reproducible benchmark, which should assist in creating better defenses. The observation that large pre-training datasets improve adversarial robustness is not new, but reiterating it in the context of ViTs and this specific attack scenario strengthens the argument for that methodology.

**Strengths:**

*   **Realistic Attack Scenario:** The use of the Creases Transformation to generate patches that appear physically plausible and are robust to natural deformations is a significant strength.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study evaluates multiple ViT models with different pre-training strategies, providing a broad perspective on vulnerability.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper presents a well-defined methodology, including details on dataset composition, fine-tuning procedures, and patch generation.
*   **Reproducibility:** The inclusion of random seed 2 for initialisation enhances the reproducibility.
*   **Practical Implications:** The discussion of implications for real-world security deployments adds to the paper's value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Adaptation:** The adaptation of the DAP framework from CNNs to ViTs is relatively incremental. While necessary modifications were made, the core methodology remains the same.
*   **Limited Defense Evaluation:** The paper focuses solely on attacking ViTs and does not investigate potential defense mechanisms specifically tailored to these architectures.
*   **Dataset Limitation:** While the custom dataset addresses the need for image-level labels, it could benefit from greater diversity or real-world data.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** There are no in-depth ablation studies to test the effects of the different loss functions, the number of creases or other key components.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in the following ways:

*   **Stimulating Development of ViT-Specific Defenses:** The demonstrated vulnerability of ViTs could spur the development of more robust defense mechanisms tailored to their unique architectures.
*   **Informing Model Selection:** The findings on the impact of pre-training strategies could guide the selection of ViT models for security-sensitive applications.
*   **Providing a Benchmark:** The reproducible benchmark could be used to evaluate the effectiveness of future defense strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, this is a solid paper which provides an important security perspective within an increasingly important field in AI/ML. It demonstrates the relative vulnerabilities of ViTs while creating a method that can be used to further develop countermeasures. However, the relative lack of novelty in the techniques, and the lack of investigation into countermeasures constrains a higher scoring.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### UniTransfer: Video Concept Transfer via Progressive Spatial and Timestep Decomposition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21086v1)
- **Authors**: Guojun Lei, Rong Zhang, Chi Wang, Tianhang Liu, Hong Li, Zhiyuan Ma, Weiwei Xu
- **Abstract**: We propose a novel architecture UniTransfer, which introduces both spatial and diffusion timestep decomposition in a progressive paradigm, achieving precise and controllable video concept transfer. Specifically, in terms of spatial decomposition, we decouple videos into three key components: the foreground subject, the background, and the motion flow. Building upon this decomposed formulation, we further introduce a dual-to-single-stream DiT-based architecture for supporting fine-grained control over different components in the videos. We also introduce a self-supervised pretraining strategy based on random masking to enhance the decomposed representation learning from large-scale unlabeled video data. Inspired by the Chain-of-Thought reasoning paradigm, we further revisit the denoising diffusion process and propose a Chain-of-Prompt (CoP) mechanism to achieve the timestep decomposition. We decompose the denoising process into three stages of different granularity and leverage large language models (LLMs) for stage-specific instructions to guide the generation progressively. We also curate an animal-centric video dataset called OpenAnimal to facilitate the advancement and benchmarking of research in video concept transfer. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves high-quality and controllable video concept transfer across diverse reference images and scenes, surpassing existing baselines in both visual fidelity and editability. Web Page: https://yu-shaonian.github.io/UniTransfer-Web/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UniTransfer paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniTransfer, a novel video concept transfer framework that leverages a progressive spatial and timestep decomposition strategy. Instead of treating video as a monolithic entity, UniTransfer explicitly disentangles it into foreground, background, and motion components in the spatial dimension. In the timestep dimension, it decomposes the denoising process into coarse, medium, and fine-grained stages. This allows for more fine-grained control during video synthesis, enabling tasks like character, motion, background, and garment transfer. The method also includes a self-supervised pretraining scheme based on random masking for more robust learning. Furthermore, a Chain-of-Prompt mechanism uses LLMs to generate hierarchical prompts at different timesteps to guide the diffusion process.  The authors also contribute an animal-centric dataset called OpenAnimal. Experiments demonstrate that the method outperforms existing baselines in visual quality, consistency, and controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to video concept transfer by combining spatial and timestep decomposition.  The simultaneous spatial disentanglement (foreground/background/motion) *and* the adaptive, LLM-guided denoising process represent a significant advance over methods that treat video as a whole or only address one aspect. The Chain-of-Prompt mechanism is an innovative application of Chain-of-Thought reasoning to diffusion models.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The paper provides a clear description of the architecture and training process, including the rationale behind various design choices. The use of a diffusion transformer (DiT) provides a strong foundation for generating high-quality videos. The self-supervised pretraining and the dual-to-single-stream architecture is designed in an elaborate manner.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The extensive experiments on various transfer tasks (character, motion, background, garment) provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of the method. The quantitative comparisons against state-of-the-art baselines using metrics like FID, LPIPS, and visual consistency metrics further strengthen the results. The creation of the OpenAnimal dataset also is a useful contribution.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and well-organized. The figures and tables are helpful in understanding the method and the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The framework is complex, involving multiple components (DiT, LLM, pretraining, mask generation). This complexity might make it challenging to implement and reproduce the results without significant effort. This could impede adoption and further research built upon this work.
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The method relies on LLMs for prompt generation. The performance could be sensitive to the choice of LLM and the quality of its generated prompts. LLMs are also expensive to run, which makes it not very friendly.
    *  **Limited failure analysis:**  While the paper shows impressive results, there's a limited discussion of failure cases. It would be valuable to see a detailed analysis of the types of concepts or videos for which UniTransfer struggles. The limitations of imperfect segmentation were discussed, but not explored in depth.
   *  **Societal Impact Discussion Vague:** The paper makes the point that the area of video manipulation will cause social issues, and the responsibility of the researchers is to bring awareness, but this discussion lacks specifics in the regulatory frameworks.

*   **Significance:**
The UniTransfer paper contributes to video editing. This technology will enhance the efficiency of video production, drive innovation in industries such as film and gaming, and enable more immersive entertainment and educational experiences.
However, as this technology becomes more widespread, society may face ethical and legal challenges, including concerns over the authenticity of video content, characters, and backgrounds. Therefore, establishing appropriate regulatory frameworks to ensure responsible use of this technology is a critical task that demands our attention.
   *  **Potential impact:** The presented is novel and might inspire future research directions in controllable video generation and editing. The OpenAnimal dataset could become a useful resource for the community.

**Justification for the Score:**

UniTransfer makes a good contribution to the field of video concept transfer by introducing a novel and effective approach to disentangling and manipulating video content. The proposed progressive spatial and timestep decomposition combined with LLM guidance provides a strong technical foundation for controllable video generation. The OpenAnimal dataset and well-designed experiments further support the paper's claims.
Despite its strengths, the method suffers from complexity and dependence on LLMs.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Are Modern Speech Enhancement Systems Vulnerable to Adversarial Attacks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21087v1)
- **Authors**: Rostislav Makarov, Lea Schönherr, Timo Gerkmann
- **Abstract**: Machine learning approaches for speech enhancement are becoming increasingly expressive, enabling ever more powerful modifications of input signals. In this paper, we demonstrate that this expressiveness introduces a vulnerability: advanced speech enhancement models can be susceptible to adversarial attacks. Specifically, we show that adversarial noise, carefully crafted and psychoacoustically masked by the original input, can be injected such that the enhanced speech output conveys an entirely different semantic meaning. We experimentally verify that contemporary predictive speech enhancement models can indeed be manipulated in this way. Furthermore, we highlight that diffusion models with stochastic samplers exhibit inherent robustness to such adversarial attacks by design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of modern speech enhancement (SE) systems to adversarial attacks.  It demonstrates that carefully crafted adversarial noise, masked psychoacoustically by the original input, can be injected into the noisy speech to cause the enhanced output to convey an entirely different semantic meaning. The authors experiment with predictive SE models (direct mapping, complex ratio masking) and generative (diffusion) SE models.  They find that predictive models are more susceptible to these attacks, while diffusion models, particularly with stochastic sampling, exhibit inherent robustness. The study uses an attack loss function incorporating psychoacoustic constraints and evaluates performance based on attack success metrics (DistillMOS, WER, ESTOI, POLQA) and perturbation impact metrics (POLQA, ESTOI, SNR).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of adversarial attacks on *regression-based* speech enhancement systems, with the specific goal of manipulating the *semantic meaning* of the enhanced speech, appears to be novel. Prior work primarily focused on attacking ASR systems or evaluating the robustness of speech enhancement to adversarial noise in the context of maintaining intelligibility and quality. The application to diffusion-based SE and the analysis of the impact of stochasticity in the sampling process add another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** This work highlights a potential security vulnerability in increasingly sophisticated speech enhancement systems, particularly those used in hearing aids or telephony devices where manipulated output could have severe consequences.  The findings raise concerns about the trustworthiness of machine-learning-based speech communication tools.  The analysis of diffusion models and their inherent robustness is also significant, as these models are becoming increasingly popular.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and presents a clear problem statement and methodology.
    *   The experimental design is sound, comparing different types of SE models and analyzing the impact of various parameters (e.g., masking tolerance, l2 budget).
    *   The use of both attack success and perturbation impact metrics provides a comprehensive evaluation.
    *   The analysis of the diffusion models and the effect of stochasticity is a valuable contribution.
    *   The psychoacoustic masking approach is well-integrated into the attack methodology, which allows for better imperceptibility of the attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The white-box attack scenario, while a common starting point, may not fully reflect real-world threats where adversaries might have limited knowledge of the system.
    *   The reliance on the EARS-WHAM-v2 dataset, while standard, might limit the generalizability of the results to other types of noise environments or speech characteristics.
    *   While the paper explores different parameter settings, a more in-depth analysis of the specific types of semantic manipulations that are achievable and their impact on human perception would be beneficial. The adversarial target is simply another utterance. Is it possible to craft adversarial noise that changes "yes" to "no", for example? A semantic evaluation of this type would make the work even more compelling.
    *   The study could benefit from investigating defense strategies against such adversarial attacks.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research on the security and robustness of speech enhancement systems.  It may lead to the development of more robust SE algorithms and defense mechanisms against adversarial attacks.  It also raises awareness of potential vulnerabilities in ML-based speech communication systems among researchers and practitioners.

* **Critique on Experiment setup**: A potential concern about the experimental setup is the sampling approach for building *Yuser* and the *Sattacker* target. Random pairing of noisy mixtures with clean utterances might skew the result: some of the pairings might lead to more successful or unsuccessful attacks than other combinations. Also, the paper lacks a comparison against a non-targeted approach.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents novel research on a relevant and timely topic. The application of adversarial attacks to speech enhancement is relatively unexplored, and the findings highlight a potential vulnerability in modern systems. The paper provides a strong experimental analysis and contributes to our understanding of the robustness of different SE models. However, some limitations exist regarding the real-world attack scenarios considered, and the depth of semantic manipulation analysis. Despite these limitations, the research is valuable and has the potential to influence future work in this area.

### Best-of-$\infty$ -- Asymptotic Performance of Test-Time Compute
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21091v1)
- **Authors**: Junpei Komiyama, Daisuke Oba, Masafumi Oyamada
- **Abstract**: We study best-of-$N$ for large language models (LLMs) where the selection is based on majority voting. In particular, we analyze the limit $N \to \infty$, which we denote as Best-of-$\infty$. While this approach achieves impressive performance in the limit, it requires an infinite test-time budget. To address this, we propose an adaptive generation scheme that selects $N$ based on answer agreement, thereby efficiently allocating inference-time computation. Beyond adaptivity, we extend the framework to weighted ensembles of multiple LLMs, showing that such mixtures can outperform any individual model. The optimal ensemble weighting is formulated and efficiently computed as a mixed-integer linear program. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the "best-of-N" approach for large language models (LLMs), focusing on majority voting as the selection criterion.  It explores the theoretical limit as N approaches infinity ("best-of-∞") and proposes an adaptive generation scheme that dynamically adjusts N based on the answer agreement and a Bayesian confidence measure. The paper also extends this framework to weighted ensembles of multiple LLMs, demonstrating that such mixtures can outperform individual models, with optimal weights being found through a mixed-integer linear program.  Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and reasoning datasets are conducted to validate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper's exploration of the "best-of-∞" concept provides a new perspective on understanding the potential of the best-of-N approach. The adaptive sampling strategy based on Bayesian confidence is a practical and principled way to manage test-time compute, moving beyond fixed N approaches. The formulation of LLM ensemble weighting as a mixed-integer linear program is also a significant contribution, allowing for optimal combination of models.
    *   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and crucial problem in LLM inference: efficiently allocating computational resources to maximize performance. The proposed adaptive sampling and ensemble weighting techniques offer promising solutions for improving the reliability of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks. The experimental results are comprehensive and convincing, demonstrating substantial improvements over baseline methods.
    *   **Rigor:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis to support the adaptive sampling strategy. The method is well-motivated and described in detail. The evaluation setup is rigorous, including a variety of LLMs and datasets, and a substantial amount of computation.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors make their large-scale generation datasets and code public, improving the reproducibility and furthering the research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Theoretical Depth:** While the paper provides some theoretical analysis, further investigation into the properties of the "best-of-∞" limit and the Bayesian confidence measure could be beneficial. Perhaps analyzing the approximation errors incurred when working with finite samples in the Dirichlet process might be illuminating.
    *   **Generality of MILP:** The formulation of the MILP depends on access to the log probabilities of each model (or some reasonable estimates), which might not be available for all LLMs (e.g., closed-source models).
    *   **Complexity of the MILP optimization:** MILP can scale poorly with the number of constraints (here, problems). In practice, it appears to perform adequately, but there might be cases where the optimization is a bottleneck.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper delivers a valuable combination of theoretical insight and practical techniques for improving LLM inference. The adaptive sampling approach is a clever way to approximate the "best-of-∞" ideal with a finite computational budget. The ensemble method provides a systematic way to combine LLMs, enabling a performance improvement over individual models.

The paper's impact may be limited by its reliance on specific LLM architectures for MILP and the computational cost of the overall approach. However, the adaptive sampling strategy is generalizable.

Taking into account both the strengths and weaknesses, a **rigorous and critical appraisal** lead to:

Score: 7.

This score reflects the paper's strong contributions to the field while acknowledging some potential limitations regarding generality and scalability of the proposed methods. The paper provides a well-founded method that moves beyond traditional best-of-N approaches.
- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### GraphUniverse: Enabling Systematic Evaluation of Inductive Generalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21097v1)
- **Authors**: Louis Van Langendonck, Guillermo Bernárdez, Nina Miolane, Pere Barlet-Ros
- **Abstract**: A fundamental challenge in graph learning is understanding how models generalize to new, unseen graphs. While synthetic benchmarks offer controlled settings for analysis, existing approaches are confined to single-graph, transductive settings where models train and test on the same graph structure. Addressing this gap, we introduce GraphUniverse, a framework for generating entire families of graphs to enable the first systematic evaluation of inductive generalization at scale. Our core innovation is the generation of graphs with persistent semantic communities, ensuring conceptual consistency while allowing fine-grained control over structural properties like homophily and degree distributions. This enables crucial but underexplored robustness tests, such as performance under controlled distribution shifts. Benchmarking a wide range of architectures -- from GNNs to graph transformers and topological architectures -- reveals that strong transductive performance is a poor predictor of inductive generalization. Furthermore, we find that robustness to distribution shift is highly sensitive not only to model architecture choice but also to the initial graph regime (e.g., high vs. low homophily). Beyond benchmarking, GraphUniverse's flexibility and scalability can facilitate the development of robust and truly generalizable architectures -- including next-generation graph foundation models. An interactive demo is available at https://graphuniverse.streamlit.app.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces GraphUniverse, a framework for generating families of graphs with controllable properties to systematically evaluate inductive generalization in graph learning. Unlike existing synthetic benchmarks limited to single-graph, transductive settings, GraphUniverse allows for generating entire graph families with persistent semantic communities while controlling structural properties like homophily and degree distributions. The paper benchmarks a variety of graph architectures, from GNNs to graph transformers, revealing that strong transductive performance doesn't necessarily predict strong inductive generalization. It also investigates robustness to distribution shifts, finding sensitivity to both model architecture and initial graph regime. The framework is released as a Python package and interactive demo.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a *generative framework* for graph *families* with persistent semantic structure and *controllable properties*. This addresses a significant limitation in current graph learning benchmarks, which predominantly focus on single-graph, transductive settings. While synthetic graph generation tools exist (e.g., GraphWorld), GraphUniverse’s hierarchical approach for inductive generalization evaluation is a clear step forward.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a crucial gap in graph learning: the limited understanding and evaluation of inductive generalization. By providing a tool to generate diverse, controlled graph families, GraphUniverse enables researchers to systematically evaluate model robustness to distribution shifts and generalization to unseen graphs. The benchmarking results demonstrating the disconnect between transductive and inductive performance are significant, challenging existing assumptions about model capabilities. This could steer the field towards more robust and generalizable graph learning approaches. The open-source release also promotes accessibility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a critical gap:** Directly tackles the lack of inductive generalization benchmarks in graph learning.
    *   **Controllable generation:** Provides fine-grained control over various graph properties.
    *   **Systematic evaluation:** Demonstrates the framework's utility through comprehensive benchmarking and distribution shift analysis.
    *   **Open-source release:** Increases accessibility and promotes community contributions.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its contributions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited exploration of complex graph features:** While controlling basic properties like homophily and degree, the framework might not fully capture the complexities of real-world graphs. Further work might be needed to extend it to more intricate structural patterns and node attributes.
    *   **Benchmarking scope:** While the authors benchmark several architectures, further investigation with a wider variety of models and tasks could strengthen the findings.
    *   **Over-simplication:** There is a risk that synthetic benchmarks might oversimplify the challenges encountered in real-world graph learning scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** GraphUniverse has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   **Improving benchmark quality:** Encouraging the development of more comprehensive and realistic benchmarks for inductive generalization.
    *   **Driving model development:** Guiding the design of more robust and generalizable graph architectures.
    *   **Enabling large-scale pre-training:** Facilitating the generation of large-scale datasets for pre-training graph foundation models.

While the framework provides a solid foundation, there's room for improvement with regards to scalability, incorporating more realistic graph structures and features, and validating performance on a wider array of tasks and models. However, GraphUniverse represents a significant and timely contribution to addressing a critical bottleneck in graph learning research.

### VideoChat-R1.5: Visual Test-Time Scaling to Reinforce Multimodal Reasoning by Iterative Perception
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21100v1)
- **Authors**: Ziang Yan, Xinhao Li, Yinan He, Zhengrong Yue, Xiangyu Zeng, Yali Wang, Yu Qiao, Limin Wang, Yi Wang
- **Abstract**: Inducing reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) is critical for achieving human-level perception and understanding. Existing methods mainly leverage LLM reasoning to analyze parsed visuals, often limited by static perception stages. This paper introduces Visual Test-Time Scaling (VTTS), a novel approach to enhance MLLMs' reasoning via iterative perception during inference. VTTS mimics humans' hierarchical attention by progressively refining focus on high-confidence spatio-temporal regions, guided by updated textual predictions. Specifically, VTTS employs an Iterative Perception (ITP) mechanism, incorporating reinforcement learning with spatio-temporal supervision to optimize reasoning. To support this paradigm, we also present VTTS-80K, a dataset tailored for iterative perception. These designs allows a MLLM to enhance its performance by increasing its perceptual compute. Extensive experiments validate VTTS's effectiveness and generalization across diverse tasks and benchmarks. Our newly introduced Videochat-R1.5 model has achieved remarkable improvements, with an average increase of over 5\%, compared to robust baselines such as Qwen2.5VL-3B and -7B, across more than 15 benchmarks that encompass video conversation, video reasoning, and spatio-temporal perception.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Visual Test-Time Scaling (VTTS), a novel approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) through iterative visual perception during inference. VTTS mimics human hierarchical attention by progressively refining focus on high-confidence spatio-temporal regions based on updated textual predictions. The method employs an Iterative Perception (ITP) mechanism, incorporating reinforcement learning with spatio-temporal supervision to optimize reasoning. The authors also contribute VTTS-80K, a dataset tailored for iterative perception. They demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of VTTS across diverse tasks and benchmarks, achieving performance improvements over strong baselines like Qwen2.5VL on video conversation, video reasoning, and spatio-temporal perception tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of iterative visual perception guided by textual predictions is a conceptually interesting and potentially useful contribution. It directly addresses a key limitation of current MLLMs, which often rely on static, single-pass visual processing. The ITP mechanism and the VTTS-80K dataset provide concrete tools for implementing and evaluating this approach. However, the idea of iterative refinement isn't entirely new, and there are connections to earlier works on attention mechanisms and visual search. The novelty lies in the specific implementation within the MLLM context and the reinforcement learning-based optimization of the spatio-temporal focus.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates tangible performance improvements on a wide range of video and image understanding benchmarks. This suggests that VTTS can be a valuable technique for enhancing the performance of existing MLLMs without requiring substantial architectural changes or retraining from scratch. The VTTS-80K dataset is a valuable contribution to the community, providing a resource for training and evaluating models with iterative perception capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing MLLMs in terms of static visual perception and highlights the benefits of iterative refinement.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The VTTS framework, including the ITP mechanism and reinforcement learning optimization, is well-defined and explained.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across a diverse set of benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness and generalizability of VTTS.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The VTTS-80K dataset is a valuable resource for the community, enabling further research in iterative perception.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach is interesting and yields improvements, it builds upon existing techniques in attention and reinforcement learning. The core novelty could be viewed as an incremental improvement within the existing landscape of MLLMs.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The iterative nature of VTTS inevitably increases the computational cost during inference. While the paper mentions this, a more detailed analysis of the trade-off between performance and computational cost would be beneficial.
    *   **Dependence on Data:** The performance of VTTS likely depends heavily on the quality and diversity of the VTTS-80K dataset. It's unclear how well the approach would generalize to tasks and domains not covered by the dataset. The failure cases show that the model struggles when localization is not accurate, and might not generalise as well when the input images are vastly different from the training data.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore iterative perception and adaptive attention mechanisms for MLLMs. The VTTS-80K dataset could also serve as a benchmark for evaluating future approaches in this area.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper presents a valuable and well-executed approach for enhancing MLLM reasoning through iterative visual perception. While the novelty is somewhat incremental, the empirical results and dataset contribution make this a significant contribution to the field.

### PerHalluEval: Persian Hallucination Evaluation Benchmark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21104v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Hosseini, Kimia Hosseini, Shayan Bali, Zahra Zanjani, Saeedeh Momtazi
- **Abstract**: Hallucination is a persistent issue affecting all large language Models (LLMs), particularly within low-resource languages such as Persian. PerHalluEval (Persian Hallucination Evaluation) is the first dynamic hallucination evaluation benchmark tailored for the Persian language. Our benchmark leverages a three-stage LLM-driven pipeline, augmented with human validation, to generate plausible answers and summaries regarding QA and summarization tasks, focusing on detecting extrinsic and intrinsic hallucinations. Moreover, we used the log probabilities of generated tokens to select the most believable hallucinated instances. In addition, we engaged human annotators to highlight Persian-specific contexts in the QA dataset in order to evaluate LLMs' performance on content specifically related to Persian culture. Our evaluation of 12 LLMs, including open- and closed-source models using PerHalluEval, revealed that the models generally struggle in detecting hallucinated Persian text. We showed that providing external knowledge, i.e., the original document for the summarization task, could mitigate hallucination partially. Furthermore, there was no significant difference in terms of hallucination when comparing LLMs specifically trained for Persian with others.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PerHalluEval: Persian Hallucination Evaluation Benchmark for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PerHalluEval, the first dynamic hallucination detection benchmark specifically designed for the Persian language. The authors develop a multi-agent, LLM-driven pipeline, incorporating human validation, to generate plausible yet incorrect answers and summaries, which are then used to evaluate the ability of various LLMs to detect both extrinsic and intrinsic hallucinations. They use PN-Summary and PQuAD datasets as the basis for creating their new benchmark. The authors evaluate 12 LLMs (including both open and closed-source models) and find that these models generally struggle to detect hallucinations in Persian text. They further observe that external knowledge can partially mitigate hallucinations in summarization tasks, and that Persian-specific fine-tuning does not always improve hallucination detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a much-needed benchmark for hallucination detection in Persian. As a low-resource language with complex linguistic features, Persian presents unique challenges for LLMs. The creation of PerHalluEval directly addresses this gap and enables more targeted research in this area. The use of a multi-agent LLM-driven pipeline for data generation, augmented with human validation and probabilistic filtering, is also a novel approach. The exploration of cultural context in QA and the post-hoc analysis of source model contribution are other aspects that add to the novelty of this work.

*   **Significance:** Hallucination is a major impediment to the reliable deployment of LLMs, and the problem is even more acute for low-resource languages. By creating PerHalluEval, the authors provide the research community with a valuable tool for evaluating and improving the performance of LLMs in Persian. This could lead to more robust and trustworthy Persian language applications in various domains. The inclusion of both extrinsic and intrinsic hallucinations, along with insights into the impact of external knowledge and Persian-specific training, further enhances the significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Focus on a critical problem (hallucination) in a challenging language (Persian).
    *   Careful design of the data generation pipeline, incorporating LLMs, human validation, and probabilistic filtering.
    *   Clear and well-defined evaluation metrics (Hallucination Recall, Factual Recall, Hamming Score).
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of a range of LLMs, including both open and closed-source models, and Persian-specific trained ones.
    *   Thorough analysis of the results, providing insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different models.
    *   Provides the code for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dataset relies on existing datasets which themselves might have some biases.
    *   The specific details of the fine-tuning process (if any) for Persian-specific models could be expanded upon.
    *   More error analysis would strengthen the paper even further.
    *   The limited size of the evaluation set, while understandable given the effort involved in manual validation, could be a potential limitation. A larger dataset would provide more statistically robust results.
    *   It is not clear in how far other cultural contexts are different or more challenging than Persian.

*   **Potential Influence:** PerHalluEval has the potential to significantly influence research on LLMs for low-resource languages, particularly Persian. It can serve as a standard benchmark for evaluating and comparing different models, encouraging the development of more reliable and trustworthy systems. It may further serve as a tool to improve LLMs fine-tuned for Persian to mitigate hallucinations in other tasks than QA and Summarization. The methods proposed in the paper can also be adapted to create similar benchmarks for other languages.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution to a relevant area of research, by developing a Persian language-specific benchmark for hallucination detection, which has clear potential for future work. The novelty lies in the topic of the benchmark, the carefully designed methodology for data generation and evaluation, and the thorough analysis of results. This allows other researchers to test their approaches on this benchmark and improve LLMs for this important cultural space. Some limitations in data size and generalization potential of the approach exist, but does not diminish the significance of the contribution, warranting a score of 8.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### BESPOKE: Benchmark for Search-Augmented Large Language Model Personalization via Diagnostic Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21106v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunseo Kim, Sangam Lee, Kwangwook Seo, Dongha Lee
- **Abstract**: Search-augmented large language models (LLMs) have advanced information-seeking tasks by integrating retrieval into generation, reducing users' cognitive burden compared to traditional search systems. Yet they remain insufficient for fully addressing diverse user needs, which requires recognizing how the same query can reflect different intents across users and delivering information in preferred forms. While recent systems such as ChatGPT and Gemini attempt personalization by leveraging user histories, systematic evaluation of such personalization is under-explored. To address this gap, we propose BESPOKE, the realistic benchmark for evaluating personalization in search-augmented LLMs. BESPOKE is designed to be both realistic, by collecting authentic chat and search histories directly from humans, and diagnostic, by pairing responses with fine-grained preference scores and feedback. The benchmark is constructed through long-term, deeply engaged human annotation, where human annotators contributed their own histories, authored queries with detailed information needs, and evaluated responses with scores and diagnostic feedback. Leveraging BESPOKE, we conduct systematic analyses that reveal key requirements for effective personalization in information-seeking tasks, providing a foundation for fine-grained evaluation of personalized search-augmented LLMs. Our code and data are available at https://augustinlib.github.io/BESPOKE/.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BESPOKE: Benchmark for Search-Augmented Large Language Model Personalization Via Diagnostic Feedback":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BESPOKE, a new benchmark for evaluating personalization in search-augmented large language models (LLMs). The key innovation lies in its realistic and diagnostic nature. It utilizes real-world user histories (chat and search) collected over a three-week period from 30 diverse annotators.  Crucially, BESPOKE includes fine-grained human annotations that pair model responses with preference scores *and* diagnostic feedback explaining why a response is satisfactory or not. This allows for detailed analysis of personalization quality beyond simple accuracy. The authors also provide an evaluation framework that leverages human feedback as additional context to assess personalization. Through experiments with several search-augmented LLMs, the paper identifies factors influencing personalization effectiveness, such as user context construction and query-awareness, and highlights the importance of high-quality web search for personalized responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the evaluation of search-augmented LLMs. While these models have improved information-seeking tasks, their ability to personalize responses to individual user needs remains under-explored. BESPOKE directly tackles this issue by providing a benchmark grounded in real user behavior, encompassing both chat and search interactions – a departure from limited QA-style datasets. Its diagnostic feedback is particularly valuable, allowing for deeper insights into the models' strengths and weaknesses. Existing benchmarks typically focus on factuality and relevance, often overlooking the nuanced aspects of personalization such as tone, style, and need alignment. BESPOKE's contribution is significant because it enables more granular evaluation of personalization and provides concrete feedback for improving these systems. The detailed analysis that the authors perform with BESPOKE furthers the field by pinpointing the importance of context construction, and a query-aware approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Realism:** Using real user histories enhances the benchmark's ecological validity.
    *   **Diagnostic Feedback:**  Fine-grained annotations offer actionable insights for model improvement, surpassing simple scoring.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** Experiments with various LLMs reveal critical factors influencing personalization.
    *   **Evaluation Framework:** Framework leverages additional context to better align with Human Judgment.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scale:** The limited number of users (30) may raise concerns about the generalizability of the benchmark.
    *   **Annotation Cost:** Creating and maintaining such a detailed, human-annotated dataset is resource-intensive, potentially limiting future expansion.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-5 for Evaluation:** While using a different LLM for evaluation helps mitigate self-preference bias, it still introduces a dependence on the capabilities and biases of a single model.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** While various LLMs were evaluated, the degree of personalization varied on context construction.

*   **Potential Influence:** BESPOKE has the potential to significantly influence the field of search-augmented LLMs. By providing a more realistic and diagnostic evaluation framework, it can guide the development of more effective and user-centric information-seeking systems. The findings on user context construction, query-awareness, and web search quality also offer valuable directions for future research.
*   **Justification for Score:** While the limited scale and reliance on GPT-5 are valid concerns, the novelty and significance of BESPOKE, along with its diagnostic feedback, outweigh these limitations. BESPOKE is a clear advancement over existing benchmarks, enabling a deeper understanding of personalization in search-augmented LLMs.
