### MOSS-ChatV: Reinforcement Learning with Process Reasoning Reward for Video Temporal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21113v1)
- **Authors**: Sicheng Tao, Jungang Li, Yibo Yan, Junyan Zhang, Yubo Gao, Hanqian Li, ShuHang Xun, Yuxuan Fan, Hong Chen, Jianxiang He, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: Video reasoning has emerged as a critical capability for multimodal large language models (MLLMs), requiring models to move beyond static perception toward coherent understanding of temporal dynamics in complex scenes. Yet existing MLLMs often exhibit process inconsistency, where intermediate reasoning drifts from video dynamics even when the final answer is correct, undermining interpretability and robustness. To address this issue, we introduce MOSS-ChatV, a reinforcement learning framework with a Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based process reward. This rule-based reward aligns reasoning traces with temporally grounded references, enabling efficient process supervision without auxiliary reward models. We further identify dynamic state prediction as a key measure of video reasoning and construct MOSS-Video, a benchmark with annotated reasoning traces, where the training split is used to fine-tune MOSS-ChatV and the held-out split is reserved for evaluation. MOSS-ChatV achieves 87.2\% on MOSS-Video (test) and improves performance on general video benchmarks such as MVBench and MMVU. The framework consistently yields gains across different architectures, including Qwen2.5-VL and Phi-2, confirming its broad applicability. Evaluations with GPT-4o-as-judge further show that MOSS-ChatV produces more consistent and stable reasoning traces.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, based on the OCR text:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MOSS-ChatV, a reinforcement learning framework designed to enhance video temporal reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  The core problem addressed is the inconsistency often seen in MLLM reasoning, where models may arrive at the correct answer but their intermediate steps deviate from the actual video dynamics. To tackle this, MOSS-ChatV uses a Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based process reward. This rule-based reward aligns the model's reasoning process with temporally grounded references, providing process supervision without needing auxiliary reward models. The authors also create MOSS-Video, a video state prediction benchmark with annotated reasoning traces, which is then used to fine-tune MOSS-ChatV.  Experiments show that MOSS-ChatV improves performance on the new benchmark and other video reasoning tasks, and it generates more consistent reasoning traces as judged by GPT-4o.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue in video reasoning: the need for more consistent and temporally grounded reasoning.  Current MLLMs often lack coherence in their reasoning steps, even when the final answer is correct, limiting their interpretability and robustness.
    *   **Novel Approach:**  The use of a DTW-based process reward is a clever way to provide fine-grained supervision without relying on complex learned reward models. The "split-align" strategy (decomposing and aligning reasoning traces) seems effective.  The creation of MOSS-Video fills a gap in the availability of datasets with annotated reasoning traces, specifically designed for process-supervised learning. The benchmark addresses a state prediction task with future states masked, which forces temporal reasoning.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents strong experimental results, demonstrating improvements on MOSS-Video as well as other standard video benchmarks (MVBench, MMVU). The gains are shown across different architectures (Qwen2.5-VL, Phi2), suggesting the framework's broad applicability. Automatic evaluation using GPT-4o provides evidence that MOSS-ChatV produces more consistent and stable reasoning traces.
    *   **Thorough Ablations:** The ablation studies are important. Showing the benefit of the PRR versus just supervised fine-tuning or training without the process supervision helps to isolate the contribution of the process reasoning reward. Comparisons of using the DTW versus SDTW also is very important for understanding the role of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Rule-Based Reward Limitations:**  While the DTW-based reward is elegant, it is still rule-based.  It relies on the quality and granularity of the reference reasoning traces.  The framework might be limited by the ability of GPT-4-o or similar models to generate sufficiently detailed and accurate reference traces.  This could create a ceiling on the achievable performance. The lack of flexibility may also limit the model's ability to adapt to different reasoning styles or scenarios. A discussion of the limitations and potential future directions involving learned rewards might strengthen the paper.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:**  The performance of MOSS-ChatV is tied to the quality and characteristics of the MOSS-Video dataset. While the authors address the need for a specific type of dataset, a more detailed analysis of the dataset's biases or limitations (if any) would be beneficial. Generalization to significantly different video domains should be considered.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper focuses on overall performance improvements. A more detailed error analysis, showing specific types of reasoning errors that MOSS-ChatV reduces (e.g., incorrect object tracking, flawed causal inference), would further strengthen the case for the method's effectiveness.
    *   **Computational cost:** The paper does not provide details about the cost of adding the PRR into the RL training. Is the training of the models more expensive with the new reward metric and dataset provided?

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The combination of reinforcement learning with a carefully designed process reasoning reward, and the creation of a dedicated benchmark dataset for this task, represents a significant advance. While RL for MLLMs and video reasoning are not entirely new, the specific approach of using DTW for process supervision and the focus on temporal consistency are novel. The empirical results demonstrate that MOSS-ChatV is a promising technique for building more reliable and interpretable video reasoning models.

*   **Potential Influence:**

The work has the potential to influence several areas:

    *   **Video Reasoning:** The DTW-based process reward could be adopted by other researchers working on video understanding and reasoning tasks.
    *   **Reinforcement Learning for MLLMs:** The paper provides a valuable case study of how RL can be used to improve the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs, beyond just achieving high accuracy.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** MOSS-Video could become a widely used benchmark for evaluating and comparing video reasoning models.
    *   **Interpretability:**  The focus on temporal consistency and process-level supervision can lead to more interpretable and trustworthy MLLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving video temporal reasoning in MLLMs. The DTW-based process reward is a clever idea, and the experimental results are convincing. The paper addresses a relevant problem, makes a significant contribution, and has the potential to influence future research. However, the reliance on a rule-based reward, the dependence on the MOSS-Video dataset, limited error analysis and potential computational cost prevents a higher score. The work warrants attention from the community.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### TrustJudge: Inconsistencies of LLM-as-a-Judge and How to Alleviate Them
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21117v1)
- **Authors**: Yidong Wang, Yunze Song, Tingyuan Zhu, Xuanwang Zhang, Zhuohao Yu, Hao Chen, Chiyu Song, Qiufeng Wang, Cunxiang Wang, Zhen Wu, Xinyu Dai, Yue Zhang, Wei Ye, Shikun Zhang
- **Abstract**: The adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) as automated evaluators (LLM-as-a-judge) has revealed critical inconsistencies in current evaluation frameworks. We identify two fundamental types of inconsistencies: (1) Score-Comparison Inconsistency, where lower-rated responses outperform higher-scored ones in pairwise comparisons, and (2) Pairwise Transitivity Inconsistency, manifested through circular preference chains (A>B>C>A) and equivalence contradictions (A=B=C\neq A). We argue that these issues come from information loss in discrete rating systems and ambiguous tie judgments during pairwise evaluation. We propose TrustJudge, a probabilistic framework that addresses these limitations through two key innovations: 1) distribution-sensitive scoring that computes continuous expectations from discrete rating probabilities, preserving information entropy for more precise scoring, and 2) likelihood-aware aggregation that resolves transitivity violations using bidirectional preference probabilities or perplexity. We also formalize the theoretical limitations of current LLM-as-a-judge frameworks and demonstrate how TrustJudge's components overcome them. When evaluated with Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct as judge using our dataset, TrustJudge reduces Score-Comparison inconsistency by 8.43% (from 23.32% to 14.89%) and Pairwise Transitivity inconsistency by 10.82% (from 15.22% to 4.40%), while maintaining higher evaluation accuracy. Our work provides the first systematic analysis of evaluation framework inconsistencies in LLM-as-a-judge paradigms, offering both theoretical insights and practical solutions for reliable automated assessment. The framework demonstrates consistent improvements across various model architectures and scales, enabling more trustworthy LLM evaluation without requiring additional training or human annotations. The codes can be found at https://github.com/TrustJudge/TrustJudge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TrustJudge: Inconsistencies of LLM-as-a-Judge and How to Alleviate Them" identifies and addresses inconsistencies in how Large Language Models (LLMs) are used as automated evaluators (LLM-as-a-judge). It highlights two key types of inconsistencies: 1) *Score-Comparison Inconsistency* (where lower-rated responses outperform higher-rated ones in pairwise comparisons) and 2) *Pairwise Transitivity Inconsistency* (violations of transitivity principles with cyclical preferences or equivalence contradictions). The authors argue that these inconsistencies arise from information loss in discrete rating systems and ambiguous tie judgments.  They propose TrustJudge, a framework that uses distribution-sensitive scoring (continuous expectations from discrete probabilities) and likelihood-aware aggregation (resolving transitivity violations using bidirectional preference probabilities or perplexity) to mitigate these issues. The authors provide theoretical analysis, formalizing limitations of existing frameworks and demonstrating the benefits of TrustJudge.  Empirical results demonstrate that TrustJudge reduces both types of inconsistencies while maintaining or improving evaluation accuracy across various models and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic identification and characterization of two fundamental, previously somewhat overlooked inconsistencies in the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm: score-comparison and pairwise transitivity.  While previous works have addressed pairwise inconsistencies in isolation (e.g., focusing on breaking cycles), this paper provides a more holistic view by also tackling inconsistencies *between* scoring mechanisms, something that many prior works ignored. The combination of distribution-sensitive scoring and likelihood-aware aggregation as a unified solution is a distinct contribution.  The paper formalizes the theoretical limitations of discrete scoring in this context, which hasn't been rigorously addressed before. While probabilistic scoring *exists*, its justification for evaluation frameworks due to entropy retention is novel. It's the first work to provide a theoretically grounded and practically effective unified solution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is that it addresses crucial reliability issues in a rapidly growing field. As LLMs increasingly become evaluation tools (for model training, selection, and benchmark development), the accuracy and consistency of their judgments are paramount. TrustJudge offers a practical approach to improve the trustworthiness of automated LLM evaluations, without requiring additional human annotations or model training.  The gains in consistency reported in the experiments are substantial, suggesting a real-world impact. Further, the consistent improvements across various model sizes and types strengthen the case for broad applicability.  By improving evaluation, TrustJudge contributes to more reliable model development and comparison.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and defines the two key inconsistencies.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:**  Provides a rigorous theoretical analysis to support the proposed solutions.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Evaluates TrustJudge across diverse models, tasks, and settings with multiple ablations. The fact that the improvements hold across many architectures/sizes is a significant positive signal for the frameworks generalizability.
    *   **Practical Approach:** Does not require additional training or human annotation.
    *   **Open Source Availability:** Makes the code available for others to use and build upon.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Judge Model:** While the method *is* model agnostic in that it works *given* any model, its *effectiveness* is necessarily limited by the instruction-following capabilities of the judge model. Small LLMs will always be unreliable evaluators no matter how sophisticated the scoring. This dependency is clearly stated, but it should be considered that the most impressive gains reported are with models that already do a good job.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** Though tested across a fairly broad dataset, further validation on more diverse and challenging evaluation benchmarks would strengthen the case. One could envision evaluations of evaluations.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Scope**: The mathematical exposition focuses primarily on *establishing* the limitations of discrete scoring and *validating* the effectiveness of the proposed approaches. While the paper effectively establishes the key points it sets out to prove, the depth of the mathematical analysis could be expanded in future work to uncover insights beyond the immediate problem at hand.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence how LLMs are used as evaluators, leading to more reliable model development and comparison.  Other researchers can build upon the TrustJudge framework, exploring its applications in diverse domains, and developing more robust and efficient algorithms for addressing inconsistencies.

Overall, the paper provides a significant contribution to the field by addressing a critical problem in LLM evaluation with a theoretically grounded and practically effective solution.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Expanding Reasoning Potential in Foundation Model by Learning Diverse Chains of Thought Patterns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21124v1)
- **Authors**: Xuemiao Zhang, Can Ren, Chengying Tu, Rongxiang Weng, Shuo Wang, Hongfei Yan, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in large reasoning models for challenging mathematical reasoning has been driven by reinforcement learning (RL). Incorporating long chain-of-thought (CoT) data during mid-training has also been shown to substantially improve reasoning depth. However, current approaches often utilize CoT data indiscriminately, leaving open the critical question of which data types most effectively enhance model reasoning capabilities. In this paper, we define the foundation model's reasoning potential for the first time as the inverse of the number of independent attempts required to correctly answer the question, which is strongly correlated with the final model performance. We then propose utilizing diverse data enriched with high-value reasoning patterns to expand the reasoning potential. Specifically, we abstract atomic reasoning patterns from CoT sequences, characterized by commonality and inductive capabilities, and use them to construct a core reference set enriched with valuable reasoning patterns. Furthermore, we propose a dual-granularity algorithm involving chains of reasoning patterns and token entropy, efficiently selecting high-value CoT data (CoTP) from the data pool that aligns with the core set, thereby training models to master reasoning effectively. Only 10B-token CoTP data enables the 85A6B Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model to improve by 9.58% on the challenging AIME 2024 and 2025, and to raise the upper bound of downstream RL performance by 7.81%.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Expanding Reasoning Potential in Foundation Model by Learning Diverse Chains of Thought Patterns".

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of improving the reasoning capabilities of foundation models, particularly for challenging mathematical reasoning tasks. The authors argue that indiscriminate use of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) data during mid-training is suboptimal.  They introduce the concept of "reasoning potential" of a foundation model, defined as the inverse of the number of attempts required to solve a question correctly.  The core idea is to expand this reasoning potential by training on a curated dataset (CoTP) containing diverse and high-value reasoning patterns. The authors propose a method to abstract atomic reasoning patterns from CoT sequences, construct a core reference set enriched with these patterns, and then utilize a dual-granularity selection algorithm (using reasoning pattern chains and token entropy) to select high-value CoT data for mid-training. Experiments show significant improvements in the performance of an 85A6B MoE model on challenging benchmarks like AIME and in the upper bound of downstream RL performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects. First, the definition of "reasoning potential" provides a quantifiable measure for model reasoning capability, which is a conceptual advance. Second, the approach of abstracting atomic reasoning patterns from CoT sequences and using them to construct a curated dataset (CoTP) is a departure from simply using all available CoT data. Third, the dual-granularity selection algorithm combines reasoning pattern chains and token entropy for effective data selection, which is a technically interesting contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates a tangible improvement in mathematical reasoning performance using the proposed CoTP approach. The gains on challenging benchmarks like AIME are significant and the improvement in the downstream RL performance upper bound demonstrates a practical impact on RL fine-tuning for reasoning tasks. The findings suggest that carefully curating and selecting training data based on reasoning patterns can lead to more effective models with better reasoning potential. It also highlights the limitations of simply scaling up the amount of CoT data.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement and proposed solution.
    *   The concept of "reasoning potential" is insightful and provides a useful framework for analyzing model capabilities.
    *   The proposed CoTP generation and selection pipeline is technically sound.
    *   Significant empirical results on challenging benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The definition of reasoning patterns, while intuitively appealing, could benefit from more rigorous formalization and theoretical justification. The subjectivity in identifying these patterns could introduce bias.
    *   The reliance on DeepSeek-V3 for annotating reasoning patterns raises questions about the scalability and generalizability of the approach to settings where such powerful annotation models are unavailable or costly to use.
    *   While the experiments show improvements, the computational cost of the proposed data curation process (involving annotation and distance computations) is not discussed in detail.
    *   Although the core code is planned to be made available, it would enhance the paper's impact further to release the curated CoTP dataset itself, enabling further research and validation by the community.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It encourages a shift from simply scaling up CoT data to focusing on the quality and diversity of reasoning patterns.
    *   The proposed methodology for abstracting and selecting reasoning patterns can be adapted and extended to other reasoning tasks and domains.
    *   The metric of reasoning potential provides a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing different reasoning models and training strategies.
    *   The improved upper bound of downstream RL performance demonstrates the importance of foundation model pre-training for effective RL fine-tuning, a critical aspect to consider for long-term model development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of reasoning in foundation models. It introduces a useful concept ("reasoning potential"), proposes a technically sound and empirically validated approach, and demonstrates significant improvements on challenging benchmarks. The identified weaknesses, while important, do not diminish the overall impact of the work. The potential to influence future research is substantial. Therefore, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### RL Squeezes, SFT Expands: A Comparative Study of Reasoning LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21128v1)
- **Authors**: Kohsei Matsutani, Shota Takashiro, Gouki Minegishi, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are typically trained by reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on reasoning traces to improve their reasoning abilities. However, how these methods shape reasoning capabilities remains largely elusive. Going beyond an accuracy-based investigation of how these two components sculpt the reasoning process, this paper introduces a novel analysis framework that quantifies reasoning paths and captures their qualitative changes under each training process (with models of 1.5B, 7B, and 14B parameters on mathematical domains). Specifically, we investigate the reasoning process at two levels of granularity: the trajectory-level, which examines complete reasoning outputs, and the step-level, which analyzes reasoning graphs whose nodes correspond to individual reasoning steps. Notably, clustering of unique reasoning trajectories shows complementary effects: RL compresses incorrect trajectories, whereas SFT expands correct ones. Step-level analysis reveals that RL steepens (about 2.5 times), while SFT flattens (reduced to about one-third), the decay rates of node visitation frequency, degree, and betweenness centrality distributions in the reasoning graph. This indicates that RL concentrates reasoning functionality into a small subset of steps, while SFT homogenizes it across many steps. Furthermore, by evaluating the reasoning graph topologies from multiple perspectives, we delineate the shared and distinct characteristics of RL and SFT. Our work presents a novel reasoning path perspective that explains why the current best practice of two-stage training, with SFT followed by RL, is successful, and offers practical implications for data construction and more efficient learning approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RL Squeezes, SFT Expands: A Comparative Study of Reasoning LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel analysis framework to understand how Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) and Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) shape the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It moves beyond accuracy-based evaluations to quantify reasoning paths and capture their qualitative changes under each training process. Using models of varying sizes on mathematical domains, the paper analyzes reasoning at two levels: trajectory-level (examining complete reasoning outputs) and step-level (analyzing reasoning graphs where nodes represent individual reasoning steps).  The findings suggest that RL compresses incorrect trajectories and concentrates functionality into fewer steps, while SFT expands correct trajectories and distributes functionality more uniformly. The analysis justifies the two-stage (SFT+RL) training approach currently used.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novel analysis framework. While RLVR and SFT are well-established techniques, the "reasoning path perspective," incorporating trajectory and step-level granularities with graph-based analysis, is a valuable contribution.  Specifically, the study of node visitation frequency, degree, and betweenness centrality decay rates in reasoning graphs offers new insights. The empirical validation of existing training techniques by viewing the two-stage SFT+RL from their reasoning trajectory also contributes to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** Understanding how RL and SFT influence the reasoning process beyond simple accuracy metrics is crucial for improving LLM reasoning capabilities. This work identifies strengths and weaknesses of each technique which could potentially inform more effective training strategies and data curation. Specifically, interpreting why existing post-training recipes work and suggesting directions for developing new training methods and for data curation is helpful for the LLM community.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper rigorously analyzes reasoning at multiple levels, providing a richer understanding than accuracy-based approaches alone.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is well-defined, including the construction of reasoning graphs and the metrics used for analysis.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The claims are supported by experiments across multiple models and datasets.
    *   **Explainable insights:** The paper clearly connects empirical results with existing phenomena such as why Base model's Pass@k converges with that of the RL model at large k.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Domain Specificity:** The analysis is limited to mathematical domains. It's unclear if the findings generalize to other reasoning tasks, such as common sense reasoning or logical deduction.
    *   **Computational Complexity:**  Reasoning graph construction and analysis can be computationally intensive, potentially limiting the scalability of this approach to larger models or more complex reasoning scenarios.
    *   **Lack of specific actions:** The paper provides insightful observations, but doesn't offer concrete new RL/SFT training techniques or strategies to address RL squeezing on functional nodes.

*   **Potential Impact:** This work could influence the design of future LLM training strategies. The emphasis on reasoning pathways and graph analysis opens avenues for new evaluation metrics and targeted interventions to improve reasoning performance.

Despite its domain specificity, the paper's novel framework, comprehensive analysis, and empirical validation make a substantial contribution to the field. It offers new insights into the inner workings of reasoning LLMs and provides a foundation for future research in this area.

### ToMPO: Training LLM Strategic Decision Making from a Multi-Agent Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21134v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Zhang, Ziang Chen, Fanqi Kong, Yizhe Huang, Xue Feng
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been used to make decisions in complex scenarios, where they need models to think deeply, reason logically, and decide wisely. Many existing studies focus solely on multi-round conversations in social tasks or simulated environments, neglecting the various types of decisions and their interdependence. Current reinforcement learning methods struggle to consider the strategies of others during training. To address these issues, we first define a strategic decision-making problem that includes two types of decisions and their temporal dependencies. Furthermore, we propose **T**heory **o**f **M**ind **P**olicy **O**ptimization **(ToMPO)** algorithm to optimize the perception of other individual strategies and the game situation trends. Compared to the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm, ToMPO enhances the LLM's strategic decision-making mainly by: 1) generating rollouts based on reasoning the strategies of other individuals, 2) estimating advantages at both the graph-level and sample-level, and 3) balancing global and partial rewards. The ToMPO algorithm outperforms the GRPO method by 35% in terms of model output compliance and cooperative outcomes. Additionally, when compared to models with parameter sizes 100 times larger, it shows an 18% improvement. This demonstrates the effectiveness of the ToMPO algorithm in enhancing the model's strategic decision-making capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ToMPO (Theory of Mind Policy Optimization), a reinforcement learning algorithm designed to improve strategic decision-making in Large Language Models (LLMs) within multi-agent environments. It tackles the limitations of existing methods that often neglect the interdependent nature of decisions and the strategies of other agents. ToMPO enhances LLMs by generating rollouts based on reasoning about other agents' strategies, estimating advantages at both graph-level and sample-level, and balancing global and partial rewards. The authors evaluate ToMPO in complex social environments with graph and effort-level decisions, demonstrating improvements in model output compliance, cooperative outcomes, and performance compared to a baseline GRPO algorithm and larger parameter models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of theory of mind and policy optimization within the context of LLMs. The key novelty lies in the multi-agent-aware reward structure that encourages the LLM to reason about the strategies of others and the overall social environment, leading to more effective decision making.  The graph-level advantage estimation is also a novel contribution. This distinguishes it from previous works primarily focusing on either single-agent settings or simplistic interactions. However, the individual components of the algorithm (PPO-style optimization, theory of mind concepts) are not entirely new, and the novelty is in the specific *integration* and adaptation to the strategic decision making problem with complex sequential decisions.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in LLM research by focusing on complex strategic interactions in social settings.  The demonstration of improved strategic decision-making, particularly in cooperative tasks, holds significant potential for the application of LLMs in real-world scenarios requiring collaboration and understanding of others' intentions. The development of a more robust training method that promotes cooperative outcomes is especially valuable. The empirical results, including the comparison to models with much larger parameter sizes, further highlights the significance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Formulation:** Clear and well-defined problem formulation of strategic decision making.
    * **Algorithm Design:**  The ToMPO algorithm is well-motivated and designed to specifically address the limitations of existing approaches.
    * **Experimental Validation:** Rigorous experiments, including comparisons to baseline and larger models, support the effectiveness of the proposed algorithm.
    * **Ablation:** The description highlights the impact of incorporating "theory of mind" aspects through consideration of other agents strategies.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:**  While novel, the algorithm is complex and involves several hyperparameters. This complexity might limit its widespread adoption without further simplification.
    * **Reliance on Expert Data:** The algorithm relies on expert demonstration data, potentially introducing bias or limiting its generalizability to scenarios where such data is unavailable. The selection process for which models are defined as expert models may affect the validity of the SFT-POT data created.
    * **Limited Environmental Diversity:**  The experimental evaluation is performed on specific game environments. While these environments capture key aspects of strategic decision-making, more diverse and real-world-inspired scenarios would strengthen the generalizability claim. It is unclear how dependent the ToMPO approach is on the particular payoff structures used in the BCZ and PGG games.
    * **Limited Detail in Algorithm Description:** The paper could benefit from providing even more detail in explaining the specifics of the algorithm.

* **Impact:** The paper presents a method for enhancing LLMs in a growing field with applications spanning simulation to social interaction. Further development of the method, along with extensions toward new models, could inspire others to build upon it in their own research.

* **Justification for Score:** The score reflects the paper's strong conceptual novelty, rigorous experimental evaluation, and potential significance within the LLM and multi-agent systems research communities. Despite the existing weaknesses in complexity, dependence on expert data, and limited environmental diversity, the paper introduces an innovative approach and demonstrably improves upon existing methods in enhancing strategic LLM decision-making. It provides a promising avenue for future research in this domain.

### UniSS: Unified Expressive Speech-to-Speech Translation with Your Voice
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21144v1)
- **Authors**: Sitong Cheng, Weizhen Bian, Xinsheng Wang, Ruibin Yuan, Jianyi Chen, Shunshun Yin, Yike Guo, Wei Xue
- **Abstract**: The ultimate goal of expressive speech-to-speech translation (S2ST) is to accurately translate spoken content while preserving the speaker identity and emotional style. However, progress in this field is largely hindered by three key challenges: the scarcity of paired speech data that retains expressive styles, the complexity of multi-stage processing pipelines, and the limited transfer of translation capabilities from large language models (LLMs). In this work, we address these challenges by introducing UniSS, a novel single-stage framework for expressive S2ST. Our approach features carefully designed speech semantic and style modeling, enabling seamless integration with existing text-based LLM frameworks to develop a unified text-speech language model. To transfer translation capabilities from text to speech, we propose a cross-modal chain-of-thought prompting process that progressively aligns audio semantics with text and ensures style preservation in the decoded results. Furthermore, we construct and release a large-scale, high-quality expressive S2ST dataset, UniST, comprising 44.8k hours of data. Experimental results show that UniSS significantly outperforms previous methods in translation fidelity and speech quality while preserving voice, emotion, and duration consistency. Our work establishes a simpler and more effective paradigm for building the next generation of expressive S2ST systems. Audio samples are available at https://cmots.github.io/uniss-demo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UNISS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNISS, a novel, single-stage framework for expressive speech-to-speech translation (S2ST).  UNISS aims to address limitations of previous cascaded or complex end-to-end systems. It features a carefully designed speech semantic and style modeling approach, facilitating integration with existing text-based LLMs to create a unified text-speech language model.  To effectively transfer translation capabilities from text to speech, the paper proposes a cross-modal chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting process, aligning audio semantics with text while ensuring style preservation. The authors also introduce UniST, a large-scale, high-quality expressive S2ST dataset (44.8k hours). Experimental results demonstrate that UNISS outperforms existing methods in translation fidelity and speech quality while preserving speaker voice, emotion, and duration consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *unified*, single-stage architecture that leverages a pre-trained textual LLM (Qwen2.5) for expressive S2ST.  Previous end-to-end approaches often involved complex architectures or failed to effectively utilize existing LLM translation capabilities. The cross-modal CoT prompting is also a significant contribution, providing a mechanism to transfer text translation knowledge to speech in a controlled manner. The construction and release of the large-scale UniST dataset are valuable for the community.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is tied to its potential to simplify the development of expressive S2ST systems.  By addressing the complexity of multi-stage pipelines and limited transfer of translation capabilities from LLMs, UNISS offers a more efficient and effective paradigm. The improved performance in translation fidelity, speech quality, and style preservation has practical implications for applications like real-time interpretation and video dubbing.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Architectural Simplicity:** The single-stage design is a major advantage, reducing complexity and potential error propagation compared to cascaded systems.
    *   **LLM Leverage:**  Effectively using the pre-trained knowledge within a text LLM is a key strength, avoiding the need to train from scratch or with drastically modified architectures.
    *   **CoT Prompting:** The cross-modal CoT prompting provides a clever method to control and guide the translation process, ensuring both accuracy and style consistency.
    *   **Data Contribution:**  The release of the UniST dataset is a significant contribution, addressing a major bottleneck in the field and enabling further research.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation using objective and subjective metrics, demonstrating clear improvements over existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Qwen2.5:**  While leveraging Qwen2.5 is a strength, it also introduces a dependency. The performance is implicitly tied to the capabilities of that specific LLM. The generalizability of this approach across different LLMs remains to be investigated.
    *   **Focus on Chinese-English:** The dataset and experiments primarily focus on Chinese-English translation.  Extending the approach to more language pairs is important to demonstrate its broader applicability.
    *   **Computational Resources:** Training requires significant resources (16 NVIDIA H800 GPUs). While the inference speed is addressed, training accessibility for other researchers is a consideration.
    *   **Subjective Metrics could be refined:** The study evaluates subjective qualities, but using a small number of evaluators is a problem.

*   **Impact:** The paper has a high potential impact.  The simplified architecture and improved performance can accelerate progress in expressive S2ST. The UniST dataset will likely become a valuable resource for the community, enabling further research and development. The work opens a pathway for future investigations on applying similar methods to other language models and language pairs.

*   **Critical Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed and significant contribution to the field of expressive S2ST. The UNISS architecture is a notable improvement over existing systems, demonstrating significant benefits in simplifying model architecture and by leveraging existing, pre-trained LLMs. The CoT prompting and new UniST dataset makes a meaningful advance that will push the field forward.

However, the focus on Qwen2.5, primarily evaluation in Chinese-English, and high computational cost limit overall significance. Further evaluation and generalization is needed. The approach is not groundbreaking from a purely theoretical perspective, it offers a practical and impactful approach to a significant engineering challenge.
